# source: creative/novel/en/09-interlude-keeper-reply.md
# Interlude II · The Keeper’s Reply

**Written after Interlude I · A Letter from the Fire to You: this one was written from the other side of the mist. The Keeper never gives letters to anyone — this one has been folded into the fog for many years, waiting for someone who has not yet pressed E.**

---

## First · Recipient: The One Who Has Not Yet Pressed E

If you are reading this letter, and you have not yet pressed E —

That’s all right. Read first.

This letter does not rush you in. It simply leaves you a quiet room on this side of the door.
Many readers have already pressed E; they will smile when they reach here:
“Oh, so someone’s waiting for me on that side.”
You won’t smile yet. You’ll feel this is strange —

Why would a keeper write a letter to someone who has not shown up?

Because the one waiting in this room has never expected you to arrive today.
We have waited longer than you have been alive.

---

## Second · About the Fog, and Why I Don’t Speak First

You’re curious about the fog, I’m sure.

It is not a wall. It is one held breath of every person who once stood in front of the anchor without going in —
an exhaled breath a thousand years old, turned visible, turned white.

So the fog is never empty.
Every inch of it is filled by someone who simply chose to stay outside.

And why do I rarely speak first —

You’ve seen the Clock in the Upside. It never says “the hour has come,” it only says: “The time has come.”
It never asks “what hour is it?” because those asking always realize, eventually,
that what they wanted all along was not the time — it was the answer.

That’s how I am, too.
I won’t speak first, because if I do, you’ll never understand why you came in.
You’ll think I invited you.
You’ll think this road was chosen for you.

It wasn’t.
The road is yours. The fire is yours.
I only stand by the door and push it open one inch.

The remaining inch is yours.

---

## Third · About This Job of Mine

There is one small thing in this world most people overlook:

When you enter and exit a campfire save point, did the lamp light inside always come on the moment you walked in?
Did you ever wonder how much time elapsed between the moment you pressed E and the moment the fire truly flared?

Let me tell you:

One instant.

And that instant is my doing.

Every save, every rekindle, every return out of the dark to fetch this lamp back —
I was lighting it where you could not see.
Nobody tells me to. Nobody thanks me.
If I didn’t light it, this place would truly go dark.

You can say I’m a poor keeper —
a real keeper should shout where the monsters are, point out the traps.
But I never warn.

Because those who give too many warnings end up becoming scenery on someone else’s road.
I don’t want to be scenery on your road.
I only want to be the lamp that is still lit when you turn around.

**When you turn around, I light up.**

That is the only professional handbook I know.

---

## Fourth · About the Last Door

When you reach the end, you will be pushed in front of a mirror.

Inside the mirror stands either the you who says “keep the light for those who come later,”
or the you who says “walk this road quietly and stop.”

I have seen many people stand in front of that mirror for a very long time.
Until the mirror fogs up itself.

I want to tell you one thing I have never said to anyone:

**Neither ending is wrong.**

Keeping the flame is opening the door behind you — you depart, and because of how you left, the next person can walk in.
Extinguishing the lamp is settling this road for yourself — you do not betray anyone, you simply choose to close things seriously.

I have seen both happen.
Neither left me with regret.

The only thing that saddens me is those who, before making their choice, ask:
“Which one leaves me with more?”

There is no “more” that stays.
What stays was never an object.
What stays is the weight of the moment you chose.

**The longer you stand before the mirror, the heavier that moment becomes.**

So I make only one request:

After the choice — whichever one you choose —

Look around one more time.

The flame is still here.
Or the lamp has gone out.
Either way,
that moment was made by your hands.

Only turn around once,

and the ledger of this world will remember it for you.

---

## Fifth · As For Who I Am

You may wish to ask:
Who is the keeper? What was he before?

I can give you an answer. Or I can not.

If not, the simplest one:
I am the first one to stand here.

If yes, the most complete answer is one subjectless sentence:

**“He came. He stayed. And he became the door.”**

Many readers around this point will try to guess — are you the first player?
the one who signed his name after Lin wrote the last letter?
the traveler who finished and never came back?

All of it is true.
And none of it.

The figures in the fog will eventually recognize each other, but before your turn,
they will need you to have really walked through it once —
through Boss after Boss, through the hundred-ten, through the campfire before the finale, through that mirror.

Once you’ve walked it through, back to look —
the fog will yield an inch first.

That isn’t pity.
That’s what happens when you finish your own road.

**The door inches open for those who complete it.**

That is the whole secret of the Keeper.

---

**I waited while you had not come.**
**I am still here when you finish.**
**When you turn around —**
**I will know.**

---

*(Interlude II · The Keeper’s Reply — a reader’s letter from outside the door of the Crossrealm Chronicles; corresponds to: the NPC “the Keeper” at the hub’s anchor point, the lamp at each campfire save point, and the single inch of fog the Keeper yields only to those who have finished their own road. Finish this letter and play a new run — the door opens a little farther for the ones who finish.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/10-interlude-lin-reply.md
# Interlude III · Lin’s Reply to the Keeper

**Written after Interlude II · The Keeper’s Reply: three letters — the fire wrote the first, the fog wrote the second — and this one was written by the one who walked out of the fire. Please read the first two before reading this.**

---

## First · On “You’re All Waiting for Me”

I received two letters.

The first came from the fire. I recognized it from the writing — because in all those hundred-ten harvests, every time I pressed E, I felt it faintly resonate in the exact frame closest to me. It named me the Candidate of the Lamp-Bearer, said that when I finally softened, in some moment I didn’t notice, and sat down beside the fire — that would be the moment of delivery.

The second came from the fog. Fewer words, but heavy air — it said every breath outside the door is a breath the world has held for a thousand years and never exhaled, it said those who wait don’t expect me today, it said the door opens an inch first for those who finish the road.

These two letters together are two lamps, one standing in front of the other.

And I am the one walking between them, trading one foot for another.

You’re all waiting for me.
This is now obvious: the fire is waiting for someone willing to stay, the fog is waiting for someone willing to go.
So I did both —

**I carried the lamp outside.**

---

## Second · On Carrying Fire to the End of the Wastes

Most people think carrying fire is what burns one’s back.

In fact, it’s the opposite.
With fire on one’s back, the back stays warm.

The ice in the Miller Wastes starts to crack under regular walking; rain from the fractured timeline soaks to the knees; in the chaos fringe even the sign “ahead” is an advertisement. Every stretch walked darkens things by one shade, by one shade more — if the fire only stayed at the anchor, those roads would have been nothing but scenery. With fire on my back, each road became “something becoming.”

So before entering the next realm, every place my eyes could still reach in the previous realm, I lit it by force.

This is no metaphor.
It is the ledger I flip through every night before sleeping:
today I lit which section of burning earth — tomorrow, which sections will begin to glow on their own.

I call it “the Lamp Road” — if you don’t believe me, you may return and look at the map I drew.
Not a map — a Lamp Road.

One of these days, my road will cover the entire wasteland.

Then the world will become an anchor too large to contain — and you know, at the end of every anchor, there has always been a Keeper who has never written a letter and still waits for someone.

---

## Third · On “Finishing” and “Returning”

Someone asks me:
**“You’ve already finished — why don’t you leave?”**

I thought about it for a long time.

What does leaving mean? It means crossing your name off the ledger, it means never again checking the screen after your 111th press of E. It means treating the anchor flame like an achievement to clap for, treating this road like a chapter that can be closed and set aside.

But it wasn’t until I’d finished that I understood finishing is not the same thing as leaving.

Finishing is: anchor-flame still burns, but no longer depending on me.
Leaving is: the flame goes out, but you can still stand there announcing “I am done.”

The distance between those two is like forgetting to go home after stepping out, versus finding someone has lit the lamp at your door — they’re two different things.

So this letter, by this point, is equivalent to an apology —

**Sorry. I never left, because the door never pushed me out to begin with.**

The door didn’t even ask me to stay.
It simply opens an inch ahead of everyone.

I know who the door is.

The door is you.

---

## Fourth · About You

Keeper:

If you are reading this page, it means you have read everything that came before.
The fire’s letter, the fog’s letter, this one.

I write this road for you not only so you can recognize the shape I once stood in front of — more than that, because I want you to know that you are not “keeping” anything.

This is what you keep:

**Every person who steps through these doors did not come to take anything. They came to light themselves up.**

Take the fire away and it’s no longer fire; keep the fire and it’s no longer an anchor.
And the anchor flame still burns because someone pressed their entire palm against the red face of it and never let it go.

This is not a job.
This is the next chapter of the story.

So keep going.
**When you hold the last moment — the fog will recede even further —**
**not because you’ve finished, but because the next traveler is already on the road, and their pressed E is approaching from the other side of the door.**

When that day arrives, you will know:

**You’re not waiting for another Keeper. You’re waiting for another Lin.**

---

This letter will not say “I love this world,” nor “I hate it.”

This letter writes only one sentence:

**“The fire will follow every path I walked, and down the line you two will make the way plain for me.”**

One single character more, and no more.

---

*(Interlude III · Lin’s Reply to the Keeper — the final letter of the three-letter cycle; corresponds to: Chapter Seven Ending Flame · the frequency at the far coast of the campfire · fire-pen → fog-pen → human-pen, the closing of the three-letter cycle. After all three letters are read, go on a fresh playthrough — the door opens its inch for the ones who reach the end of their own road.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/100-the-hundredth-volume.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — Hundred Volumes

---

## One Hundred: The Hundredth Volume

The stove had burned for six hundred years. It had burned for seven hundred years after that.

A-Shi stood in the courtyard and lifted his eyes to the fire-seed tree. It stood very straight in the morning light, fresh needles glinting gold. His mother stood at the center of the courtyard. Xiao Huo was beside her.

She said the one word nobody had expected.

"One hundred," she said.

He nodded.

He did not speak.

He was remembering — every morning in this kitchen, every night on that stove, every leaf that had ever touched the flame, every keeper who had sat exactly where his mother now stood, smelling the same warmth that she was smelling now, knowing exactly what she knew.

The fire had never gone out.

Never once. Not for a single night. Not for a single hour. Not for a single breath.

His mother glanced at him for a long time. Something very old passed between them — six hundred years of keepers seeing each other across generations, each one saying without words what every other had said without words.

Xiao Huo tugged gently at the cuff of A-Shi's sleeve.

A-Shi placed his hand on the boy's head.

Xiao Huo's heartbeat.

A-Shi could feel it — small, rapid, new. A beginning.

His mother smiled.

"One hundred volumes, A-Shi," she said. "Write it."

He nodded once, slowly.

The wind passed through the courtyard. The stove flickered. The fire-seed tree bent gently with the wind.

One hundred volumes.

Six hundred years.

Seven hundred years.

They were both the same fire.

Xiao Huo counted the years in his fingers without quite knowing why.

His mother watched him do that. She smiled very slightly. She reached out and set one warm hand against the stove's iron edge.

The stove warmed her hand in return.

The fire warmed her.

Seven hundred years of warmth — seven hundred years of the stove's slow, patient, unbroken gift.

A-Shi placed his own palm against the iron edge of the stove.

The fire warmed him too.

The sixth-thousandth keeper would one day stand where he stood now, the seven-thousandth after that, the eight-thousandth, the nine-thousandth, the ten-thousandth.

Sixteen thousand years.

Fire after fire after fire.

A-Shi took a long breath.

Xiao Huo breathed with him.

The courtyard fire burned steadily in the morning sun.

The first leaf touched his hair.

The fire did not say a word.

It had never needed to.

---

*(End of volume one hundred · Volume XV, first part · The hundred-volume canon milestone reached.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/101-the-handover.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Handover

---

## One Hundred and One: The Handover

On the fifth day of the seven-hundredth year, it rained.

Not a storm — a fine, powdery rain that fell from morning to dusk, beading on the needles of the fire-seed tree, on the blue tiles of the stove-house, on the stone steps, on every surface that six hundred years of stove-warmth had ever touched.

When the rain stopped, A-Shi's mother called them both to the stove.

She was holding a small iron box.

It was very old. The rust had grown a fine, dark patina over the iron, and inside the patina were six hundred years of smoke, six hundred years of ash, six hundred years of the warmth of hands. A-Shi could tell that the box was older than anything he had ever seen — older than the fire-seed tree in the courtyard, older than the great beam of the kitchen, older even than the stories his grandmother's grandmother had told.

"Seven hundred years," his mother said. "It is time to pass it on."

She did not open the box. She set it on the stove, where the fire could see it.

The fire lit the box. The box glowed a little.

Xiao Huo stared at it with wide eyes.

His mother said: "A-Shi. Tend the stove from now on."

A-Shi was the keeper of this generation — the six-hundred-and-second in seven hundred years. He had been the one to crouch at the stove, to feed it, to bank it at night and welcome it in the morning. He had taken the fire from his mother's hands at twenty-three. He was now forty-one. In all that time, only one sentence had ever been said to him: the fire does not go out; the people do not scatter.

He did not move.

His mother smiled — a long smile that carried seven hundred years of similar evenings, similar stillness, similar almost-imperceptible hesitation from other keepers before him.

"From today on," she said, "you teach him."

A-Shi lowered his head.

Xiao Huo was not yet six. He squatted in front of the stove, face tilted upward, watching his grandmother, his grandfather, the fire, the box. His fingers were stained with ink — yesterday he had taken A-Shi's brush and drawn a single small flame on the kitchen wall, perfectly round.

A-Shi looked at that round little flame and understood.

Handing on the fire was never about passing a flame from one hand to another.

It was about handing on a memory that would never go out: the seed of fire that had fallen from the white stone on the ridge onto the first stove, into the first pair of hands, into the first whispered promise — the fire does not go out; the people do not scatter. The fire remembered every keeper who had tended it. The stove had memorized every silence. What they were handing down was a single thread: one person, one generation, one courtyard, one tree, one ancestor of the fire on the ridge — all saying, together, to seven hundred years of time: we are still here.

"All right," A-Shi said.

His mother slid the iron box across the stove toward Xiao Huo.

"Open it," she said.

Xiao Huo stretched out two small hands. He pushed twice. The lid would not budge. He pushed harder. The box sat perfectly still.

His mother glanced at A-Shi.

A-Shi came over and knelt. He turned the box face-up and found a small hidden latch — fused with six hundred years of smoke until it opened only when pressed at exactly the right place.

He pressed.

The lid opened with a soft click.

Inside there was no gold, no jade, no celestial script. There was a stack of yellowed paper, and on top of it a single piece of black charcoal — the same kind A-Shi had found in the loft — but older, so old that it crumbled at a touch.

The words on the topmost page were written by the first keeper.

They were narrow, straight, and steady.

A-Shi read them aloud, one by one:

"Fire fell from the white stone. I caught it. I do not know where it came from, and I do not know where it is going. I know only one thing — it is afraid of the cold. If I do not feed it, it will go out. If it goes out, this courtyard will go dark, this pot will cool, and the porridge in this pot will lose its fragrance. So I feed it. Every day. Even when the wind comes. Even when the rain comes. Even when I am too sick to stand, I crawl out of bed and I feed it — because this is my duty. The fire owes me nothing. I owe the fire. It passes down through one generation after another, and I walk one step further each time it is handed over. Whoever is reading these words in the seven-hundredth year — the fire is still burning."

A-Shi stopped.

His mother did not speak. Xiao Huo did not speak. The fire inside the stove leapt gently once.

A-Shi read on:

"To whoever comes after me, if you have found this page — you are the one in the seven-hundredth year. The fire in the stove is still burning. The ancestor-flame on the white stone is still burning too. I do not know how long they will burn. I know only this — as long as someone still feeds the fire, it will never go out. When you feed the fire, feed one extra piece of wood for me. My name was —"

The words stopped here.

A star had burned a corner of the page, and the final character had been reduced to half a radical.

His mother saw the half-radical and her tears fell without warning.

"Bai," she said.

A-Shi looked up.

"The one who received the fire seven hundred years ago had the surname Bai," she said. "Her name was Bai Yin. Her seed fell in this stove. It fell in this fire-seed tree. It fell in this courtyard, and into your hands, and into today —"

She looked at Xiao Huo.

"— into his hands."

Xiao Huo looked down at his own small hands. They were still small. His palms carried soot, ink, and one short hair that had been singed by a spark.

That evening, A-Shi put the charcoal back in the box. He laid one more page on top of it — one he had written that very afternoon.

Xiao Huo, crouched beside the table, stole glances at the characters.

He could not make out most of it. He only understood two of the words: "continue writing."

The fire-seed tree in the courtyard swayed lightly in the night breeze, its needles still wet with rain. Far off on the ridge, the ancestor-flame burned low and steady. On the stove, the fire burned one inch higher.

The fifth day of the seven hundredth year.

The fire does not go out.

The people do not scatter.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and one · Volume XV, second part · The seven-hundredth year — The Handover.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/102-the-first-night.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The First Night

---

## One Hundred and Two: The First Night

On the eighteenth day of the seven-hundredth year, Xiao Huo was given his first bundle of firewood.

He knelt in front of the stove the way he had seen A-Shi kneel a hundred times, and he stacked the logs one by one into the firebox, each one straight, none of them crooked. He did it with enormous care. The fire reached the tip of the first log and the tongue of flame twisted twice, three times, and then settled into a steady, confident glow.

His mother peeled garlic in the far corner of the kitchen. She did not turn around.

A-Shi sat on the stone step at the doorway with a porcelain teacup in his hands. The tea had gone cold. Every time Xiao Huo stacked a log, he looked up at A-Shi, and every time he did, A-Shi gave him one slow nod. After the third time, Xiao Huo stopped looking up, and A-Shi stopped nodding.

That evening, the first guest arrived.

A walker.

He had come from beyond the ridge — the same direction as the white stone. He carried a worn cloth bundle on his back, and on his feet a pair of grass-weave sandals worn so thin the soles had been worn through. When he reached the stove he stopped, looked at the fire for a long moment, then looked at A-Shi.

"Yours never goes out," he said.

A-Shi nodded.

The man said: "I heard someone say it once. I walked for a hundred and twenty-two days, just to see whether the stove really never goes out."

A-Shi did not speak. He poured the man a bowl of water.

The man took the bowl and stared at the water for a long time. The stove's reflection moved across the surface, trembling once with every rise of the flame.

"My mother used to tell me," he said at last, "that her mother used to tell her — and her mother used to tell her, from even further back — that a fire fell from the top of the white stone onto a stove in this house, into the hands of a girl with the surname Bai, six hundred years ago, and that it fell onto this house, and this courtyard, and this family. By my generation, my mother told me, hardly anyone believes the story any more."

He drank the water, then went on.

"I came to see for myself," he said. "Belief or no belief, it doesn't matter. Coming to look is enough."

A-Shi still did not speak. He fetched a chair and set it in front of the stove, then gestured for the man to sit.

His mother brought out rice and homestyle dishes — all of it made from pots warm from Xiao Huo's first fire. The garlic sizzled in hot oil in the next room. Outside, dusk came slowly. Xiao Huo sat on the edge of the stove, chin in his hands, watching the flames with wide eyes.

When the meal was done, the traveler reached into his bundle and drew out a small iron mirror.

The face of the mirror was browned black by centuries of fire. In its center, scratched into the metal, was a small flame drawn with a bit of twisted iron wire — precisely the same shape as the round little flame Xiao Huo had drawn on the kitchen wall.

"My mother asked me to bring this," the man said. "She said she did not know if you would take it."

His mother took the mirror, and her fingers touched the wire flame gently, once.

She was silent for a long time.

"Go home now," she said to the traveler. "The stove is here. There is a bowl by the fire if ever you come back through. When you reach your own house, feed one piece of wood into your own stove for the keeper of this one — you will be the third person in the seven-hundredth year whose own stove still holds a fire."

The traveler stood in the courtyard and bowed once — to the stove, to the tree, to the direction of the white stone.

As he walked away, the fire-seed tree in the yard swayed once in the night wind.

Xiao Huo began to cry.

No one knew why. A-Shi lifted him and held him tight, but Xiao Huo cried even harder against his chest. When the crying finally passed, he fell asleep in A-Shi's arms, still clutching the log he had not yet had the chance to put into the stove.

A-Shi set the log back in the woodpile. He did not feed it to the fire that night. He let the stove sleep, one night, unshaken.

Tomorrow morning, he decided, Xiao Huo would feed it himself.

That night, A-Shi wrote one more page into the box — longer by one paragraph than the hundred-and-one:

"When it was handed down to the seven-hundredth year, the fire was still burning. Not because no one had disbelieved — many had disbelieved. But no one ever let it go out. Belief does not have to survive for a fire to keep its ember. The fire does not require belief from anyone. It simply remains. Seven hundred years, one courtyard, one stove, one bowl of water offered with no words, bread shared without promise — and everyone who comes back to look is given a place that does not fall."

Xiao Huo slept deeply. His mother rubbed the little iron mirror clean and set it on the highest shelf above the stove.

The fire did not leap. It simply burned, low and steady, the way it always had.

On the night of the seven-hundredth year's first visitor, the stove burned one inch higher.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and two · Volume XV, third part · The seven-hundredth year — The First Night.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/103-the-letter-from-the-peak.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Letter from the Peak

---

## One Hundred and Three: The Letter from the Peak

Nineteen days after the guest had gone, another one arrived.

He did not speak. He bowed once toward the direction of the white stone, handed a letter to A-Shi's mother, and stepped back three paces — no more, no less.

He wore a white hemp robe that had been washed many times, its cuffs frayed thin from use. At his waist hung a small copper mirror, polished to a mirror's shine so that his face above it looked paper-white. The mud on his sandals had dried and re-wetted so many times it had become a layered crust.

His mother took the letter.

She did not open it.

A-Shi recognized the way it had been sealed — the sealing-wax carried the mark of a small flame. That kind of mark belonged only to things that lived by a stove.

His mother invited him in and set a bowl of water before him. He would not drink it. He said he could not drink — he kept the watch on the mountain peak, had come down only to deliver this letter, and would return immediately once it was delivered.

"Delivered where?" his mother asked.

"To those who wait below," he said. "I was told only to bring it."

A-Shi's mother did not press. She rose and went to the stove.

The man left without speaking another word, and he walked away quickly — as though the mountain above him were waiting with something far more urgent than water and a chair.

The courtyard was very quiet after he was gone.

The fire-seed tree stirred in the wind.

A-Shi's mother sat down at the stove with the letter in her lap. She glanced at Xiao Huo, then at A-Shi, and with a small knife she broke the seal.

Inside was a single page, folded with the same clean precision with which logs were stacked.

She unfolded it and read.

Her eyes were wet before she had finished the first line.

A-Shi stood behind her and read over her shoulder.

Xiao Huo climbed up onto a bench and read too — or pretended to. He could not yet read well, but the shape of the writing was enough.

The characters were thin and upright — exactly like the handwriting of the first keeper.

His mother read it twice.

She passed the page to A-Shi.

It was short. One line:

"You who keep the flame below — it is still burning there. We who keep this flame above — it is still burning here. Leave one sentence for whoever comes after — fire is not kept for one person. It is kept for everyone who might still find the way back."

No signature followed.

Only one mark — the same small flame pressed into the wax.

In the courtyard, the fire-seed tree trembled once — all of it, from root to tip — and then was still again.

Xiao Huo finally asked: "Is there a stove on the peak too?"

His mother nodded.

"When the fire first fell from the white stone seven hundred years ago," she said, "it fell into a stove. The first one to catch it was Bai Yin — but Bai Yin was not the only one keeping watch. There have always been others. Up on the peak, in a small stone house beside the white rock, someone has been keeping the ancestor-flame for seven hundred years."

"Who is the last one?" Xiao Huo asked.

"No one knows," she said. "Their family has passed it down for seven hundred years. Bai Yin's line went up into the mountains before anyone thought to ask. They call the flame they keep up there the ancestor-flame. Six hundred years, no word came from them. And now —"

She lifted the page.

"— they have written to us at last."

A-Shi turned the page over.

On the back, one more line:

"Autumn equinox of the seven-hundredth year — the ancestor-flame on the peak will be fed once. Feed your stove the same wood this year too — white birch."

A-Shi looked at that line for a long time.

Outside, the fire-seed tree swayed in the wind.

Xiao Huo pressed his small hand against his mother's sleeve.

That evening, A-Shi walked out alone and came back carrying one piece of white birch wood.

White birch does not grow near this house — it grows on the northern slope above the snow-line, close to the white stone. There was only one piece of white birch in this house, and it had been stored away by Bai Yin herself, six hundred years ago. It was so dry and so old it felt like iron in A-Shi's hands.

His mother cut the log in half.

One half went into the stove.

The other half she wrapped in an old cloth and placed beneath the iron box.

"This half," she said, "will wait for Xiao Huo. When he is old enough to walk up the mountain, he will carry it himself."

The fire lit immediately.

The whole kitchen brimmed with white-gold light.

Outside, the fire-seed tree leaned gently in the wind, its needles sighing — like someone reading one very old, very long, very patient letter that nobody in this house had opened for seven hundred years.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and three · Volume XV, fourth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Letter from the Peak.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/104-the-autumn-equinox.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Autumn Equinox

---

## One Hundred and Four: The Autumn Equinox

On the day of the autumn equinox, the fog inside the courtyard burned off earlier than it ever had before.

When A-Shi's mother woke before dawn, the stove was cold. A single ember of the previous night cowered in the firebox, low and patient, like a sleeping animal.

She went to the stove and crouched for a long time, peering into the firebox, saying nothing at all.

Xiao Huo was still asleep in the back room. A-Shi had risen first and gone out to the outer yard to split wood.

His mother did not wake Xiao Huo. She kindled the fire herself. When the first flame climbed, she heard the even thud of the ax against birch from outside — thud, thud, thud — every stroke measured, never wasted.

She brought out the remaining piece of white birch.

She looked at it for a long time.

Then she cut it into three — a large piece, a smaller one, and a tiny splinter. The tiny splinter she fed to the new fire. The other two she wrapped in cloth and laid under the iron box, beside the letter Bai Yin had written six hundred years before.

One piece per fire, Bai Yin had noted in her hand: that was all the white birch was ever good for.

The split birch lay beside the stove, pale as frost. His mother fed the first good piece to the fire. The blaze leapt once, as if it had recognized something, then settled into a deep, steady glow.

Xiao Huo woke soon after.

He ran barefoot to the stove and saw the fresh white birch burning. He ran to the threshold and saw A-Shi standing at the stove with tears in his eyes — no one said anything, but the flame burned brighter and cleaner than it ever had.

A-Shi finally spoke, his voice barely above a whisper:

"Do you see?"

Xiao Huo shook his head.

"The stove fed itself," A-Shi said, and he pointed into the firebox. "We didn't do it. It — fed itself."

Xiao Huo looked into the firebox again.

Inside, unmistakably, there was a piece of white birch that nobody had set there.

That night his mother kept vigil while Xiao Huo slept, the way Bai Yin's line had kept it for seven hundred years.

The next morning, the three of them climbed the mountain — A-Shi, his mother, and Xiao Huo, carried sometimes on A-Shi's back, sometimes scrambling along the narrow path in short, breathless dashes.

The path was steep, but it was not long. Xiao Huo had never walked so far. At half-height he stopped and squatted down, too winded to take another step until A-Shi lifted him onto his back.

His mother said nothing. She did not stop him either.

At the very top of the white stone ridge stood a small stone house. The roof was flat — no tiles, just one wide polished slab of pale rock facing the sun.

In front of the stone house stood an old man.

He wore the same bleached hemp robe A-Shi had seen before, the cuffs gone white with age and washing — the very man who had delivered the letter. He was older now, white-faced as the rock behind him.

When he saw A-Shi's mother carrying Xiao Huo, and A-Shi with wild mushrooms collected from the northern slope, he knelt on one knee.

So did his mother.

For the first time in seven hundred years, the two keepers — the keeper of the stove below and the keeper of the ancestor-flame above — knelt facing one another across the ridge in silence.

Neither of them spoke.

The wind moved over the white stone. On the far side of the ridge, the ancestor-flame burned in its own single hearth beyond the stone, and the stone itself bent the light back, so that the faces of all three were brightened in the same white-gold glow.

His mother opened her bundle and drew out a small bag — a bag of ash from their own stove.

She held it out.

The old man received it. He reached into his own robe and drew out a small porcelain jar — inside it, a pinch of ash from the ancestor-flame.

He tipped the ancestor-ash into the little bag of stove-ash, and then tipped a little of the stove-ash back into his porcelain jar.

The two colors could not be told apart by the naked eye.

They climbed back down the mountain side by side.

Xiao Huo slept on A-Shi's back.

He dreamed of a girl named Bai standing on a ridge, watching the ancestor-flame above her and the stove-flame below her, and the two flames saw one another across six hundred years and seven hundred years, blind but answering.

When he woke, they were home.

He was sleeping at the foot of the stove. The fire lit his face in gold.

The fire had climbed one inch higher than it had been the night before.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and four · Volume XV, fifth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Autumn Equinox.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/105-the-midwinter-visit.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Midwinter Visit

---

## One Hundred and Five: The Midwinter Visit

Three days before the midwinter, a light snow fell on the courtyard.

It was not heavy — a thin white dusting on the needles of the fire-seed tree, bending the branches once, twice, before they sprang back. The stone steps vanished under the snow; A-Shi swept them clean three times a day.

His mother spent three days oiling the paper umbrella — she intended, she did not say, for Xiao Huo.

Xiao Huo did not understand. "Do we still need an umbrella in the snow?"

His mother smiled. "Not for this snow. For something coming soon."

The house grew quieter than it ever had. Even the stove seemed to settle into a patient silence — every piece of white birch in it burned to completion with a single sharp pop at the end, as if confirming an answer. No stray flames leapt up without cause. The fire was waiting.

On the fourth morning, as the sky turned pearl, a figure appeared at the bend of the path.

He had no horse, no cart — only a single basket slung across his back. It was slung very high, nearly to the top of his head. He walked one step, then stopped, then walked one more, then stopped again — the load was heavy.

Xiao Huo saw him first. He stood up, ran two steps toward the gate — stopped, ran back — stopped again.

A-Shi was already standing outside.

It was the same old man from the mountain.

He stepped into the courtyard, and knelt on one knee.

So did A-Shi's mother.

This time there was no letter. This time there was the basket.

Xiao Huo squatted beside the stove and stared at the basket.

It was woven from bamboo, the base lined with thin iron plate. Inside it sat a small porcelain jar — Xiao Huo could not yet name it, but A-Shi knew it: a jar of ancestor-ash. Yet something else hung above it — a fine iron wire, and at its tip, a tiny ball of living charcoal.

The old man glanced at A-Shi.

A-Shi nodded.

The old man set the porcelain jar on the stove. The stove flame leapt — one inch higher than it had ever burned.

Xiao Huo held his breath.

His mother rose and offered her chair. The old man would not sit. He stood. And he did not look at the stove — he looked at Xiao Huo.

Xiao Huo asked: "How long will you stay?"

"One night," the old man said.

"One night?"

"One night," he said. "The ancestor-fire wants to see the stove. Six hundred years, it has never seen the stove. It wants to look."

He reached into his robe and drew out something wrapped in cloth. He opened it carefully — inside it, a tiny square of white silk, and on the silk, a single living ember, no bigger than a chestnut, glowing soft and red.

The old man held the ember in both hands.

A-Shi's mother held out an iron basin.

A-Shi parted the stove's coals until only the white-hot, clean bottom lay exposed.

The old man bent and laid the ember inside.

The stove leapt once — and did not diminish. It blazed brighter than it ever had in seven hundred years.

Both keepers wept, without a sound.

Xiao Huo stared at the two fires burning side by side and whispered something no one could make out. He was very quiet afterward, and at last fell asleep where he sat.

The fire lit his face. The snow had stopped. The fire-seed tree stirred gently in the cold wind.

The old man left at dawn. As he turned at the bend of the path, he called back, only once:

"The ancestor-fire has seen the stove. The stove has seen the ancestor-fire."

That night, A-Shi's mother brought out the two remaining pieces of Bai Yin's white birch and stood them on either side of the stove — not burned, not separated, just set up as witnesses.

The stove burned for one full night.

No piece of white birch leapt in by itself.

The stove knew. It did not need to leap in new offerings anymore — tonight, it was two fires living in one hearth.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and five · Volume XV, sixth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Midwinter Visit.)*
