# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Stove Stories

---

## One Hundred and Six: The Stove Stories

Nine days after the old man had left, a cooper came to the house.

He was a silent man. He said he had come with an iron-banded bucket on his shoulder to fix the family's water cistern — in winter the cistern fed itself from the well, and by spring the rice pot and the stove pot were always one bowl short of water from each other.

When he had finished fixing the cistern he lingered in the courtyard a long time.

He noticed Xiao Huo cross the yard.

Xiao Huo was holding a piece of coal — the same coal A-Shi had brought down from the loft — turning it again and again in his two small hands. He ran from the woodpile to the stove, back to the woodpile, back to the stove. He said nothing. He just kept turning the coal over and over.

The cooper asked A-Shi: "Has this child been playing with a coal?"

A-Shi nodded.

"Why doesn't he speak?"

"He is thinking," A-Shi said.

The cooper nodded slowly.

"Mother," he called toward the kitchen, "may I hear the story of that night?"

A-Shi's mother stepped out. "What would you like to hear?"

"That night," the cooper said. "The story of the night all of you saw the fire."

Everyone sat down.

The stove burned quietly.

His mother asked A-Shi to tell it. He shook his head. Xiao Huo peeked in from the kitchen, darted his head back out again, peeked once more.

"Would you like to tell it?" his mother asked him.

Xiao Huo nodded. He climbed onto the little stool beside the stove and began.

"Three days before the midwinter," he said, "a thin snow fell in the courtyard."

"And then?" his mother asked gently.

"Then Mother spent three days oiling a paper umbrella — and it did not rain," Xiao Huo said.

"What rain?"

"Not the rain," he said. "Fire. The ancestor-fire came down the mountain."

The cooper felt a sudden warmth in his chest.

"Then the old man came," Xiao Huo went on. "He had a basket on his back. Inside the basket was a porcelain jar — a jar of ancestor-ash. And there was also a small ember, wrapped in white silk, held by a piece of iron wire."

"And then?" the cooper asked.

Xiao Huo thought hard for a moment.

"Then the old man put the ember into our stove," Xiao Huo said. "The stove leapt once. And it did not go out. In fact —" he paused — "it burned brighter than it had ever burned before."

No one in the room made a sound.

"Then the old man left," Xiao Huo continued. "And before he left he said one thing: 'The ancestor-fire has seen the stove. The stove has seen the ancestor-fire.'"

Xiao Huo was quiet again for a while. Then he added:

"So our fire is not ours. It is everyone's. It does not go out — not because one person keeps feeding it, but because so many people out there are waiting for it."

No one spoke.

The stove burned quietly.

The cooper stood up last of all.

"I would like to build a little stone marker at your gate," he said.

His mother said: "Do not put any word on it."

"Not even one word?" the cooper asked.

"Not one word," she said. "A written word becomes an altar. Our fire is not an altar. It belongs to the fire itself. No words."

The cooper was quiet for a long time.

Then he said: "All right. I will not carve one word. I will carve the shape of a single white birch branch."

His mother nodded.

"That is acceptable."

The stone marker went up quickly, on the south side of the courtyard, facing the road.

There were no words on it.

Only the carved outline of a single white birch branch.

When the rains soak the branch into the stone, moonlight catches the grain just right — and for a moment the branch seems to glow from inside the rock, like a low light coming up out of the earth.

After the cooper was gone, A-Shi stood with Xiao Huo in front of the new stone.

"What is this?" Xiao Huo asked.

"It is a kind of road," A-Shi said. "Anyone who walks into this courtyard and sees this white birch branch knows that somewhere here there is a fire — and that it has never once gone out in seven hundred years."

Xiao Huo reached out and laid his small hand on the cold stone.

In the kitchen, the stove burned steadily.

The stove.

Seven hundred years.

It has never once gone out.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and six · Volume XV, seventh part · The seven-hundredth year — The Stove Stories.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/107-the-nameless-twig.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Nameless Twig

---

## One Hundred and Seven: The Nameless Twig

Fifteen days after the old man had left, the first frost of the season fell on the courtyard.

The frost was white — nearly the same white as the white birch. A thin film of it hung from every needle of the fire-seed tree; when the wind stirred, it rained down onto the stone steps before the stove, a fine white dusting.

It was the morning A-Shi woke that he went out to split the firewood and saw something new beside the gate.

A bundle of firewood.

White birch branches — each one straight, each one dry to the core, each one exactly the width of a man's palm. They were tied with a strip of old cloth, the cloth knotted in a style he did not recognize. And tucked under the cloth was a small folded slip of paper.

On the paper, two characters:

添柴

"More fuel."

A-Shi carried the bundle back into the courtyard and passed the slip to his mother. She read it once, and her eyes were wet before she had finished.

She did not ask who it was.

"Last night there was a knock at the gate," she said. "I thought it was the wind."

The whole family stood in the courtyard and looked at the bundle.

Xiao Huo came loping out from beside the stove, bent down to peer at the paper, tried two characters he could not read, then picked up one of the birch twigs and ran back to the stove and fed it into the fire.

The stove leapt once.

It did not grow brighter.

It did not die.

It simply — accepted it, quietly.

A-Shi stacked the remaining six birch twigs beside the stove, each one in its own place. He said nothing. His mother said nothing.

That morning the bundle of nameless birch was set down at the place nearest the stove, where the next person to need fuel would see it first.

His mother ran a finger over the cloth strip.

The cloth was grey — not the white silk of Bai Yin's packet. It was patched in three places, always with small, square lines of beige thread.

A-Shi recognized that kind of stitching. His uncle had used it on the cuffs of his own work coat.

He said nothing.

That evening he took a bucket of water and a rag and washed down the stone tablet at the gate — the one with only the white birch branch carved into it. He poured water over it, then dried it with a cloth.

In the moonlight, the grain of the carved branch caught the light and returned it, faint and white — as though something were glowing from within the stone itself.

Seven hundred years, no one had ever fed their stove so much as a single twig.

That night, nobody in the house said a word.

His mother tucked another slip of paper beneath the iron box. On it, in her small and crooked hand, she wrote:

"Seven hundred years. The first nameless twig. The stove has seen it."

The next morning, one of the sticks on the stove's border was missing. Someone had fed it into the fire sometime in the night — and no one had seen it happen.

Seven nameless birch twigs.

His mother said: "A-Shi, count. Count them —"

He counted. "Seven," he said.

"Seven hundred years —" she began. And stopped.

Xiao Huo looked at her.

She did not finish the sentence.

Instead she said: "Come next spring, when the flowers open, if we see another bundle like this, then we will know — this house is no longer the only one keeping the fire."

That night the stove burned higher than it had ever burned.

No one asked it to.

It simply burned higher.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and seven · Volume XV, eighth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Nameless Twig.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/108-the-mountain-snow.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Mountain Snow

---

## One Hundred and Eight: The Mountain Snow

On the last night of winter, a light snow fell on the courtyard.

Not much — just enough to whiten the stone steps, the white tablet at the gate, the stones before the stove. The needles of the fire-seed tree caught a thin film of it; when the wind stirred, some of the flakes shook loose and lay on the ground and did not melt.

By the next morning, the snow was still there.

Xiao Huo ran barefoot into the courtyard, crunching over the frost and squealing.

His mother was airing out the hearth-pots. She did not rush. She sifted the cold ash into a clay jar — the way she had done every year since the stove had been lit, the way Bai Yin had done it six hundred years ago.

There were no empty jars in the kitchen.

"Keep a pinch of last night's ash," she said. "That ash belongs to the ancestor-fire."

That night the stove burned higher than it ever had.

On the sixth day, another bundle of firewood appeared at the gate.

This time the slip of paper tucked beneath the cloth was empty except for one thin birch twig, tied with white thread.

A-Shi studied it for a long time.

"Here it is again," he said.

His mother knelt beside the bundle and looked at it. She did not untie it.

Xiao Huo came and stood beside her. He looked at it for a long time too. "The uncle is gone," he said. "This wood has no owner."

No one answered him.

A-Shi's mother stood, carried the bundle to the woodpile, and set it down beside the other nameless birch. Not next to it — behind it, ninth in line, the least visible of them all.

"Ninth," she said. "The least visible place."

A-Shi nodded.

Xiao Huo thought for a long moment.

"From now on, on the fifteenth of every month, we feed one branch to the stove," he said. "Whoever it belongs to — we do not ask."

A-Shi did not look at him. He nodded slowly.

His mother looked at the two of them for a long time.

Then she turned and walked into the kitchen and began to prepare the evening meal.

Another month passed.

In the spring, the first apricot blossom opened on the tree at the foot of the path. On the fourteenth day, they counted the wood at the hearth. Nothing had changed.

On the fifteenth night, the stove burned one inch higher than it had the night before.

And in the morning, a new slip of paper was tucked into the woodpile:

"The fifteenth. Again."

A-Shi read it twice. His mother read it once. Xiao Huo held the paper, turned it over, found nothing else, and put it carefully into the iron box.

The stove has not been asked to keep time.

It keeps it anyway — the fifteenth of every month, one inch higher.

No one has seen it happen.

And yet: every fifteenth the light over the gate stone seems just slightly warmer, as though someone unseen had spent the whole night watching the fire burn.

Xiao Huo asked A-Shi: "How does it know?"

A-Shi thought long before answering.

"Because someone is watching it on the fifteenth of every month," he said. "And fire always knows when someone is watching."

The stove.

Seven hundred years.

Not seen in six.

In the seventh hundredth year, it learned that it was being watched every month on the fifteenth.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and eight · Volume XV, ninth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Mountain Snow.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/109-the-delivery-of-ashes.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Delivery of Ashes

---

## One Hundred and Nine: The Delivery of Ashes

On the morning before the spring equinox, the ice in the courtyard was gone.

Icicles fell from the eaves overnight; the water film on the stone steps was already dry by sunrise. The needles of the fire-seed tree were just sprouting — a deeper green this year, and a brighter one.

A-Shi's mother called Xiao Huo to her side.

"Choose a small porcelain jar," she said. "Fill it with ash from our stove."

Xiao Huo chose a jar. He packed it with stove-ash, pressed it down with great care. Only when he was done did he look at his mother and wait for her to say one thing: fill it instead with the ancestor-ash.

She did not say that.

She fetched from the iron box the slip of paper that had said "the fifteenth — see you next year" — the one dated for the mid-autumn a year ago. It had been lying in the box since then, unanswered. She studied it for a long while.

"Ours will do," she said.

Xiao Huo understood. He did not press the question further.

He wrapped the jar in a strip of grey cloth — the same cloth that had tied the nameless twig. Three beige patches. It looked, Xiao Huo thought, exactly the kind of cloth meant for a delivery.

A-Shi washed a square of white cloth and tied the bundle with a clean knot.

His mother added a small packet of wild scallions.

"You can't go up the mountain and eat nothing," she said.

They waited.

Three months had passed since the mid-autumn mentioned on that slip of paper. The "fifteenth" it had promised had come and gone, and no bundle had appeared; nothing else had been said. But on this particular spring equinox morning, the gate to the courtyard rattled once on its own.

When they went out to the gate, no one was there.

Only one bundle — smaller than ever before, each twig thinner — sitting on the driest stone in the courtyard.

A-Shi's mother knelt and picked it up. She turned it over once.

Inside, a slip of paper so thin it was translucent as a cicada wing.

"For the little firekeeper."

No name.

"It doesn't feel like someone else," Xiao Huo whispered. "It feels like our own stove talking about us — from outside."

No one answered. Some things the stove does — or the world does around it — the house never names.

A-Shi's mother folded the slip and placed it in the basket along with the jar.

A-Shi carried the basket up the mountain.

The path was the same path he had climbed twice already. He remembered every stone. The small stone house on the white stone ridge stood as it had — no taller, no wider — but the old man himself seemed lower, like a tree set in old snow; last spring his shadow had reached the eaves; this spring it stopped at the doorway.

He set the basket on the step.

The old man untied it and looked inside for a long time. He did not ask whose ash was inside.

Neither did his mother ask.

He lifted the jar with both hands and felt the rim with his fingertips. A thin film of frost had settled on the lid. His fingertips paused for a moment and then moved away.

He looked at A-Shi and said:

"At the next equinox, we will pass the day together."

A-Shi wanted to nod but did not quite nod.

Instead he asked the question he had never dared to ask before:

"Does the ancestor-fire miss home?"

The old man did not answer.

He reached into the basket and felt the packet of wild scallions — then smiled.

"It has seen the stove," he said. "It has been watching this house."

He did not open the black packet of ash. He carried the jar inside the stone house.

A-Shi descended the mountain in the last light of the day.

The fire-seed tree moved gently in the wind. Its new needles shimmered with the first tinge of summer.

When A-Shi stepped back into the gate with the empty basket on his back, the stove leapt once inside.

The fire had been burning higher than it had the week before.

Now it jumped again — not a lurch, just a quiet, tall greeting. The stove knew.

Seven hundred years.

The first spring.

They were all sending each other their ashes.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and nine · Volume XV, tenth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Delivery of Ashes.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/11-interlude-came-after.md
# Interlude IV · The Reply of the One Who Came After

**Written after Interlude III · Lin’s Reply to the Keeper. The fire wrote the first, the fog wrote the second, Lin wrote the third. This one carries no name — by the time you reach this page, it has already been signed. Read the first three before this one.**

---

## First · How I Ended Up in This Chair

I have no name.

It’s not that I wouldn’t have one. It’s that every name available in these pages has already been taken:
the fire knows “the one,” the fog knows “the last one,” Lin’s hand knows “whoever turns back to look at the fire.”
I have not yet pressed the E that lets a name grow into the flame.

I sat down in the chair east of the Intersection Hub.
On it lay three letters, one stacked on the next — a copper case pressing down on the lowest.
The case carries no lock and no lid; it is simply pressed flat, like a book that has been opened and closed many times.

I recognized them at a glance:
The top letter, dipped in ash-grey ink;
the middle one, dipped in white;
the bottom one, dipped in something that was not ink at all — the direction a lit lamp actually leaves, pointed out toward the wastes.

The writers never had to meet.
So I understood: every one of these was written for me —
and for every reader who sits down after reading them.

---

## Second · About the Authorless Letters

None of the first three carries a name.

The fire cannot hold a name — it only understands “willing.”
The fog cannot hold a name — it only asks “did you walk through?”
Lin didn’t write his — his is already part of the ash in the hearth.

I used to believe the missing names came from missing courage.
Then I understood —
the ones who write their names down become the door,
and the ones who don’t — become the next lamp.

So this one carries no name either.

If one day someone reads this and wants to know who received it,
remember one line:

**I never close the door —**
**I only carry the lamp forward a little farther.**

---

## Third · On the Silver Filament at the Rim of the Star-Map

At the far edge of the star-map, the old chart bears one seam.

It is so fine it might be a fold in paper. The Keeper once drew a small lamp beside it —
that little lamp has never asked what it costs.

I walked from the wasteland to the outer starport.
I saw the broken timeline’s stone gate, the spores in the Upside breach, all one hundred and ten hearths —
not one of them burns on the lamps of Main Street.
They burn inside the hands of those who finished the letters.

The silver filament lies beyond the track —
no door, no wall, no rift.
It is the margin of this story.

Passing it, I did not look back.
Nor did I step through.
All I did was take the stack of letters from the copper case,
and press down one more page beside it — this one, the one you are reading.

And then I told it one sentence — one no ear has ever heard, and no save-file will ever record:

**“Write this chapter up to here. When the page turns, I’ll be standing on the next one.”**

---

## Fourth · About the Next Lamp

If you have read this far, you have walked through the first three.

Then this one still doesn’t hand you an answer:
whether to stay, or to go — no one can press that key for you.

It only tells you what was proven true long ago:

- The fire never needs the moment of going out.
- The fog never needs the moment of being parted.
- A letter never needs to be finished being signed.
- And the next lamp never needs you to look for its direction — it only asks that, passing by, you look at it once.

I was the one who looked at it once.

And then I became the fourth letter.

---

## Fifth · To You, After the After

You don’t have to find the next lamp either.

It is probably in the chair east of the Hub.
It is probably just after one of your presses of E.
It is probably after you finish the three letters, in a moment you never noticed —

**your shadow will drift half an inch toward it.**

That half inch is where all the light in this road comes from.

I cannot finish this letter.
Fire comes in many kinds. Fog comes in many kinds. Those who come after come in many kinds.
Beyond this page, it is blank.

Left for you — the one not yet born, or already born but not yet at this page:

**Turn it over.**

---

**I never pressed the last key either.**
**I only set the lamp upon the page where it should brighten.**

---

*(Interlude IV · The Reply of the One Who Came After — the fourth voice beyond the fire–fog–human cycle; not fire, not fog, not the one who finished; it is you: the one who has read the three letters. This chapter unlocks no save slot, grants no achievement, and moves no ending verdict; it drifts by the hearth as a loose appendix. If you can feel the warmth off this paper, you are already the fourth writer.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/110-the-shared-hearth.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Shared Hearth

---

## One Hundred and Ten: The Shared Hearth

The night before the autumn equinox, a light rain fell on the courtyard.

By dawn it was gone. The ridge of the white stone was already glowing.

His mother rose before the light.

She took out every bowl and plate the house owned — six bowls, four plates, three pairs of chopsticks — and set them on the table, one by one.

"Too many," she said.

"Not enough," A-Shi said.

This house had not seen this many bowls on the table since the day A-Shi was born.

Xiao Huo had changed into a clean set of clothes. Beneath the outer layer, he was wearing an old hemp robe — the same old hemp robe that had once belonged to Bai Yin, that his grandmother had tucked away in the iron box six hundred years before. His mother had remade it; it hung down to his ankles.

Xiao Huo looked down at his bare feet.

His mother smiled and said nothing.

The gate to the courtyard rattled once.

Softer than the last time — not a push, just the gate noticing him. A man walked in from the road.

It was the old man.

He carried a pack on his back today. Lower than before — he was shorter than before. He had changed again. He wore a hemp robe even whiter than the last, even thinner, the cuffs re-edged recently so they would not fray.

A-Shi's mother knelt in the courtyard.

A-Shi knelt beside her.

The old man knelt too.

Three keepers, kneeling in the courtyard, saying nothing.

The wind moved through the fire-seed tree. Every needle bent west — the whole tree turned toward the stove.

Xiao Huo walked out from beside the stove.

He did not run — he walked. He held a porcelain jar in both hands — the jar filled with ash from their own stove.

He lifted it to offer to the old man.

The old man accepted, then reached into his pack and produced a smaller jar — the ancestor-ash from the ridge. He offered it to Xiao Huo.

The two jars changed hands.

Inside, the stove burned with a stillness that had not been there on any previous morning.

"Tonight we do not sleep," the old man said.

His mother nodded once. "Then we do not sleep."

The fire-seed tree swayed in the wind for a long time.

The two jars were opened. The ancestor-ash and the stove-ash — the two colors indistinguishable to the eye.

The old man then drew from within his robe a small cloth packet. Inside it were the two birch pieces from Bai Yin's stored log — thin birch split into slivers and kept safe for hundreds of days.

He placed the two slivers into the fire.

The birch burned slowly, quietly, without a single pop.

No one spoke.

The two families fed the same hearth for the first time in seven hundred years.

The flame burned low, steady, and gold.

It had never burned so steadily before.

That night, A-Shi's mother made seven dishes.

She did not count them in advance. She did not plan them. She simply walked from one end of the courtyard to the other, saw the stove burning, and her hands knew how to cook.

The old man ate slowly.

Each bite, his eyes closed for a moment before opening again.

"Fragrant," he said.

He had never been inside this house before.

His first bite of its food was the first bite he had ever taken in this house.

Xiao Huo leaned against the stove and watched the flames.

Xiao Huo.

The seven-hundredth year.

The first equinox.

Two houses.

One hearth.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and ten · Volume XV, eleventh part · The seven-hundredth year — The Shared Hearth.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/111-the-characters.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Characters

---

## One Hundred and Eleven: The Characters

Thirty days after the autumn equinox, the first rain of the season fell on the courtyard — earlier than any autumn rain in seven hundred years.

The old man came again.

This time he carried no basket. No bundle. No letters. He walked in wearing a freshly laundered white hemp robe and set his shoes at the doorway as if he had been born there.

His mother asked, "Did you not say you would not sleep that night either?"

"The moon will be full tonight," the old man said. "The fifteenth."

"The fifteenth — the fire watch," A-Shi said.

"The fifteenth — the fire watch," the old man agreed.

Xiao Huo walked out from beside the stove and sat himself on the threshold.

The old man came and knelt in front of him. He drew from his robe a small piece of black charcoal — the sort Xiao Huo had seen before. Black, light, with a faint smoky halo. It was a chip of coal from the ancestor-fire itself; the very ember the first keepers had carried seven hundred years before.

"You will learn three characters from me," the old man said. "火. 灶. 守."

Xiao Huo nodded.

The old man took the charcoal and drew one stroke with it on the ground before the stove — 火, the fire character: six strokes, an anchor of a stroke in the center, a forked head, a single sweep of the ground line.

"This character," he said, "has been alive seven hundred years."

Xiao Huo stared at it.

He pointed at the stove with one finger. "That one?"

The old man shook his head.

"First learn fire," he said. "The character for stove has one more stroke. The character for keeper has yet another."

He drew 灶 below 火, and 守 below 灶 — three characters, one above the other.

"火 — 灶 — 守," he repeated, slowly, once.

Xiao Huo watched him for a long while.

Then he took the charcoal in his small fingers and started copying the strokes on the ground. He made one attempt, rubbed it out, made another, rubbed it out again. Thirteen times he tried. Only on the thirteenth did his characters begin to settle.

No one in the house made any sound.

His mother appeared at the kitchen doorway, looked at the three characters in the dust, and said:

"That one looks like fire."

A-Shi looked at them and said:

"That one looks like Xiao Huo."

The old man looked at the three characters a long time, then said nothing at all.

He asked Xiao Huo to do one more thing.

He asked him to carve the characters into the kitchen wall — the wall above the stove, at eye height for the fire, two feet from the stove iron. He sat as close as he could and checked every stroke Xiao Huo carved, one by one, until all three characters were standing steady.

火. 灶. 守.

The last stroke finished, the flame leapt once.

Everyone knew it.

Seven hundred years, and no one had ever taught anyone how to write the characters for the fire they were keeping. Tonight, someone had.

"Bank the stove tonight," the old man said.

"I only know how to cover it in ash," Xiao Huo said.

"That is enough," the old man said. "If you know how to bank the stove in ash, then you are a keeper."

The stove.

Seven hundred years.

No one had ever been taught. No one. Not one keeper. Not in seven hundred years.

Today.

There has been.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and eleven · Volume XV, twelfth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Characters.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/112-the-night-of-the-vigil.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Night of the Vigil

---

## One Hundred and Twelve: The Night of the Vigil

On the day of the winter solstice, the courtyard had no daylight at all.

By nine in the morning it was light, but by three in the afternoon the sky was already pitch black. Every branch of the fire-seed tree was silhouette against frost, and the one warm thing in the yard was the tofu-cube glow of the stove at the center of the kitchen.

His mother made a small supper — nothing abundant — this house had learned to live on cabbage and tofu since autumn's first cool mornings.

But tonight was different.

Tonight the stove was not to go out.

His mother called Xiao Huo to the stove.

"For seven hundred years," she said, "whoever was in the family would take turns watching the fire. But tonight — no one takes turns."

"And A-Shi?"

"A-Shi is asleep," she said. "Tonight A-Shi lets you take the watch."

Xiao Huo sat at the stove. The fire leaned toward him, the way it always leaned toward warm hands.

He reached out to touch the three characters carved against the stove iron — 火 · 灶 · 守 — each stroke worn a little smoother by the heat of six and a half centuries.

The stove was steady.

"Bank it lightly," she said. "And keep the ash level."

He knelt on the hearth stones and took the long iron spoon in his small hand. He raked the coals until the white ash lay flat and even, and the buried embers glowed even at their edges.

All night, wind pressed against the door. Every two or three hours it rattled the latch — once, twice, once.

Xiao Huo watched the fire. He fed it one sliver of birch at a time — slow, sparing, steady. He never let it grow too high. He never let it fall too low.

He never fell asleep.

At three in the morning, the wind stopped. Every sound in the courtyard went silent.

At four, the horizon began to turn white.

At five, the branches of the fire-seed tree came back into relief.

At six, the gate rattled — the same rattle they had come to know. His mother, dressed in white, came out and stood in the courtyard.

"The stove?" she said.

"Still burning," Xiao Huo said.

She went inside. She looked at the warm hearth, at the level ash, at Xiao Huo standing beside the stove in his small night-robe.

She said nothing for a long time.

Then she bowed — to Xiao Huo.

Not the way elders bow to children — the way a keeper bows to a keeper.

In the hearth, one piece of birch he did not remember feeding sat glowing at the edge — the one A-Shi had tucked in before lying down at midnight and told nobody to tell him about.

That was the night.

Seven hundred years of keepers watching this fire — and no one had ever watched it alone. Tonight was the first time.

Tonight the fire had an entrusted watcher.

At dawn, she kissed the top of his head.

"Happy solstice," she said.

Xiao Huo did not answer. He was too tired, and too proud.

He banked the fire again, the way he had been taught.

The stove was very quiet.

Seven hundred years, every night someone had said "tomorrow" — until tonight.

Tonight he said "tomorrow" himself.

And meant it.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and twelve · Volume XV, thirteenth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Night of the Vigil.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/113-the-awakening-hearth.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Awakening Hearth

---

## One Hundred and Thirteen: The Awakening Hearth

The next morning, fog rolled in from outside the courtyard.

It was thick the way porridge is thick — the doorway vanished into it. Every needle on the fire-seed tree was furred with frost, and the frost gleamed where the fog pressed against it, like an extra set of white candles.

His mother was already cooking — cabbage, tofu, the last of the rice.

The stove was burning steadily, the way it had burned all night.

A-Shi woke. He had not slept at all the night before.

Xiao Huo woke.

He sat up slowly. He looked at the stove. It was still burning — same level, same calm, same golden yellow it had been at three in the morning when the wind had stopped for an instant.

He said nothing.

A-Shi stood in the doorway with his back to the room. He said nothing.

The stove crackled twice — one soft pop, one lower roll deeper down in the coal bed.

"It knows me now," A-Shi said finally. His voice was lower than ordinary.

His mother did not turn around. She only nodded.

The fog kept coming.

The fire-seed tree swayed somewhere in it, grey needles whispering against grey air. Spring was still a fortnight away, but the wind already carried one extra degree of warmth.

Xiao Huo knelt at the stove. He looked at the fire a long time.

He reached out and placed one small hand flat against the iron edge. The iron was warm — not hot, exactly, but warm in the way iron is warm when the fire beneath it has burned steady all night.

"Last night was me," he said.

No one answered.

Out in the fog, a crow called once.

A-Shi knelt slowly beside Xiao Huo at the stove. He did not look at his brother, nor at his mother. He looked at the fire — the way a person looks at a road home.

Then he reached out both hands and rested them on the stove's edge.

"Seven hundred years," he said.

His hands were lit red-gold by the fire.

His mother came to stand behind them both. She reached out and folded a corner of her apron over the stove's edge — not to touch it, just to be close to it.

"From now on," she said, "the two of you will keep the fire. In turns."

She paused.

"Whoever keeps it knows."

The kitchen was silent for a long time.

The stove crackled once more — a single, deliberate pop, the way a stove answers when it is ready.

From that day forward, nobody in the house used the words "keeping the fire."

The stove had become the third member of the family.

Out in the yard, the reeds had browned.

Spring was still a fortnight away.

But already here, already close, already in every warm square of light this stove poured into the kitchen.

Spring was coming.

The stove was ready for it.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and thirteen · Volume XV, fourteenth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Awakening Hearth.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/114-the-first-day-of-spring.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The First Day of Spring

---

## One Hundred and Fourteen: The First Day of Spring

On the night before the first day of spring, a light snow fell on the courtyard.

It was not heavy — a few strong breaths of wind and it was nearly gone. The strip of frozen mud along the far edge of the yard was already turning dark.

At three in the morning, the courtyard gate rattled once.

Not the wind. Not the snow falling. A different sound.

A-Shi's eyes opened.

He sat up. He did not wake his mother. He went out to the door alone.

The door opened a crack. No snow came in — a figure stood in the doorway.

Not the old man from the mountain. Not the cooper. Not the letter-bearer.

A person whose face was hidden by a white cloth, fragrant with something he could not name.

The stranger drew from within his robe a small oil-paper parcel and handed it into the room. He said nothing. He turned and was gone.

A-Shi picked up the parcel.

Inside was — a single small piece of white birch. A fresh-looking one, still rimmed with frost, with a little snow clinging to the white.

His mother came to the doorway. One look was enough.

On the oil-paper was written four characters only:

"灶火春安。May the stove fire be at peace in spring."

She set the piece of white birch into the fire.

The flames shook once.

A-Shi placed his hand near the fire's edge. The warmth was intense — a spring kind of warmth.

"Stove," Xiao Huo said, rubbing his eyes.

"Stove," A-Shi confirmed.

The room went quiet.

"The stove ate a piece of white birch last night," his mother said.

Everyone in the house knew about the log of white birch Bai Yin had stored away six hundred years ago. It had been split into three pieces — the smallest had already gone into the stove in midwinter; the two larger pieces still sat in the yard.

Someone — someone no one had seen — had left this one on the doorstep.

The ones who came to leave white birch — they were folded into the stove's own kin. The fire knew all their names.

The morning of the first day of spring came up white and clean.

On the fire-seed tree, one tiny leaf pushed out — just one — and the stove lit it up like gold under the rising sun.

This house. Seven hundred years.

The first day of spring.

The stove is still burning.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and fourteen · Volume XV, fifteenth part · The seven-hundredth year — The First Day of Spring.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/115-the-hearth-named.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Hearth Named

---

## One Hundred and Fifteen: The Hearth Named

Ten days after the first day of spring, the yard grew ever warmer.

Half the needles on the fire-seed tree had turned a soft ivory yellow; every wind made them sway and hum. The ditch by the gate had turned to flowing mud. Moss had begun to creep out between the stones of the path.

His mother was cooking.

Xiao Huo crouched by the stove, watching the flame.

The old man had come again.

He had not brought any of the things he usually brought — no basket, no letter, no bundle of birch twigs. He had walked in wearing only a clean blue cotton robe, the color of a spring sky just deepening toward noon.

He said nothing as he came in. He only bowed his head once — a formal, old-fashioned bow that neither A-Shi nor Xiao Huo had ever seen him make before.

His mother smiled. "Come in."

He took a stool and sat beside the stove, facing it.

She brought out the meal she brought whenever a guest came — rice, cabbage tofu soup, preserved radish. Nothing unusual. Nothing added.

They filled the bowls high. There was a lot of rice. Only a little food.

The old man sniffed the air.

"Fragrant," he said.

A-Shi sat close to the stove, watching the fire. The fire swayed.

"Stove," he said.

The flame leapt once — then softened — then settled back into its long, low burning.

"It is spring," A-Shi's mother said. "Spring must have a name."

The kitchen went quiet.

"What name?" Xiao Huo asked.

His mother glanced at A-Shi.

A-Shi pointed at the stove. "Stove."

"Stove is not enough," the old man said.

From his robe he drew out a small clean piece of paper — no larger than his palm. He pressed one finger against it, leaving a faint soot print behind.

"炉," he said. "炉火."

"炉火," A-Shi's mother repeated slowly.

炉火.

After that day, no one in the house said "the stove" anymore.

They said: 炉火.

The mother said it first, the next morning, while stirring the porridge: "The stove-fire is coming warm."

Xiao Huo said: "The fire-fire is warmer today than yesterday."

A-Shi said: "炉火 breathes on its own at night."

Everyone left it at that.

The soup boiled a few minutes longer than usual.

The family gathered at the long table.

They ate.

In the iron box beneath the stove, there was a thin slip of paper that Bai Yin had written six hundred years ago. No one looked at it. They all knew what it was.

It was the name of the fire.

炉火.

In the first month of spring.

In this house.

Finally, it had a name.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and fifteen · Volume XV, sixteenth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Hearth Named.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/116-the-next-visit.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Next Visit

---

## One Hundred and Sixteen: The Next Visit

On the twelfth day after the spring equinox, the first guest of the season came to the house.

Not the old man from the mountain.

It was the neighbor, old Li.

There were only three households next to them. Old Li's family kept six mouths to feed. He stood at the door with a slightly embarrassed look.

"The fire," he said, "the fire — would it come to our house tomorrow?"

A-Shi stared at him for a long moment.

His mother came out of the kitchen.

Old Li wrung his hands. "Our stove at home — it doesn't work as well as yours. I heard you say it before — your fire, it's alive. Our fire, it's not alive."

He said "alive" with a lot of confidence. A-Shi did not contradict him.

"If it's all right with you," A-Shi said, "you can come see it tomorrow."

"See it?" Old Li took one step sideways.

"The stove," A-Shi said, "doesn't really leave. But you can come over and eat here if you want."

Old Li shifted his stance again. "I'll come," he said finally.

The next day, the weather turned. A real rain — thunder rolling from far off, the street below the house streaming red-brown with water.

At breakfast time, the gate rattled once.

Old Li stood under the eave, one sleeve already wet.

The whole family stood.

His mother ushered him inside.

The stove was burning steadily.

Old Li stood before the fire. He stood very properly, the way someone behaves when he's worried he'll touch something fragile.

His mother asked A-Shi to serve the rice.

Old Li could not sit down. He kept looking at the fire, then down at his own feet.

"I can't do this," he said finally — very soft, almost to himself.

No one laughed.

A-Shi brought two bowls of noodles and set them on the low table.

"Stove," Old Li said, barely audible.

"Mm," A-Shi said.

"I'll come back," Old Li said. "I'll come back tomorrow."

He said nothing more. He left.

The gate swung shut behind him.

The fire-seed tree shook.

"He was scared," Xiao Huo said after the gate closed.

"He wasn't," his mother said.

"He was really scared."

His mother did not reply.

She washed Old Li's bowl and turned it over on the shelf.

The stove burned steady as ever.

"Next time," A-Shi's mother said, "let him come."

The room was quiet.

Outside the gate, the fire-seed tree stirred.

Seven hundred years, and for the first time the neighbor's hearth and this hearth had eaten at the same table.

The fire had never seen this many people sit with it at once.

It had not gone out.

It had not gone out.

Spring had come for keeps.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and sixteen · Volume XV, seventeenth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Next Visit.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/117-the-neighbors-stove.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Neighbor's Stove

---

## One Hundred and Seventeen: The Neighbor's Stove

The rain at the neighbor's house had not let up.

It rained for fourteen days. The ditch along our gate carved itself deeper. The great flat stone at the corner of the yard wobbled once, twice, and slid halfway into the flow.

Old Li came every day.

He came earlier each time.

Mother did not say anything. She simply told A-Shi to let him stand a while and watch.

The first day he stood for nearly half an hour. The stove burned steadily. He watched the tips of the flames.

The second day he stood the whole morning. Mother made four dishes. Old Li did not touch his chopsticks. He only watched the stove.

The third day he stood closer — one foot from the stove iron — for nearly three full hours.

On the fourth day it rained, and he came early, walking over the path with his shoes soaked.

"I came for the fire," he said.

A-Shi looked at him.

"On my own. I won't ask my six to do it. Just me."

No one said anything.

Old Li stood a little longer. Then he said, "Stove. Tomorrow I come to cook."

His mother smiled.

"Then come," she said.

Old Li nodded.

On the tenth day the sky cleared. The ditch outside the gate ran red-brown with silt.

Old Li came carrying a small iron pot.

He stood at the door a long time. Then he walked in and stood before the stove.

He set the pot on the stove.

No one said a word.

He crouched down, filled the pot with a little water. Then from his pocket he drew a handful of rice — only a few grains — and poured them into the water.

The stove.

It was burning.

The pot stirred — barely a sound.

Old Li stood a little longer. He went out to the porch and stopped there.

"Stove," he said. "I'll come again tomorrow."

No one answered.

A wind swept through the yard. It pushed the gate open.

The sky cleared.

Everyone in the family stood on the porch and watched.

The stove had not gone out.

For the first time in seven hundred years, these people and this stove were waiting together for the neighbor to come.

The stove.

It was burning.

It was calling.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and seventeen · Volume XV, eighteenth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Neighbor's Stove.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/118-the-spoonful-of-soup.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Spoonful of Soup

---

## One Hundred and Eighteen: The Spoonful of Soup

The rain ended for good seven days ago.

Old Li came every day for those seven days. On the sixth day he laid a small cloth bundle on the stove. His mother opened it and found a white patch of fabric — a faint flame drawn on it, stitched very finely along its edges.

He said nothing, and left.

On the seventh day he came again. The moment he stepped inside he went straight to the stove, picked up an old wooden spoon, and ladled one spoonful of hot broth from the pot and drank it.

His mother watched his hands on the spoon.

"I've come to learn how to feed the fire," he said.

His mother stood still.

"I don't want a title," he said.

A-Shi came out of the kitchen with a bowl of rice.

"My six family members haven't eaten," he said.

His mother smiled.

"Anyone who comes in from the rain," she said, "doesn't need to ask to be fed."

Old Li stared at the stove.

"Seven hundred years," he said slowly. "I've been looking at the wrong fire this whole time. These days I'm finally beginning to see it properly."

"It breathes," he said.

A-Shi stepped back.

In the yard the fire-seed tree shook gently. The third flush of new leaves was opening.

Seven hundred years of keepers in this house — the stove, the kitchen, this room. No one invited anyone else inside the gate. It was a quiet rule the household kept without speaking aloud.

A-Shi watched the fire. His mother tended the stove. This house had always been a Hearthkeeper's house.

Today, for the first and maybe the last time in seven hundred years, another hearth-hand would join them at the fire.

Old Li did not take the spoon back. He let it rest in the pot.

Fire.

He placed the spoon back in the pot and his hands stayed in the glare of the flame a moment longer than necessary.

"Tomorrow," A-Shi's mother said. "Tomorrow you'll come."

Old Li did not say anything. He only nodded.

He left, walking back down the lane in the rain-fresh air.

A-Shi and his mother and Xiao Huo stood at the porch for a long time watching him go.

The fire-seed tree swayed.

The stove burned very steadily.

They had six bowls tonight.

They had always cooked six bowls.

That night in this house, the fire was very bright.

All the trees in the yard, all the stones, all the ash in the stove, all the water on the path — they were all a little warmer than any previous night this year.

The stove had not gone out.

The whole yard had not gone out.

Spring had come.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and eighteen · Volume XV, nineteenth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Spoonful of Soup.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/119-snowan.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — Snow'an

---

## One Hundred and Nineteen: Snow'an

On the fifteenth day after the spring equinox, Old Li came again — this time with a child.

Not his own.

It was Tiě mù sùmù, a boy who had been taken in by neighbors across the ridge. The child — eleven, or maybe twelve, with a gaze that went straight through anything he looked at — stood in the courtyard and did not move. He stood before the fire-seed tree for a long time. The tree's new leaves were unfurling in layers.

"I'm Snow'an," he said.

"Sit down," Mother said.

He did not sit. He kept looking at the stove.

"Your stove —" he said.

"炉火," Old Li said.

"炉火," Snow'an repeated.

He stared at the fire for a long time. Its light warmed his face to a soft gold.

Mother went to bring the rice out. A-Shi moved the low stool around the table so Snow'an could sit and eat with the family. Snow'an finally sat down. He did not speak.

"This house —" he started, very softly.

"This house is seven hundred years old," Mother said.

Snow'an considered it. "One thousand four hundred?"

"Seven hundred," A-Shi said.

"Two of you?" Snow'an asked.

"Seven," Mother said. Everyone laughed.

The fire-seed tree swayed.

"The tree is also seven hundred years old?" Snow'an asked.

"The tree is itself," Mother said.

"And the stove — seven hundred years old?" Snow'an asked.

No one answered.

This stove had ground flour for starving children in famine years. It had tended the dying during winters no one counted. It had received white birch roots from elders no longer living. This house never spoke of these things aloud.

炉火 burned.

Snow'an quietly reached his small hand toward the stove. The warmth came through the iron edge slowly. He pulled his hand back. The warmth stayed in his palm.

He stood up.

"I should go," he said.

"Come again tomorrow," Mother said.

He nodded.

"He wants to watch the fire," Old Li said.

He said that — and that was the only thing that got spoken for a long time in this house.

The fire-seed tree swayed.

Snow'an left without looking back.

The fire burned.

The yard was very old. Every grain of soil in it had been warmed by 炉火 for seven hundred years.

炉火.

Seven hundred years.

It was still here.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and nineteen · Volume XV, twentieth part · The seven-hundredth year — Snow'an.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/12-interlude-before-the-flame.md
# Interlude Five · Before the Flame

**Written after *Interlude Four · The Reply of the One Who Came After*: this volume is not the fifth letter — it is the paperwork from before any of the letters were inked. Fire wrote the first, the Mist wrote the second, Lin wrote the third, the One Who Came After wrote the fourth.**

**This is their shared rough draft — the hundred unattributed pages at the very bottom of the canon, the ones with no author and only footnotes.**

---

## I · The Nine Unlit Lamps

Nine unlit lamps, drifting.

Back then there was no *place* — only *before*. The Graywood was a lamp no sun ever reached; the Miller Wasteland was a lamp the sand had filled in on its own; the Shattered Timelines were a lamp whose rain had not yet agreed on a color. Every one of them was drifting in the same way — each had opened its mouth, and none of what it said had ever landed.

There was no mist yet. The mist would not learn its first syllable until the fire fell to the ground.

None of the nine had ever gone out.

Extinguishing requires a fire to exist — and fire was still asking itself whether it wanted to.

---

## II · Why Nobody Lit the Fire

Later someone wrote a fat book on this. The most important sentence in it was — **“Every realm assumed someone else was doing it.”**

The Miller Wasteland said the Hub had one; the Hub said the House of Hours had one; the House of Hours said the Deep Space jump had one; the Deep Space said the Graywood was waiting for someone else to move. The Graywood did not answer.

For untold ages no lamp opened its mouth — nothing spoken in that place carried; the unsaid sank into the ground, and stayed buried — until the fire fell.

Then someone stopped waiting.

He had been, in some way, an ancestor of Lin — not father, not father's father — he was **every** Lin's inner voice: the small voice that counts "how many steps left" every time Lin thinks about looking back into the fire.

He simply didn't wait.

---

## III · The Hundred and Eight Times He Nearly Left the Ember Behind

He was carrying a lump of coal on his back.

It was the one thing the fallen Dominion had owned that nobody had thought to confiscate. The Dominion did not fall to force — it was simply stood witness to, end to end, until it stopped.

Later, people turned that number into a tidy sum. It was no mere arithmetic — it was added together from two counts.

A hundred and eight — the travelers who had carried the same ember, set it down, and never picked it up again.

One — the one who never set it down.

He was that one.

He took the lump out for a look — and closed his hand around it harder.

**Fire does not need to be lit.**
**Fire only needs one person who refuses to let go.**

---

## IV · What the Fire Said When It Hit the Ground

The World Anchor fell here — at this very spot in the Crossway Hub.

Mark the word *fell* — not placed, *fell*. There was no expectation when the lump touched down: it was nearly spent, the sack was gone, thirty seconds of scavenged warmth remained, it was near midnight, the keeper-of-the-lamps' ancestor was not keeping any lamps, Lin's ancestor was not yet Lin, the mist did not exist, and fire did not yet admit it was fire.

It spoke one sentence as it fell.

It was not spoken by anyone alive, and it was not said by fire either — fire learned the line from "how many steps left":

**“One Realm to bind them all.”**

Nothing happened for ten seconds after. Then one more poster appeared on the wall. Then somewhere by a campfire, someone surfaced for a moment and said something unintelligible:

**“The flame never dies while the anchor holds.”**

— He had no idea he had just laid down a rule. He had simply woken up, once.

---

## V · The Former Life of the Mist, the Former Life of the Keeper

The mist is something that was born only after the fire fell.

It is the world's unsaid sentences. In the years before the fall, none of the nine lamps lacked for a voice — they lacked an echo. Whatever a lamp said, nothing would carry — and the unsaid, unheard, sunk down into the ground, with nowhere to go. After the fire landed, those buried words woke — and with no place left for them, they rose as one mist, drifting back over the nine lamps.

The mist *is* the shape of the act of opening your mouth, trapped. Every breath in is a question asked backward into the night; every breath out is an answer nobody held.

Which is why the mist **cannot be dispersed** — dispersion would only mean someone finally spoke. The mist wouldn't be gone; it would be speaking with a different accent.

The keeper was the first person to stop moving.

Not clever enough to investigate, nor foolish enough to charge in, nor far-sighted enough to plan — he just **sat down first**. Knees bent, shoulders against the direction of the beacon — he didn't sit down because he thought it was remarkable; he sat down because there was a little warmth and sitting was more reasonable than standing.

Every keeper since has simply been doing this: not clever, not foolish, not planning, sitting down, and watching the fire.

---

## VI · One Line, For the Next Reader

If you are cold while reading this, do not run toward the fire — the fire runs toward no one.

Put this at the very bottom of the canon:

**“By the time you reach this line, the hundred and nine echoes have possibly rung a few times.

No one keeps the tally, and none is needed.

I know you, and I do not, because I learned the syntax of fire: unsigned — therefore in need of no signature.

And you — you made it to this page.

Your turning it over decides exactly as much about the fire surviving as anything else ever has.

**The fire has always been waiting**.”**

---

**This volume counts toward no seven chapters, no achievements, no codex entries. It only means one thing: one more poster on the wall. If the cloth is old enough, it is the thirteenth.**

*（Interlude Five · Before the Flame — the canon's draft. Fire answered the only question it was asked: why me? Because it asked itself whether it wanted to. There is room for a fourteenth poster on the wall — it is being held for you.）*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — Saturdays

## One Hundred and Twenty: Saturdays

Saturday morning began under a light rain — not the kind that comes in sheets, but the kind that hangs in the air and clings gently to the eaves and the needles of the fire-seed tree. The yard was cooler than it had been for days. But the stove burned steadily.

His mother was cooking — cabbage and tofu. The pan hissed and sighed.

A-Shi sat in front of the stove, watching the fire.

Xiao Huo sat beside him doing the same.

His mother walked to the stove, walked back to the kitchen.

The gate rattled softly.

A-Shi stood.

A small figure stood in the doorway — young shoulders, small bag on his back, a grey jacket, brand-new cloth shoes.

It was Snow'an.

He did not speak.

He stood in the yard looking at the fire-seed tree. Each layer of leaves was larger now — summer was coming. The green was beginning to shift from cool to warm gold.

He walked into the kitchen.

He sat in front of the stove.

The firelight lit his face softly, from within.

He watched the fire for a long time. Then he slowly got up and reached out one small hand toward the stove iron. He pressed his hand against the iron edge.

The iron was warm.

His hand did not get burned.

The stove burned steady.

He left.

He returned on Sunday the following week.

Then the week after that.

The fourth week he did not come.

The fifth week, he came carrying a small oil jar. He set it on the low table in the yard. Some of the villagers had started to suspect something — that this boy came every single Saturday, sat silently by the fire, and left without speaking.

A-Shi told his mother, "He keeps coming."

"He keeps coming," Mother said.

The fire-seed tree swayed.

"He won't keep coming forever," Xiao Huo said.

"He will," A-Shi said.

The tree shook in the breeze.

A-Shi and Xiao Huo and Mother all understood — every Saturday, the boy would come — sit — watch the fire — and leave — without saying a word.

Sometimes Mother would slip him a bowl of rice.

The stove burned.

Seven hundred years had passed.

The stove still burned.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and twenty · Volume XV, twenty-first part · The seven-hundredth year — Saturdays.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/121-the-fire-comes.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Fire Comes

---

## One Hundred and Twenty-one: The Fire Comes

Week six after the spring equinox — a Saturday before daylight.

Mother had already risen. The dumpling filling was mixed — cabbage and pork.

Xiao Huo sat in front of the stove, silent, watching the fire.

Snow'an should have arrived by now.

He came — with company.

His mother trailed behind him.

A-Shi stood up from the straw mat and stepped closer.

"Mother-of-Snow'an," he said.

Snow'an's mother stopped in the doorway. She looked at the fire.

The stove burned steady — through the doorway they could see it faintly glowing.

"I'll sit here," she said.

She sat down beside the stove.

She didn't speak.

The entire household came inside.

Mother brought the dumpling filling over.

A-Shi said, "Making dumplings."

Mother and A-Shi sat down to make dumplings.

Snow'an's mother stayed in front of the stove, watching the fire.

She did not speak.

The stove burned.

The fire burned.

Sometimes a family does not speak — they simply sit together — the fire leaps up from within the stove — every person is silent — silence is the deepest language.

Snow'an's mother sat like that for almost two hours before she spoke.

"From now on," she said.

She stopped.

"From now on, I will come every Saturday," she said. "I'll come to watch the fire."

No one stopped their hands.

The dumplings were finished.

Mother served one bowl to each person.

The whole family sat down in the kitchen and ate dumplings.

Snow'an's mother held the bowl.

She ate.

This house was seven hundred years old — no outsider had ever sat in this kitchen before.

Everyone in the family knew.

Half a pot of dumplings remained.

The fire-seed tree swayed.

Within days — the whole village learned — Bai Yin's stove — the one across the ridge — now had a guest.

And from that day forward — every Saturday — Snow'an's mother came.

And watched the fire.

This house — seven hundred years old — had finally gained one.

炉火.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and twenty-one · Volume XV, twenty-second part · The seven-hundredth year — The Fire Comes.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/122-always-here.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — Always Here

## One Hundred and Twenty-two: Always Here

Week seven after the spring equinox — Saturday — overcast, heavy with that low moisture that doesn't quite rain and never does. The stove burned steadily.

Mother was frying cabbage and pork dumplings in the pan — the steady sizzle of fat against hot iron, the kind of sound the house had known for generations.

She set the dumpling filling aside and started doing other preparations with more than her usual quietness. A-Shi crouched by the stove, watching the fire. He didn't speak either.

Xiao Huo scratched his eyes, then saw the doorway.

There stood Snow'an's mother at the gate.

Behind her were three children — two girls in blue jackets, one boy in grey. They stood there without speaking. All the light in the doorway came from the stove behind them.

Mother moved from kitchen to door and saw them.

Snow'an's mother smiled. She set a small cloth basket on the stove — the contents covered by a cotton cloth. She said nothing. She only looked once at A-Shi.

A-Shi didn't speak either.

The three children's faces glowed softly from within as they watched the stove.

"Come in and sit," Mother said.

The children stepped in. They watched the fire.

The cabbage stewed in the pan smelled rich and salty in the warm room.

Mother brought three stools and lined them up beside the stove. The children sat side by side. They watched the fire — they watched — they watched — they watched.

Outside, the fire-seed tree was making that particular sound leaves do when they are wet and wind-carries.

Mother approached the stove a third time. This time she crouched. She didn't speak.

Snow'an's mother unwrapped the cloth bundle. Inside were three pairs of cotton shoes — the needlework identical — the stitches identical — brand new.

Mother didn't speak. She picked up the smallest pair, then the largest one — her hand journeyed up the sizes.

Snow'an's mother said only:

"These are from the house my mother came from."

No one asked which house.

Years ago — that old pot — when it was made — the fire in the stove had been going in her hands too.

Everyone in the kitchen didn't ask.

Outside, that small stream came sliding through the ditch.

The smell of coal smoke drifted from the stove.

炉火.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and twenty-two · Volume XV, twenty-third part · The seven-hundredth year — Always Here.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/123-snowan-turns.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — Snow'an Turns Back
