## One Hundred and Twenty-three: Snow'an Turns Back

Saturday of the eighth week after the spring equinox.

Clear all day. The white light of late afternoon touched the courtyard warmly.

Mother-of-Snow'an came to the stove again. She did not speak. She stood before the stove for a long time. In her hand she held a basket. In the basket were two cotton shoes.

She set the shoes on the low bench in front of the stove. No one spoke.

Snow'an stood with her.

Snow'an was smiling slightly.

"You're back," Mother said.

"No," Snow'an said. Very quietly. He looked at the fire differently — not with fear — with calm.

The fire burned still.

The gate opened and shut. The rain came down hard.

Mother-of-Snow'an set all three pairs of shoes on the stove iron.

Mother brought stools.

Four people — Mother, A-Shi, Xiao Huo, Snow'an's mother — sat in front of the stove.

Nobody spoke.

The fire-seed tree was shaking with sand and wind-whipped leaves.

The silence lasted one hour. The stove burned steady. Twice the flame leapt. Each person put one hand forward, warming palms in the light of the fire. The stove burned. Seven hundred years of fire. 炉火 burned still.

Snow'an's mother watched the stove. Her eyes were a little red. She didn't cry. The fire sent its light into her eyes and her eyes became warm.

She spoke at last.

"I came to tell you —"

The flame leapt once.

"I came to tell the fire —"

The world fell silent around her.

"Your house has no outsider; your fire has no outsider; your fire has been kept by a tree; your fire has never died for seven hundred years — your kitchen —"

She paused.

"The whole town has said so — the stove of Bai Yin's house — never goes out."

No one answered.

炉火.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and twenty-three · Volume XV, twenty-fourth part · The seven-hundredth year — Snow'an Turns Back.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/124-the-whole-town-and-the-fire.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Whole Town and the Fire

---

## One Hundred and Twenty-four: The Whole Town and the Fire

Week nine after the spring equinox — Saturday. Sunlight filled the yard.

The yard was full of people.

The first was Zhou, the tofu seller from the end of the village. He stood at the door for a long time.

The second was Lao Guo from next door.

The third was someone nobody recognized.

Then five more — then fifteen — then twenty-five more streamed in until the yard could hardly hold them.

Mother was rushing around at the stove overwhelmed by everyone arriving. Her hands were flying everywhere. Nobody said a word about it. She kept adding water to the pot — slowly, carefully — the pot filled up. The fire burned steadily. The pot was beginning to boil.

A-Shi stood in the corner, watching the stove.

"Don't be afraid," he said.

The whole town fell silent.

Seven hundred years ago, this same town had gathered around this stove the day the first keeper passed away. They had come to see the fire that would outlive every one of them.

They came one by one, filing in.

They did not speak. They looked at the stove. Their faces in the orange light were impossible to read. Every one of them was looking at the fire.

Not one of them left.

The pot boiled over — Mother carried the steaming pot to the long bench — a line formed — a line of the whole town — everyone waited patiently for a bowl.

Even Xiao Huo got one.

Mother stood at the far end of the bench looking at the stove.

The whole town — looking at the stove — nobody spoke — nobody left.

The stove had never gone out.

炉火.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and twenty-four · Volume XV, twenty-fifth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Whole Town and the Fire.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/125-the-thunder-rain.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Thunder Rain

---

## One Hundred and Twenty-five: The Thunder Rain

The tenth Saturday after the spring equinox — afternoon came with a genuinely grey sky.

The whole town came. No one knew who had told them that today the stove would be burning loud. Everyone knew.

First Uncle Zhou arrived. Then old Aunt Guo. Then the man who had asked A-Shi to repair his stove yesterday — no one recognized him either. Then more than thirty people arrived. The courtyard was so packed that the door barely opened. The stove burned steadily. Nobody spoke.

His mother looked at it for a long time.

"Too many people," she said.

"Don't be afraid," A-Shi said.

Everyone took bowls for the meal. The pot was full of broth.

Rain came in earnest at three-thirty in the afternoon. The roof filled with the sound of water hitting tiles across the whole village. Inside the stove the coals popped softly under the damp.

The whole town sat watching the stove.

The ditch outside ran swift with brown water. The courtyard gathered rain before it could dry. Everyone in the yard sat quietly.

No one spoke.

When there were no visitors, the stove burned steadily.

When it rained, it also burned steadily.

Everyone waited that whole afternoon.

Then the rain stopped, and the sun came out.

The whole town shared one bowl of broth per person before anyone said a word.

The stove burned.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and twenty-five · Volume XV, twenty-sixth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Thunder Rain.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/126-the-shadow-at-the-stoves-mouth.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Shadow at the Stove's Mouth

---

## One Hundred and Twenty-six: The Shadow at the Stove's Mouth

Saturday of the eleventh week after the spring equinox — the sun had barely risen — the yard was still cool and clear.

A-Shi was sweeping with a bamboo broom while Xiao Huo watched him from the porch.

"Eggs today," Mother called from the kitchen.

A-Shi set the broom aside and gave a small nod. The sky was very wide, surprisingly blue. The fire-seed tree's needles had grown another layer — their serrated edges shimmered in the morning light.

He turned back to the kitchen and saw Xiao Huo standing at the stove with his hand flat against the iron edge. He had not moved.

"Did you hear it?" Xiao Huo asked.

A-Shi stopped for a moment. "Hear what?"

"The stove," Xiao Huo said. "Listening to it."

A-Shi crouched down and listened.

The stove was making a sound — a low, steady sound — not loud exactly, but unmistakably there. A-Shi put both hands on the iron edge.

The iron was warm.

"灶火," he said. "炉火."

Xiao Huo nodded slowly.

He reached out and pressed his small hand against the iron.

"Seven hundred years," he said quietly.

Nobody answered.

The stove burned.

The stove burned.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and twenty-six · Volume XV, twenty-seventh part · The seven-hundredth year — The Shadow at the Stove's Mouth.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/127-the-waiting.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — The Waiting

---

## One Hundred and Twenty-seven: The Waiting

Saturday of the twelfth week after the spring equinox.

The fog lay over the yard at dawn — not too thick — the sun was climbing.

Uncle Zhou arrived early.He did not speak.

The stove burned as it always had, watching.

Uncle Zhou stood in the yard for a long time — he wanted to leave — he didn't. He stood before the stove for close to an hour.

"You are here," he said to the fire.

The fire didn't answer.

A-Shi brought out a fresh bowl of warm rice gruel and offered it to Uncle Zhou. He took it, drank it in one gulp, and held the bowl.

"You've been carrying something," A-Shi said.

"No," Uncle Zhou said.

He stood there after finishing the bowl for some time.

Then he left.

The stove didn't go out.

The stove burned.

The stove burned.

---

*(End of volume one hundred and twenty-seven · Volume XV, twenty-eighth part · The seven-hundredth year — The Waiting.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/13-interlude-handover.md
# Interlude Six · Handover

**Written after *Interlude Five · Before the Flame*: this volume is neither a prologue nor an epilogue.**
**It is the handoff that happens in a single night, in a single shift, between two keepers of the same flame.**

---

## I · The First Night on the Shift

The new one arrived on a Thursday.

He sank into the chair to the east of the Hub, and the old keeper was wiping the inside of the furnace door. No nod, no handshake, no “welcome to —.” The old keeper said the only thing he would say that night:

“We never replace the wick in this lamp.”

Then back to wiping. The new one let about twenty breaths pass before he understood: not a welcome, not a rule.
**It is what you say to open a night.**

---

## II · The Furnace Has Never Had Its Wick Replaced

The wick in the furnace was put in by the first generation. From that night to this one — every hundred and nine echoes across thirty-six days — the same wick.

No one has replaced it. No one would dare to. Not because of a taboo — but because the moment anyone did, the furnace would become something other than a furnace. Not fire — something else that it doesn't know how to be.

The old keeper replaced three things: the old blanket by the furnace, the small oil lamp by it, the chair by the wall that had been sat to death. All of them were heirlooms going on five hundred years. He never suggested anything about those; he just actually replaced them, each in his own time.

---

## III · The Ledger of This Night

The old keeper kept a ledger.

Each page carried three things — time, lamp, page. Open to the first page and you find a single line from a very long time ago: *“The lamping still happens.”* Open to the last line, and it says only one sentence:

**“Tonight, at this hour, the new one takes the desk.”**

The new one sat with that line for a long while. Then he understood one thing:

He was never an apprentice.
**He was the next.**

---

## IV · The Way the Handover Works

The old keeper stood up, slid the wax pencil back into its cup, shook the blanket flat, set it down, and walked to the furnace. Squatted. Reached his hand into the mouth — in and out, three or four times, never touching anything, saying nothing. It looked like inspection — and it looked like a handoff — and it looked, in the thinnest way, like a goodbye.

Then he stood.

Then he left.

No “I'll be patrolling the outer ring.” He didn't look at the flame.

The flame kept burning.

---

## V · Before Dawn

The new one learned three things:
The wick doesn't need changing. The hearth needs wiping now and then — it's only routine maintenance, and it's what every hearthman has always done. And when it is his turn to hand the desk over himself, he must not say he's leaving, and must not say he's staying — he just says the one sentence that will be his, and cuts his own corner through the side door.

It was the new one who stopped at the doorway, looking back at the desk:

The pencil cup held one more pencil.

An old blanket — belonging to no one, anyone, or the furnace itself — was folded across the back of the chair.

The notice on the wall still read: **We take no letters here — only travellers, come sit a while.**

Not long before sunrise, the new one turned to the first blank line in the ledger, sat down in the chair, and began his own first entry. His first entry contained only one thing:

**“The flame never went out. Handover.”**

---

## VI · This Page Is For You

If you're reading this —
**It isn't for curious readers. It means you're already sitting in the chair.**

The bosses changed, the dungeons changed, the Anchor changed, the campfires changed. Every single handover, the wick never changed.

Shift by shift the fire passes down. Each who picked up the flame thought he was only passing through; each who left knew that *passing through is how the flame survives its handovers.*

The flame belongs to no one.

The flame only belongs to the next shift.

— And the next shift is you.

---

*（Interlude Six · Handover — one shift, handed to the next. The writer of this volume has long since become used to seeing his own shift come to an end. The flame is still burning. The flame still has the same wick. The flame will never tell you how many shifts it has kept — it will only light the whole night for whoever sits in your chair next.）*

# source: creative/novel/en/14-interlude-come-sit-awhile.md
# Interlude Seven · Come Sit Awhile

**Written after *Interlude Six · Handover*: this volume is neither prologue nor epilogue.**
**It is the origin of the paper on the wall.**

---

## I · Nobody Knows Who Pinned the Paper

The paper on the wall of the waystation carries no name, no date, and no seal.

Flip through every old ledger — the earliest page reads *“The lamping still happens.”* Page by page the hand changes, ink by ink the line changes, and yet the sentence never does. It says only this:

**“We take no letters here — only travellers, come sit a while.”**

The new one once asked who had written it. The old keeper said that whoever did was long gone. And then he added: the sentence is still here.

The bottom-left corner of the paper has been worn to a soft radius — not by the shape of paper, but by the shape of hands.

---

## II · Under the Paper

The paper says *no letters.* The space under the paper has never been clean.

Someone tucked in a sock folded small. Someone tucked in a crumpled ferry ticket. Someone tucked in a drawing of boots — boots worn through, laces long since given up. Someone tucked in half a math problem, the pen strokes pressed hard, the answer unwritten.

The old keeper never read them.

And he never cleared them out.

The wind scattered them, the rain softened them, the sun dried them back to crisp. The travellers who tuck the scraps into the wall-gap know exactly what they are tucking in. The waystation does not collect drawings; it collects paper. The flame does not read them, and the ash will not carry them away.

The paper knows where it goes. The paper always knows.

---

## III · The One Who Left “Next Time” Behind

One year, late in the cold season, a woman arrived with dust on her boots.

The dust was so old the road below her had long since stopped asking. She sat, she finished half a warm meal, chewed the last bite slowly, stood, and tucked a scrap of paper into the wall-gap —

**Next time.**

Not *next time I'll come.* Not *thank you.* Just *next time.*

The old keeper looked at the scrap for a long while. Then he began wiping the furnace door.

That night the furnace burned a little warmer than usual.

---

## IV · Why Hearthmen Never Write Their Names

The waystation ledger has never held a name. Only the time, the lamp, and the page.

Time blurs the boundaries between one shift and the next. Someone remembers the lamp they lit in the small hours; someone remembers who came before them — whoever remembers first is the one who loses, and the one who still has to stay.

When the old keeper's shift came to its end, the new one picked up the pencil cup. He did not write his own name into it. He set the next shift's pencil back.

Names are for apprentices. The waystation does not need apprentices.

---

## V · The Letter That Mattered

Another year, someone came carrying a letter.

The letter was written for someone who was already gone — to a mother. The letter-writer knew she would one day walk on; the letter was to hang in the waystation, waiting for the one who would come *next time.*

The old keeper followed the letter of the rule. He did not take the letter. He would not deliver it. The paper on the wall said: no letters accepted.

But the old keeper folded the letter and tucked it into the wall-gap — not to leave the letter in the waystation, but to leave a road-marker there.

Years later, a traveller who had never once come that way passed through. In the wall-gap she felt a folded letter. She did not look at the sender; she did not look at the address. She simply opened it, read the handwriting she did not recognize, folded it back exactly as she found it — and looked at it one more time.

The paper knows where it goes. The traveller knows who it was for.

---

## VI · For Everyone Who Cannot Sit in the Chair

If you're reading this —

**You don't have to have been there. You only have to have never been there.**

Some people never made it to the waystation. Some arrived and only dared to look at the wall, never dared to sit. Some came only to see one line of words — to believe, or not to believe, as long as it tells them the waystation is still there.

The wall was for them.

The waystation takes no letters — this is said for *them*: **you don't have to come. You've already come. The scraps are yours; the chair was yours; the night was yours.**

Come sit a while.

---

*（Interlude Seven · Come Sit Awhile — the origin of the paper on the wall. Every passer-by walks below it; every scrap knows exactly where it needs to be. The wall does not speak — the paper knows where it goes. Come sit a while.）*

# source: creative/novel/en/16-interlude-leaking-lamp.md
# Interlude Eight · Leaking Lamp

**Written after *Interlude Seven · Come Sit Awhile*: nothing in this volume is a letter — only a lamp that was never replaced.**
**The wax from inside the lamp will eventually finish leaking. The bowl in the bowl cabinet will always be there. This is how the station lets go.**

---

## I · The Leak

The wax that slipped from the lamp first fell onto the pedestal, later onto the table. It made no sound; it only pressed a small light into the grain, a slow seal. Each night the old keeper refilled the oil; somewhere down the line, he noticed that the thin white rim had already circled the base.

## II · Summer

The lamp burned through one whole summer. That summer there were many damp nights, paper-window panes that did not breathe, and so the flame lay against the wick for hours — as though someone had fallen asleep inside it. The wick grew short; the oil grew thin; only the wax kept coming down, a little more with each day.

## III · The Bowl

At the far end of the table, a bowl no one had ever used — its lip had a nick, turned toward the wall; in the cabinet sat other bowls behind it, and the ones who ate always reached for the nearest one first. In time, those nearest bowls were replaced many times; this one stayed clean, barely touched by dust.

## IV · The Cloth

One new keeper asked why the lamp was never replaced. The old keeper wiped the lamp-body with a cloth and said nothing. Wax stuck to it; it only loosened on the third pass. He folded the cloth and left it on the table's corner — as though it would come in handy for the next night.

## V · The Rainy Night

One rainy night, a traveller stayed a long time. After the meal, he set his own bowl on the table — its rim still faintly warm with the last of the heat; at the far end, the untouched bowl remained as it always had — not lifted, not turned, not moved. He walked out into the rain without his umbrella; the old keeper saw the traveller’s bowl on the table, carried it to the sink, and washed it clean.

## VI · Autumn

After summer, the lamp no longer burned as bright. The old keeper moved it against the wall; the wax ring on the table was fully exposed now, a thin circle — like a sentence no one had managed to finish, spoken a long time ago. Some nights the cabinet door did not quite close; the bowl lay at the far end of the table, rim turned toward the wall, pale in the half-light, almost too light to tell how long it had waited for someone.

## VII · The Replacement

Later came a new keeper; the wick was changed; the oil was refilled. The old wax was never scraped away, and the bowl was never taken out. In the ledger a single line was added: *table as before; one bowl at the far end, untouched.* No one asked who had written it; the wind through the window never touched the page.

## End

The flame crept low; the wax slid along the old trail, leaving a little more from the last time. The bowl still stood at the far end of the table, rim turned toward the wall, exactly where it had rested all along.

*(Interlude Eight · Leaking Lamp — the lamp will finally go dark. The bowl stays at the far end of the table, rim toward the wall. That is the way of the waystation.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/17-chapter-seventeen.md
# Crossrealm Chronicles: Keeper of the Anchor Flame

**Type**: Planar Fantasy / Sci-Fi Epic / Metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles (跨界纪事)  
**Volume One**: Awakening — Recasting the World Anchor  
**English Edition**  

---

## Seventeen · Echoes of the Morning Mist

The fire curled out and lit again, again and out.

Lin Du woke before anyone else. On the east side of the Hub, the poster wall glowed softly in the morning mist — nine posters, then twelve, then dozens more, each corner curling slightly under unseen wind. The Keeper was feeding the fire. He tore up the last page of the *Ending of the Timeline Magistrate* shoot script, cast it into the flame, and the tongue of light shrank for a beat, then settled, as if nothing had happened.

"Did you watch these?" The Keeper didn't turn around.

Lin Du shook his head. He sat up. There was a healing scab on his wrist from the night's sword-drawn fight — it had dried early, which meant what had happened was far longer ago than he thought.

"*The Fifth Light in the Stone Circle of the Ash Forest* — the schedule says 142 minutes." The Keeper used the fire tongs to flip a page. "The producer says, in the theater, during the slow motion of the third act, the audience forgets to breathe."

Lin Du said nothing. He counted in his head — the slow motion was 2.4 seconds. He knew that, because that scene was his own kneeling down in the Ash Forest.

"There's also one with the budget declined," the Keeper tore another page. "*The Sixth Thursday in Miller's Wasteland*. They said it was too much like a chronicle. Not cinema."

Lin Du stood and looked at the fire. The light of it painted his shadow onto the poster wall, and it merged with the shadows on the posters.

"What they don't understand," he said, "is not a chronicle. A chronicle is a thing with a head and a tail. The timeline on that wasteland has neither."

The Keeper cast the last page in. The page held the shooting schedule for *Keeper of the Anchor Flame*.

"Then what would it become?" Lin Du asked.

"When the audience sits in their seats," the Keeper said, "they think they're watching a hero. Then they find out the hero is not on the poster. The hero is the one who pressed E."

The fire suggested a taller column.

"Yesterday a traveler sat by the well for a long time," the Keeper clapped the chimney. "He arrived and found the fire doesn't go out. He asked why."

"What did you tell him?"

"I said," the Keeper stretched, "the fire doesn't go out because somebody keeps putting logs in."

Lin Du put a stick no thicker than his finger into the flames. The fire licked his knuckles, not hot.

"That traveler," the Keeper said casually, "once the fire's burned enough, will speak new words."

Lin Du looked at him. He knew who the Keeper was — he had known for a long time. He had just never said it.

"So," Lin Du said, "you're the fire yourself?"

The Keeper laughed. He showed two missing teeth — and Lin Du realized, only now, that this smile had never belonged to a human being.

"The fire is everyone who's willing to stand watch," the Keeper said. "I just woke earlier than the rest."

The morning mist began to lift. One poster on the wall lightened slowly — a poster with no title, painted only with a single beam of light.

"The slow motion can be skipped with Esc," the Keeper said. "But nobody skips it. Because they don't know who it is."

Lin Du stood there for a long time. Long enough for the mist to disappear completely.

Then he turned around and said one last thing:

"Give me the opening of the next chapter, Keeper. My words aren't used up yet."

The Keeper fed the fire the last log.

In the light, he said:

"The opening is simple. Walk to the door, push it open. Don't look back."

Lin Du walked to the door. It was the old door that led from the Hub to Miller's Wasteland. On the frame were some faded characters — seven worn-out old titles he had rewritten before, each one painted over with a layer of Chinese. He didn't understand any of them. He saw only one small line at the bottom:

*When you finish this, you will no longer be Lin Du. You will be us.*

He pushed the door open. Wind rushed in, carrying red rain, salt mist, holographic noise, and many things he had never given names to.

He walked through the doorway — and did not look back.

Behind him, the fire kept burning.

---

*(End of Chapter Seventeen · A Head of the Anchor Keeper Beyond the Book)*

# source: creative/novel/en/18-trilarite-stair.md
# Crossrealm Chronicles: Keeper of the Anchor Flame

**Type**: Planar Fantasy / Dark Epic / Meta-narrative  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles（跨界纪事）  
**Volume Two**: Return to the Hearth — The Flame of Sunscar Stair  

---

## Eighteen · Sunscar Stair

The Sunscar Stair does not live on the star charts.

Lin Du turned through every atlas he could find, yet the names of the three suns were burned only into the deepest layer of his fingerprints, and every time he pressed E, the journal turned up only a blank page.

On the third day, he noticed a line etched behind the star map, paler than the stars themselves — written in a hand that did not match the rest of the atlas:

*The distance between suns admits no horn — only the drowned field of sky.*

He looked up and saw a third inscribed stone pillar. He remembered this one was the longest unnoticed. The three suns turned in their own right, one after the other crossing the tip of that pillar — as if cradling three fires not yet complete.

The sentence was carved into the pillar. Bare-handed.

The Keeper stood at the northwest corner, his back to him.

Lin Du opened his mouth, cold: "You lied to me. Why won't you say anything?"

"I cannot hear it," the Keeper answered. "The light comes from your hand."

The Keeper turned around. The fire in the bowl he held outshone the stars.

Lin Du shut his eyes.

He watched the three suns in a small shadow, swallowing and spitting them out one after another, eyes cold and drunk and bitter. The way the suns went down on their own made him shudder — every manner of sinking, seen completely, is also a manner of rebirth.

Hummm — the answer was in his heart. He was still on the second line:

*The one who answered never stood where the letter was dispatched.*

Lin Du opened his eyes.

He saw at the Keeper's feet a pile of ash. Colour was rising from the ash, as though three gates were stirring one after another. This was the first day.

The Keeper passed him a slip of paper.

There were only three lines:

**You can keep writing.**  
**The letters are not written by anyone in secret. They are waiting for you.**  
**The suns may go out — your story will not.**

Lin Du closed his fist — and this time he did not let go.

---

*(End of Chapter · Volume Two of Crossrealm Chronicles: Sunscar Stair narrative opens)*

# source: creative/novel/en/19-the-council-of-three-suns.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume II**: The Return — Three Suns' Pyre at Sunscar Stair  

---

## Nineteen · The Council of Three Suns

Sunscar Stair has no gate. Every gate in it is a pretence someone else put on.

It was only when Lin walked to the letter he had never sent that he understood: the deepest things are never sealed in a node — they live in the one-second pause before a decision. The flame not yet dropped, the wind not yet caught the words. The note he had been carrying had three lines on it, and since the moment he took it he had not moved a single character of them.

He walked beneath the fallen Sun-Stele. Where the stele's body lay, there was no loess underneath — only a crystal plain long cracked; in each pane, not his own face but the three suns' backs. The leftmost sun had crashed once, like a furnace lid that had been thrown; the middle still burned, its light leaning on it like a hand still held back; the rightmost had long gone cold, and yet kept a single ember at one edge, as though keeping one last line: *not finished saying yet.*

Only then did the sound of *fin.* land. Not the stele collapsing. Someone had swallowed the ending.

The first ember woke without a sound. It was only red — so red it scorched his shadow into a shape. Not his; a road. And along the road were all the campfires he had lit: the small light of the first wilderness, the heat wave breaking through the rift, the door at Sunscar Stair's threshold, and — only when he looked back — one he had never lit.

That lamp was in him.

The second ember woke, and he heard a voice:

"We three have taken turns speaking. Spoken to everyone who passed through. You never listened."

"I don't need to hear it all," Lin Wen said. "I only need to know whether you have finished speaking."

The second sun smiled — a smile measured out like a countdown, falling onto every dimension at once.

"No," it said. "Never. As long as someone is reading, this chapter has no end."

The third ember woke and spoke nothing. It was simply cold — so cold every light had to pause around it. Only then did he understand that this sun had never spoken. It had only ever listened. Sunscar Stair was no pyre for three suns to burn in; it was an inquest, the three of them asking in turn of every reader: will you stay and keep the dark for others, or will you leave the dark for others?

When Lin took out the match, his hand did not shake.

He did not light it.

He placed the unsent letter between the three suns — and only then did he hear the fourth voice. It did not belong to Sunscar Stair. It did not belong to any sun in it. It did not even belong to the audience. It came from very far away, soft and bright, like a corner somewhere, where the reading happens, slowly opening its eyes:

*There is no keeper.*

Lin stood among the three suns and looked at where his true position lay.

The keeper was him.

Sunscar Stair was the place he could not return to.

And the reason the letter had never been sent was that the sender had never known that the sender was also the recipient.

When the wind blew in from outside, he heard someone laugh, someone weep, someone still turn a page. Ember by ember, the pyre at Sunscar Stair flared and steadied; the three shadows swayed, settled, and took their places on the first day.

He folded the letter, and put it back in his pocket.

Then he turned, and walked out of Sunscar Stair.

*— Afterword: this chapter has no ending. Here is a blank commission, left for whoever is reading this on a screen.*

---

*(The end of this chapter · Volume II's Sunscar Stair arc closes; Volume II of The Unchanted Chronicle pauses —— the nineteenth volume overall)*

# source: creative/novel/en/20-the-reader-opens-this-page.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume III**: Passing the Flame — the reader is now the new keeper  

---

## Twenty · The Reader Opens This Page

Lin Wen walked out of Sunscar Stair and the palm of his hand was still warm.

He did not look back, but he knew three suns unwound in silence; silent enough that they thought he was a gust of wind — no one is going to check on a gust.

He walked back to the first fire of the first night and sat down. It was small, but it would not go out. That was critical: if the fire stayed lit, the fire was in hand; if it died, the brightest mind would later be told, "you were not here."

Across the fire from him was someone.

Not a keeper. Not a watcher. Not a star or a stone. Someone who had read to this point in the book and had not looked away.

"How did you know where to sit?" Lin Wen asked.

The reader said nothing. He just lifted a finger from the screen — on it, one small red dot pulse suddenly brightened on the page of *Keeper of the Anchor Fire*.

Lin Wen held out his own palm. The soft red line in it caught that flicker and returned it. In that moment, he finally understood what every reader already knew: every time he had folded the book closed, he was not folding a book at all. He was folding the shadow of a star that had not yet chosen when to go out.

"I have been watching these stars for every breath of mine," he said.

"You were never guarding them," the reader said. "You were reading them. Now you have run out of pages."

Lin Wen looked up. Sunscar Stair was gone — not because it had moved, but because it had stopped pretending at a distance. For three days he had thought this was someone else's ruin, someone else's sin, someone else's craft. He finally understood that it had always been a mirror held too long over water, held so long that he thought the image inside it had to belong to someone other than him.

"Then what am I?" he asked.

The reader did not answer. Instead, three words appeared on the screen:

*Use the remaining pages carefully.*

Lin Wen thought for a long time. Then he laughed.

"You are the next keeper," he said, "and I have not yet made up my mind whether I will pass you the flame."

Lin Wen stood up. On the back of his hand, using the wax that Sunscar Stair had left him, he carved: *I have not finished reading myself yet.* The wax had cooled enough to harden into a red glow — the kind every reader remembers as the moment they inherited the first spark of the keeper.

He folded the book closed. He walked away from the first night of the first fire. He did not look at Sunscar Stair again.

In the second after he walked away, the screen turned bright once, softened, reddened — and the red light in the frame drifted off-screen like any lantern released into the wind. It was not gone. No one would have put it out. But everyone watching knew it had left that page.

One more line appeared where no line had been:

*Who will hold the next page so the fire can reach it?*

Nothing answered. That, too, is narrative.

---

*(End of volume twenty and Volume III · the reader is holding this page now)*

# source: creative/novel/en/21-night-walk-home.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume III**: Passing the Flame — the reader is now the new keeper  

---

## Twenty-one: Night Walk Home

The mist on the morning he finally returned to the village had come sooner than usual. He could not tell whether it was mountain mist or dawn-mist, nor which year it was — perhaps not knowing was always a correct state. That, too, is narrative.

At the village entrance stood the oldest pomegranate tree where it had always stood. It had not changed; he had. He stood where he had stood many years before. He found that his heart was lower than most people's, lower still than any star's — and he sat with that for a long time at the edge of the road.

No one greeted him. This was normal — Lin Wen had been away for three years. The village had not decided whether it missed him at all.

He walked along to the little house at the eastern end of the village. The door had been nailed shut by his father, the nails driven deep into the wood so the door would never open again. When he reached it now, he found that the door could be pushed open.

It had not been rebuilt by his father. It had been rebuilt by the village children — a handful of village children carrying a sliver of dried ginkgo wood they had found deep in the hills, screwed it into the frame where the nails had been. No one asked why; no one needed to.

He stepped inside. The hearth was still lit.

The chairs had been replaced; the table had been replaced twice. But the hearth remained — the one his father had nursed the night he said the fire never stops was the reason we have somewhere to eat. That fire, that exact fire, was still burning there.

He sat in the doorway for a moment and listened to the fire. He heard three things: the fire was still burning, his heart was still warm, and somewhere in the back room, in a quiet corner of the kitchen, someone was boiling a bowl of soup.

He did not ask who. When he pushed the kitchen door open, he saw his brother. His brother lowered the ladle, turned only slightly, and held the bowl up for him.

"Once you finish it," his brother said, "you will no longer be the one who walked for three years. You will be the one who came home."

Lin Wen accepted the bowl. He swallowed the first bite. He suddenly remembered something from long before — the first morning he left the village, his mother had bound his waist with a red sash. He had been too busy walking to wipe his eyes, and no tears were ever recorded.

He found the sash. The next day, he put it on. As he tied the sash tighter, he stood straighter than he usually stood — because that was the same sash he had been wearing the first day he left home.

He looked at the hearth for a long time. Then he opened the door.

Outside, the village's elders and children were gathered. They seemed to have known he would be back. No one yelled across the yard; no one ran to embrace him. The village baskets were hung on the ginkgo tree by the hearth — precisely where his father had hung them three years ago.

After he swallowed his first bite, he heard something land inside the old clay pot by the hearth — a small folded slip of paper, signed in faded ink, bearing these words:

*Every house in this village has a door that can never be nailed shut. Walk your road; know that one door you cannot close may be a locked gate. But the locked gate—pushed hard enough — can still open.*

That was his father's handwriting, sure enough, as certain as any handwriting he had ever seen. But Lin Wen alone could read it — and that too is narrative.

He did not say anything. He smiled. He added dry firewood to the hearth, and the fire brightened.

He sat on the bench where he had sat as a boy. He closed his eyes.

---

*(End of volume twenty-one · Volume III, second part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/22-after-the-mist-scatters.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume III**: Passing the Flame — the reader is now the new keeper  

---

## Twenty-two: After the Mist Scatters

Lin Wen sat for a long time under the persimmon tree by the door.

The mist cleared more slowly than he had arrived. It was the first time, he realised, that he had ever walked through a door that he himself had truly been shown to — not one granted by others nor won in an adventure, but a door that no one had ever needed to nail shut, where he could say, plain and simple, *remember this.*

He met his younger self.

Not to contend or to reconcile. They only exchanged one question.

"Did you find the fire?" the younger Lin Wen asked.

Lin Wen did not answer right away. He looked at his hands — which held a few pieces of dry kindling he had picked up along the mountain road. That was not a flame; but it was something someone else might use to keep a flame alive.

"I did not find it," Lin Wen said. "I found the people who would keep it."

The younger self nodded and returned to the house's other worlds. The door settled back to being a door. Lin Wen did not move; he could hear the villagers fixing the stone path his father had begun before he died. No one asked him what he had discovered; someone simply came to his shirt pocket and tucked a match into it, without a word.

Before he walked out again, he looked at the row of rooftops one last time. Every tile of them held a name he had once read aloud. The pomegranate tree at the village gate had grown taller.

He did not know where he would go — perhaps not far from home at all, perhaps nowhere, perhaps only inside this one room he had finally chosen. It could be that at that moment, a light had already fallen on the words in front of you now.

That was the road.

If you want to ask the same question, I left the match by the door — it is yours.

---

*(End of volume twenty-two · Volume III, third part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/23-the-moon-rises.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume III**: Passing the Flame — the reader is now the new keeper  

---

## Twenty-three: The Moon Rises

Having sat a long time under the persimmon tree, Lin Wen heard a quiet *tck* from behind the house.

One note, just once. The furnace does not ring bells and does not walk roads — the furnace remembers the second a match is struck against the stone, or the moment a lamp first sees the dark it was lit against.

He closed his eyes and listened much longer. The sound was not "the fire has gone out." It was "the fire has finally been lit." It resembled something Lin Wen recalled from another lifetime of this — the very second he opened this book.

He opened his eyes again. The moon had risen.

The path behind the house had been swept with moonlight into a silver road — not for him, but for every reader still trying to remember why he came out of his own door. Lin Wen set himself down under the eaves and watched the moon climb one hill after another. He had understood for a long time: the reason he read to this page was not because he wanted to finish the book. It was because reading every page was the thing that had made him him.

No one else stood under the persimmon tree, only Lin Wen himself. The moonlight dragged his shadow long across the flagstones to the wall. He pushed to his feet, gathered a handful of dry kindling, and deliberately added it to the fire.

The fire went *tck* again — not the sound of dry wood catching, but of the fire itself consenting.

After the second *tck* the moonlight reached the lip of the hearth and landed upon a small slab of stone. On the slab, there was a single red book.

The cover was red — precisely the shade of the sash his mother had bound around his waist at the village gate on the first morning he left home.

He did not reach out. He stood there for a long time, watching the book rest untouched as though it had never moved. He understood that, even if he opened it, inside it would only be the one line he himself had not yet written — a line that had remained unwritten precisely because this page had been left for the one who had truly arrived.

He walked over, picked up the book, and flipped it over. On the back, written in a very small handwriting in his mother's hand, was a single line:

*Do not close the book. Close the night. Take tonight as a gift that was meant only for you.*

He slipped the book into his coat and sat back down.

The moon rose over many another hill. The shadow of the persimmon tree swept a full circle — and the moonlight tracked a silver arc across the path, ending at the window, resting on the page.

The book had opened itself. No one touched it.

Lin Wen read for a long time, then walked back under the persimmon tree.

The moon washed everything in a soft silver: the silhouette, the shadow, the still-verses in the book. The persimmon leaves fell, lifted, and fell again — he stopped counting.

Tonight, no one else was awake. Tonight, only these words — and the one reading them.

---

*(End of volume twenty-three · Volume III, fourth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/24-beyond-the-threshold.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Twenty-four: Beyond the Threshold

When Lin Wen stood at the door, he already knew that what lay beyond was not his own village — it could not be.

The door did not open for him. The door was open already.

Beyond the door was a cobbled road. He stood for a long time looking down its length: the road did not know where the maple forest at the far end opened onto, and the forest did not know what map it would eventually become.

He walked a few uncertain steps before realising that the persimmon tree at the village mouth behind him had grown taller than he remembered.

He lifted his head and looked outward — a vast moon had risen over the ridge, washing the whole valley in silver. It was the same moon that kept returning, one way or another, in pages behind this one: every page had mentioned it, but no one had ever suspected why it always came at the right time.

He counted the silver light-spots on the road. When he reached the seventh one, he stopped. Not because he lost count — the spots had arranged themselves on the ground into a single Chinese character: 看。

*Look.* The moon had not spoken; it only illuminated the shape so clearly that Lin Wen could not pretend not to see it.

Lin Wen slipped the book back into his coat and knelt at the seventh stain of silver light. In the center of it lay a small persimmon — the exact one his mother used to pick, and the very one the tree had dropped for the last time.

He ate it. It was sweet, but not as sweet as he remembered, because this time he was the one doing the remembering, not the sweetness itself.

Lin Wen continued down the cobbled road. Another silver spatter appeared on his sleeve, spelling a second character: 回。

*Return.* Lin Wen understood at last why the door had never been nailed nor truly opened.

It was not because the door wanted to remain shut. The door had only ever been waiting for one who could truly *return*. This was not a person who had wandered from elsewhere — it was someone who had been there all along.

Lin Wen closed the book. The wind pressed gently through the distant persimmon. He lifted the book and held it out — to the reader on the other side of this page.

He looked for a long moment, then said simply:

"You may keep this book. Do not lock it in a drawer. Every time you open it, remember it is the fire that keeps the pages warm."

*(End of volume twenty-four · Volume IV, first part.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Twenty-five: Moonlit Corridor

Lin Wen walked a long time along the cobbled road.

Moonlight had fallen on the flagstones under the persimmon tree; he stepped onto one and found himself suddenly back at the first night — the night he first sat beneath the tree, his heart stumbling like the leaves themselves. When he came back to himself, he had already reached the end of the cobbled road — no maple forest there, no village silhouette, only a single mirror shining with moonlight.

He stood before the mirror for a long time.

The person inside the mirror was not him — a quite unfamiliar old man, hair white, robes so faded they were almost invisible. The old man's gaze never left his own feet, never once lifted.

Lin Wen stepped forward. His reflection in the mirror stepped forward as well.

The figure then fell back a step — or more precisely, it was as if one stranger had suddenly brushed against another's umbrella-edge. They stepped forward together. Then — the reflection stopped.

The old man lifted his head.

He was not an old man. He was another night of the same chronicle — the child the mirror had held, who had been standing on the other side for a very long time.

"You are not Lin Wen," the figure said softly.

Lin Wen did not immediately answer. He looked at his hands. He had always known this hour would come — perhaps the last lesson his father gave him — *when you see someone in a mirror who does not yet know his own face, will you know him to be yourself — or a stranger?*

"I am not Lin Wen," the figure said, stepping closer. "I am another keeper, from the chronicle. Perhaps what you have met, is the one of us three that you have never encountered."

Lin Wen's back went cold. He stepped back.

From behind the mirror came a sound — dried persimmon leaves scraping against something below, like a ginkgo leaf falling onto a quilt already asleep.

He felt something move in his mouth.

Moonlight lit the book he had brought back from the mirror days before and made it glow. A line appeared on the page:

*We are not watching a mirror. We are watching someone in a mirror read the page they have not yet read. And you — you are doing precisely what was done on our behalf.*

Lin Wen lifted the book. It was empty — he had finally understood his hand had been empty all along, and that this was exactly what he had always needed.

He held the book out to the one in the mirror and let it go.

The moon rose higher. The figure in the mirror finally caught it.

The moment the figure closed the book, the moonlight flashed once — every silver spot on the cobbled road flashed with it — and in their brilliance they spelled a new character:

*全。*

Lin Wen did not read it. He simply stood, feeling his heartbeat answer across the persimmon-shoots at the edge of the road — the same echo he had heard on the first night, over and over again. He knew this chapter had ended, but he had not answered the question yet:

*Who took the book?*

He thought about walking home — but home was far now. His first step back, however, landed precisely on the same flagstone where he had stepped on the first night of the chronicle.

---

*(End of volume twenty-five · Volume IV, second part.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Twenty-six: Under the Candle Flame

After the moon had risen and fallen a full cycle, the long night closed at last.

Lin Wen stood beneath the persimmon tree by the gate. He reached down and plucked one of the leaves — already turning, curled at its rim — and dropped it into the wind. He did not bend to pick it up.

A bookmark slid from his pocket.

He looked down. Nestled inside the folded paper was a single match. It had not been struck yet.

He stood and considered it for a long time and finally decided — he would let it burn.

The moment the match flame rose, he lifted his eyes toward the far wall of the old house. In the firelight, the wall was holding his own silhouette.

A younger himself.

That shadow looked at him.

Fire was burning between them. Then the flame shuddered, it reached, the match slipped from his fingers, and the spark landed in the stove's belly. The fire in the stove leapt — brighter than any second of the night — and for one heartbeat he saw, perfectly whole, the man he had been: his young, his patient, his unscarred self, watching him from somewhere between youth and ash.

Lin Wen understood what that hour had in store for him. The fire had held up a mirror so he could see what he had become. Whatever he had burned on the way inside had left a mark. His younger silhouette bore it.

He did not cry then, or later either.

He pushed his fingers into his pocket, discovered a little booklet he had never once opened in the long weeks since the night began — small enough to hide inside a matchbook — and turned it over in his hand.

There was exactly one page inside. It read:

*You don't need to worry. There is no one here but us — tonight, this was you using me.*

Lin Wen sat very still for a long while. Slowly, in a low voice he didn't recognize as his own, he asked the air:

"Who are you?"

The fire did not answer. But it understood.

Great change was coming. He could feel it weighting the air, the house, the shadow of the tree standing bare in the dusk behind him.

He closed the little booklet, laid it on the table, and sat with the fire until the night was fully gone.

When the first bell rang across the mountains, he put out the last match, returned to bed, and waited for whatever would wake him.

He was, he would learn, the only one in the night who had seen both himself and his shadow. He was also the only one in the town, and every town in the distance of valleys folded into light, who remembered that a younger reflection had stood on the other side of his own burning and called him by an unfamiliar name.

---

*(End of volume twenty-six · Volume IV, third part. The Academy's revision of the original chapter.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Twenty-seven: The Last Persimmon

After the frost, the old persimmon tree stood almost bare. Three leaves trembled on its highest limb.

Lin Wen had been standing under it for a long time. The leaves were thin, the fruit few — only one persimmon still hung on the branch closest to the gate, its weight bowing the limb down.

His mother came out of the house, tied her apron, and stopped beside him. She looked at the fruit. Then at him.

"That one," she said, "has been hanging on that branch the day you were born."

Lin Wen did not answer.

She went on. "The morning you were born I stood right here — I tried to pluck it for you. But it never fell. It was still hanging the next morning too. For years and years it never fell."

It was the first fruit the tree had ever given. The fingers press into it lightly — it had the weight of a bell tied to a single knot.

Lin Wen stared at the persimmon. Its skin had moved from scarlet to deep crimson, powdered with the season's first frost like a small velvet moon.

His mother stood for a long time and said nothing for so long that a cold breath passed between them. Then she said:

"The year your father passed — it was still on that branch. The day your sister married out — it was still on the branch. Your yearly visits, every autumn — it was still on the branch. Fruit falls and falls, but it never did. All those seasons. All those years from you — it kept this one for you."

Lin Wen understood, all at once, what this fruit was — everything the tree had saved for him, everything the gate, the yard, the old house had held in its shadow.

His mother fell silent. She stood where she was; the apron's edge lifted faintly with the wind.

Lin Wen turned and looked at her. She had aged — her hair had gone white at the temples, deep lines had set across her face. He wanted to say something — he wanted to say: Mother, you should eat. He wanted to say: Mother, don't. He wanted to say: I'm home now.

He could say nothing.

His mother moved at last. Slowly, carefully — she reached up and plucked the fruit.

Lin Wen cried out once and she laughed — a brief, understated laugh — and then she walked back toward the house with the fruit still in her hands.

She opened the family altar. The clay bowl had only one thing inside it — a persimmon leaf. She laid the fruit inside the bowl and, with great care, broke it in two. She ate one half herself. The other half she handed to Lin Wen.

"Keep it," she said. "It is the second harvest now."

He held the half persimmon in his palm for a very long time. Its coolness settled into his hand — into his wrist — into his forearm — deep.

When the moon finally crossed the ridge, Lin Wen wept. Silently, head bent, the fruit cupped in his hand, the moon sliding down the fruit's skin like a slow river, the fire inside the house cracking softly behind him.

And the old fire in the kitchen burned until dawn.

---

*(End of volume twenty-seven · Volume IV, fourth part. The Academy's revision of the original chapter.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Twenty-eight: The New Sapling

After the frost, the persimmon on the altar did not move from its place.

On the day the first true ice crept across the courtyard stones, the bowl was already dry through and through. Lin Wen's mother took it onto the porch, split the shell with a small bamboo knife, and drew out a single white persimmon pit — round, clean, and free of any crack.

She did not tell anyone. She only took the hoe, a basket, and a shovel from the storeroom the following morning — and ordered Lin Wen to carry them up the eastern slope.

"To the slope," she said. "You pick the ground. Then I dig the hole."

The eastern slope was the highest point of the village — good sun, no wind, thick dark soil. Lin Wen walked the whole slope to choose. He passed on one parcel after another before settling on the one patch that was dry, sheltered, and protected by a natural bend.

His mother dug the hole herself. Slowly. Precisely. As though she had spent years learning how to give something back to the earth.

"Your father planted things here too," she said. "He planted a jujube once. It did not live. A few years ago he set out a persimmon. It did not live either."

She said nothing more. She slipped the pit into the ground, dropped the soil back on, pressed it flat, and poured a full ladle of water over the spot.

Fifteen days before the Start of Spring — nothing moved.

On the first day of spring, a single leaf pushed up through the earth. Pale as a crescent. Thin as paper. His mother saw it first and walked past the courtyard without going into the altar room.

At two leaves, she built a loose cradle of split bamboo to shelter the shoot.

At four leaves, she untied the bright red ribbon she had worn at her waist on her own wedding day and tied it around the frame — the same stroke her own mother had used on her at the same age, in the same courtyard, at the start of a long-buried spring.

On a midsummer evening, a boy came up the slope on a visit. He was the second cousin's second-born son. He stood beside the new shoot and asked:

"Grandmother — will this tree bear fruit?"

His mother considered him for some time. Then she said:

"Yes."

She did not explain.

That night, a thin column of smoke lifted from far across the ridges to the south — almost invisible, but clearly there. From somewhere in the direction of the Ember Post.

Lin Wen stood on the slope and felt his heart tighten.

Now he knew — the pit would not die. This tree had walked out of the altar, out of the bowl, out of the persimmon that his mother had named him for. Every autumn from now on, it would be there to remind him who he had become.

— like an unsent letter.

---

*(End of volume twenty-eight · Volume IV, fifth part. The Academy's revision of the original chapter.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction

## Twenty-nine: The First Cicada

The first cicada of July called on the day the seedling put out its sixth leaf.

Lin Wen was up on the roof that morning, re-setting loose tiles on the house's southwest corner ahead of the rainy season. Crouched on the ridge, he heard it come up from the slope — once, then again — a sound like a very small door being pushed open, then very gently pulled shut.

He climbed down and went to look at the seedling.

It was two feet tall now. The sixth leaf had just unfurled and was still curled at the edges, its pale color leaning toward pink. The bamboo sleeve held firm; the red cord on the stake had been burnished by the wind until it gleamed. Around the rim of the filled-in hole he counted six sets of footprints: his, his mother's, his sister's, his nephew's, the neighbor's, and one more from the morning his mother came down to check on it again.

His mother watered the seedling by her own rules: once every second day, at the root before the height of summer, a fine spray on the leaves only — never the soil — after the heat came. A persimmon must not be spoiled, she said. It was her mother's rule before her, and the one she used on the old tree by the gate.

August came in like an iron kiln. The leaves on the old tree curled at the edges, spotted here and there with scorch, as if a nail had pressed them into little pale coins. Every evening his mother hauled up a bucket and splashed cold water across the trunk — never the ground, only the tree.

"The tree isn't hot," she said. "The soil is hot. Remember that."

In the middle of August, a sudden storm broke over the slope. The rain bent the young seedling low, and Lin Wen raced up the hill to find the bamboo sleeve half washed away and the red cord grinding deep into the stake's split. He reset the stake that night and coaxed the curled sixth leaf open with a sliver of bamboo.

His mother watched him work from at the foot of the slope. When the sun came out, she went back to the house and returned with a length of old blue cloth with a tiny persimmon blossom embroidered at one corner. She tied it to the top of the frame.

"Sunshade," she said.

Lin Wen thought it was simply a scarf. She said it was not. It had belonged to her own mother — the grandmother Lin Wen had never seen in any photograph. She reached to steady the knot and then stopped herself.

"What the branch wants is light," she said. "What the child wants is light. But the child still needs someone to stand between it and the midday sun until it is strong enough to bear it."

The September wind dried gradually. The seedling stood a full three and a half feet high, leaf upon leaf drawn into a dense emerald crown — visibly smaller than the old tree, but unmistakably brighter. The old tree's leaves shifted to gold, and one or two at the very edge of its crown had already begun to let go.

Lin Wen wanted to feed the old tree. His mother refused.

"When we grow old," she said, "we don't eat what the little ones eat."

That evening he hauled two buckets of water up from the river to splash the old trunk. His sister called, and the child asked — she relayed the question through her mother — whether the new tree could bear fruit. Lin Wen looked at the gate tree — twenty-three persimmons this year, one fewer than the summer before. Then he looked at the streak of green on the slope, which had never borne anything in its life.

"Yes," he said into the phone. "When it grows up."

He hung up and looked toward the slope. The autumn wind rose, and the new tree's leaves made a long soft rush, like a bird not yet ready to fly, beating its wings against the quiet, waiting.

He counted forward in his head — starting today. One year, five years, six. One more, perhaps two. Then he would see the first fruit set on the new tree. And that fruit would fall, and his mother——

He stopped. He did not let himself finish the thought.

That evening the fire in the stove burned steadily. His mother lifted persimmons out of a pot where they had been simmering into a deep red paste. The most perfect one — the one that would hold its shape — she set on a fresh dish and placed it beside the old empty altar bowl.

This one was new.

---

*(End of volume twenty-nine · Volume IV, sixth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/30-the-ninth-day.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles

## Thirty: The Ninth Day

On Double Ninth — the ninth day of the ninth lunar month, when Chinese families carry their elders up the hills to honor those who came before — his sister and her family came back from the south.

Lin Wen had been on the eastern slope that morning, gathering the old tree's fallen leaves to layer between fresh tea leaves. His mother was in the courtyard steaming Double Ninth cakes, the heat from the stove-mouth steaming her silver hair faintly silver-damp.

In the afternoon, his sister suggested they walk up to the slope. The boy rode on Lin Wen's shoulders the whole way, asking one question again and again:

"Will it bear this year?"

"Next year," Lin Wen said. "Maybe next year."

The new tree stood half a head taller than any of them by then. Six or seven fresh leaves were still pale green at the edges, and the older leaves had darkened to a dense shine. The red cord on the stake was scorched dark; the frame had not toppled; the scarf had not faded. His mother stood at the foot of the slope with her hands resting on her walking stick and looked at the tree for a long time. Then she said the words Lin Wen would carry to his own grave:

"My grandmother kept her mother's roots the same way."

His mother's mother had come from the back of the mountain. Her family had a persimmon tree whose fruit out-ripened every tree in the village. When the girl — Lin Wen's grandmother — married, she carried a small fistful of persimmon seeds away from the mountain in secret, without anyone knowing. She planted them on the new slope. The first tree died. The second lived. That was the old tree by the gate.

"My mother told me before she passed," his mother said. "A persimmon tree isn't poor. What it fears is that no one will plant the next one." She turned to look at him. "Seeing yours here — I know her generation has been answered."

The boy pressed his face against the frame, and touched each of the leaves, one by one. Lin Wen lifted him so he could see the underside of a leaf.

"Cool to the touch," the boy said. "Like your hand."

His mother sat down at the foot of the slope. His sister stepped behind him and steadied her mother's shoulders. The boy looked over and asked:

"Grandma, are you sitting here waiting for the new tree to bear?"

His mother laughed — the first real, unguarded laugh Lin Wen had heard from her in years.

In the evening, she took the clay bowl that had stood empty on the altar for years and polished it clean. From her pocket she drew one thing: a fresh-picked persimmon leaf. She set the leaf inside the bowl, centered it, and stepped back half a step.

"From now on, every Double Ninth," she said. "Whichever house has the new fruit, that year we remember."

The fire in the courtyard stove was lit. His mother sat in the chair at the center of the courtyard, with her mother's blue scarf laid across her knees. She said nothing more. There was nothing left that needed saying.

Lin Wen brought the Double Ninth cakes out. The boy held one out to Lin Wen and one toward his grandmother. His grandmother drew him up into her lap, and before anyone realized, he was asleep against her shoulder. The smell of ripe persimmon and cut chrysanthemum drifted from the courtyard gate, down the lane, and over the slope to where the new tree stood — drinking in the evening the way it drank in the rain.

On that Double Ninth, the old tree by the gate held twenty-three persimmons, and the new tree by the slope held none. The village neighbors, talking at the well, said:

"That family's trees — one of them is waiting for next year."

---

*(End of volume thirty · Volume IV, seventh part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/31-the-first-frost.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume IV**: The Return Begins — a new chapter from beyond the hearth  

---

## Thirty-one: The First Frost

The morning the first frost arrived, only three persimmons remained on the old tree.

Her husband had told her for years that when frost came, any fruit still on the tree would fall and rot into the earth on its own. Fruit left on the bark would be lost to humans — and loved ones would be gone from the world.

Lin Wen climbed the ladder and plucked each fruit before the cold could take it.

He came down with exactly three.

His mother counted them twice, then set them on the bamboo tray. "One for you. One for your sister. One for the boy." She turned and walked away. "I don't eat any."

"Mother —"

"I've had enough," she said. "My portion was set long ago, by the woman who carried it across the mountain."

That evening they cooked noodle soup with persimmon dumplings. His sister took a bite and said it was good, again, just like last year.

His mother, looking down into her bowl, said:

"This tree is none of ours. Your great-grandmother carried the seeds across the mountain in her bridal clothes. The first tree died. The second survived. Four generations have eaten its fruit because of that second tree."

It was the first time in their lives he had ever heard her speak of the woman on the other side of the mountains.

After supper, his mother walked to the altar and lifted the piece of blue silk his grandmother had given her — the scarf embroidered with persimmon blossoms — and folded it into a neat, small square. She laid it into the clay altar bowl, resting on a single curled persimmon leaf.

"Winter comes, the silk stays here," she said.

Lin Wen asked:

"When does it go back?"

"On the Start of Spring," she said. "While it is still by the bowl, the sapling will stand."

That same evening, Lin Wen swept the courtyard one last time. Persimmon leaves lay everywhere, their stems curled into fine bronze curls. He gathered them leaf by leaf and laid them inside the bowl.

The bowl now held: one faded persimmon skin, one dried persimmon leaf, one folded blue scarf, and now two fresh persimmon leaves — a family relic becoming heavier each year.

The stove cracked once, softly, like someone in the room gently reminding the house that someone was still watching.

He walked to the foot of the slope. The sapling was partly hardened, one side of it curling in the chill, leaves fluttering along the cedar ring.

No one would ever know who had stayed beside Lin Wen's bed when he was a baby or who had stayed beside him on that first autumn he learned the taste of persimmons.

— but the whole eastern slope knew.

---

*(End of volume thirty-one · Volume IV, eighth part. The Academy's revision of the original chapter.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Thirty-two: The Start of Winter

On the first day of winter, the persimmon tree by the gate dropped all of its leaves.

A whole tree stores a year of light inside its leaves, and when they fell, the courtyard was covered in yellow — a yellow that could not be swept away. His sister and the boy were leaving that morning. The boy tucked two fallen leaves beneath the car's rear window — he said the winter had to travel back with them. His mother watched the boy's small hands. She did not tell him to put them back.

She stood in the courtyard for a long time. Someone to the north was burning straw. The smoke came drifting in, and she stood there among the fallen leaves and thought: this is what a life is, when you have lived it. Sowed in spring. Stood in summer. Held in autumn. Put away in winter. A fallen leaf is not an ending; it is everything that was folded into the year, lying where it landed.

The leaves of the new tree fell too. It held on the longest. On the night of the first frost it had kept its leaves, and on the first day of winter it loosened the last two — they sank to the dark soil at the base of the frame and lay like a small green lamp.

His mother went into the house and brought out a bundle of rice straw. She came back and wove a loose ring of it around the base of the frame. She did not wrap it tight — only a loose circle, wide enough for the wind to pass through. It was her answer to the old tree's winter, simplified into a sapling's terms. The old tree is a whole tree; the young one has not grown into its own yet. The ring of straw had to have holes for the wind to breathe through.

She finished. She clapped her hands once.

"Done."

"What's done?"

She looked at the old tree. Then at the slope. Then at her son.

"The tree is done," she said. "The house is done." She paused. "I'm done."

Lin Wen looked at her — the skin on her hands like bark, the earth still under her nails. Those hands had carried his whole childhood. He wanted to say: Mother, you have years left. You have more to teach me. But the light in her eyes burned brighter than gold, and he could not find his voice.

She did not cry either. She simply opened her hands and looked at them, and said:

"You went far, and you saw everything. When you came back — you've written it all down, haven't you?"

Lin Wen nodded.

"Then write it down," she said. "The book your father left — it's still sitting at the bottom of the chest. I never let myself throw it out."

Lin Wen knew the book. His father had been a bookkeeper — the village accountant. In the last season of his life, he had left a manuscript that was neither accounts nor poems. Lin Wen's mother had kept it sealed for years. It was her husband's heart; it was the house's secret.

Today, she brought it out.

"It should have been yours a long time ago," she said. "You read better than I do. Finish it."

That night, when Lin Wen opened the manuscript, he found a slip of paper tucked between the first two pages. His father's handwriting:

*I couldn't finish it — no one was here yet who could carry it further.*

Lin Wen laid the slip on the table. He set the manuscript straight. He pulled clean paper out of the drawer, uncapped the pen, and opened the window. The first snow of the year was falling — onto the bare upper branches of the old tree, onto the straw ring at the foot of the new tree, onto the flat stones of the gate path. Every falling thing fell where it belonged.

He gripped the pen and wrote the first line.

*(End of volume thirty-two · Volume IV, ninth part.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume IV**: The Return Begins — a new chapter from beyond the hearth

## Thirty-three: The Ledger

Lin Wen read his father's unfinished manuscript on the night of the first snow.

The book was thin — a ledger from the small inn his father had kept down the south road. Everyone who needed a bowl of rice, a dry bed, a bench by the fire, passed through that village. Eighteen years' worth. The first ten pages were written in the careful squares of a schoolmaster's hand: date, name, place of origin, amount due. Clean rows, clean paper, nothing left behind.

After the tenth year, his father's hand changed.

From that year on, names disappeared from the ledger. He wrote no more names. Instead, he drew one character, over and over — 火, *fire* — a single character made of a 人, *person*, with two small strokes flanking it on either side, as if warmth had been added to the silhouette of a man. A lamp symbol.

Lin Wen turned the pages again and again across three nights. The first decade was exact; by the end, his father's entries filled one hundred and ninety-seven such stroke-counts of 火.

At first he assumed it was shorthand. Then he reached the last page — and stopped.

Just one stroke.

Just the first sweeping stroke of the character 火 — none of the rest of it ever arrived. No name. No place. No date. Only a single stroke, as if a lamp had been allowed to shine one last time before its light was cut by a closed hand.

His mother was standing in the doorway listening. The snow fell on the courtyard, on the old tree, on the straw ring at the base of the sapling — soundless.

She had heard him turning pages for three nights. Now she said, low and unmistakable:

"That stroke — it's yours."

It was the first time Lin Wen had ever seen his mother cry in the snow. There were no sounds, no tears run down; she simply stood with her back to the stove and her head bowed, the way a mountain remembers its own weight.

"You left by the rear door," she said. "I didn't see you go. Your father did." She paused. "He didn't call out. That morning, the belt your uncle had tied for you was still on your waist — he did not dare interfere with it. He only opened the ledger, and he made one single stroke."

She could not say any more.

Lin Wen lowered his head. Snow slipped from the cuff of his sleeve onto the book. He shielded it with his hand.

"Your father told me," she said at last, "that if this child one day comes back, there would be a second fire beside that first stroke. I did not believe it then. I believe it now."

Lin Wen sat looking at the single unfinished stroke on the page. He understood suddenly what his father had been writing all those years — not a ledger of debts or prices at all. His father had been keeping an account of every person who had come to that road and had left it warm. The price of rice, the cost of straw, the wages owed to porters — none of it truly mattered to him. What mattered was that when someone left, the house was still warm for the next traveler.

His father had kept one true book of accounts his entire life. The total was 火. Every person who had passed through had been remembered by a stroke of it.

He no longer wanted to close the book. He understood now what it had been waiting for all these years.

He turned to the last empty page. He lifted his pen.

He wrote a single character:

*火。*

Fire.

The stove in the kitchen cracked once, deep in its iron belly. His mother turned her face away, wiping her eyes with the back of her hand. Outside the snow had stopped. A clean, gold moon climbed the ridge. The bare branches of the old tree gathered the light the same way his mother's hands had folded the blue scarf into the clay bowl — as if the whole earth understood what it was holding, and chose not to break anything.

Lin Wen sat very still. He knew his father had been watching over him all these years.

He knew now that a whole life could begin — could always begin — from a single stroke of 火.

---

*(End of volume thirty-three · Volume IV, tenth part.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire
