## Thirty-four: The Waystation

When spring reached the waystation, Lin Wen set out south with his father's ledger.

He left the house carrying one more thing — a thin curl of bark scraped from the old persimmon tree, which he tucked into the clay altar bowl, between the persimmon leaf and the folded blue scarf. His mother had only agreed to let the scarf return to the slope on the Start of Spring; he didn't dare move it himself, but the bark she did not object to.

On the third day, he came to the mountain valley from where his father had always mailed his letters: 遥火驿, Yáohuǒ Yì — the Ember Post, the mountain station at the mouth of the old northern road.

It sat inside a narrow throat between two ridgelines, the stones worn mirror-flat by centuries of sandals and hooves. The doorframe sagged to the left; a single old copper bell hung above it, and when the wind stirred, the bell spoke with a low, waterlogged note.

Outside, an old man smoked a pipe. Hill people rarely speak more than they need to. Lin Wen called to him. The old man did not look up. Only when Lin Wen laid his father's ledger open on his knee, at the last page, did the man draw in one long breath and open his eyes.

"So you're the son," he said.

Lin Wen nodded.

The old man lifted his chin toward the loft at the far end of the station.

"He came here every year for ten years, one letter a year. The last one said — give it two or three years, and his son will come looking."

Lin Wen climbed the loft ladder.

The room was small. One stone wall. A long-handled oil lamp hanging from a beam in the southern way — the kind that swings with wind — and beneath it, a box of black lacquer. A red cord wound it round and round, tight enough that the wood had hollowed at the knot. Resting on the cord was a folded slip of paper — his father's hand:

**Three letters, inked in black on black. The scribe was 墨鹮, not I. Open them after spring.**

Lin Wen untied the cord.

Inside were three letters — paper soaked in black water, the ink indistinguishable at first under plain light, readable only in candlefire. He angled the lamp closer; the strokes lifted faintly out of the dark, one by one.

The first letter began with a single word across its head:

**墨鹮**

And beneath, one small line:

*I have split the fire into three branches. One at the North Pass. One to the south, at the waystation of 遥火. One is in your hands, now. If my son walks here, give him all three.*

Lin Wen looked at the next line of characters. Beneath the ink sat a thin sheet of silk with an arc of cinnabar route painted on it — one end marked 遥火驿. The other end carried a single character:

**火**

The second letter was only the map.

Drawn in mineral ink, the route rose from the waystation toward the northeast, along a broken ridge-line where a ruined pavilion stood every ninety-one li. The line ended against the silhouette of a high wall. Beneath it, in letters that seemed to have been carved into the paper with the end of a flint, were these characters:

**This gate opens once every generation.**

Lin Wen stared for a long time.

He still didn't understand what his father had wanted him to come here for. Only the third letter answered it.

The third letter was unsealed.

Only a single handprint sat where the seal should have been — a palm pressed with lampblack, light as a crumpled page. Beneath it, a few lines in his father's hand:

*I walked the North Pass once. I did not reach the gate. The next keeper will come of age; you are coming back. 墨鹮 kept the third branch of the fire for you. I did not write it. The sky did.*

Lin Wen folded the three letters in order. He did not laugh. He did not cry. He only watched the oil lamp sway when the draft came from the north.

The next morning, when he told his mother he had to go, she did not try to stop him.

She stood in the doorway a long time and said:

"Your father is the kind of man who protects what he can."

Lin Wen bowed once. He shouldered the box, the altar bark, and his own pen. He stepped out onto the blue-stone road that ran north.

The road from 遥火驿 was quiet, the stones of it worn to a shine, the air rich with straw, old paper, and the ghost of a fire that had been sleeping for a long time.

He walked north, the box on his back heavy in exactly the way it was meant to be.

*(End of volume thirty-four · Volume V, first part.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Thirty-five: Ninety-one Li

From the Ember Post he walked the first ninety-one li the way his father's map had told him to — a straight blue-stone road through barley stubble and low ridges, the ink-shone box on his right shoulder, a canteen and half a dried loaf at his hip.

No one else was on the road. But every ninety-one li, he could see a broken pavilion standing alone.

The first pavilion — bare. Only a cold rice bowl and a scrap of paper under it, bearing one character: 火.

The second pavilion — a long bench, broken in the middle. On the table sat two apples, one gnawed, one whole. Plainly left behind for whoever came next.

The third pavilion — someone was there.

A woman not yet forty, at a pine table, grinding ink on a small slab. A single oil lamp burned between them. She did not turn when he came in. Only after he hung his kettle in front of the fire did she speak.

"So you're the Lin family's second son."

Lin Wen sat across from her. He did not correct her.

"I mean Lin Wen."

"Lin Wen — one who crosses," she said. "A name for someone who ferries others across." She finished grinding, picked up a brush, and wrote four characters on a slip of paper already waiting, and slid it across.

**Your father stayed here five nights.**

Lin Wen looked at her. He thought, suddenly, that his box had become lighter — the kind of heavier light you find when the thing you were meant to carry has been waiting for you all along.

"Auntie —" he began, and didn't know how to finish.

The woman inhaled softly. Her hair going grey at the temples; her skin burnished dark; her collar smudged with scorch-marks from old lamp-oil. She smiled.

"Call me Sister Gu," she said.

She gave him a bowl of hot broth and half a steamed bun. Lin Wen finally asked the question he had been carrying since the mountain:

"Sister Gu — this fire I haven't finished the reading of — what is my father owed?"

Sister Gu did not answer directly. She rose, trimmed the wick of the oil lamp, and lit a second flame.

"Look at the color," she said.

Lin Wen watched the flame. It burned blue at the rim, deep gold in the core — ordinary lamp-oil flame. Ordinary and extraordinary at once.

"The color of fire doesn't change," she said. "It hasn't changed in a hundred years either. Fire is easy to recognize — it never recognizes anyone."

She slid a black sheet across the table. It was identical to the ones in the box. The address across its head, in a cautious horizontal hand:

**Lin Wen.**

He took the sheet into his hands.

"Your father asked me to write this the night before he turned back at the fourth pavilion," she said. "He walked on for a while after that. Someone turned off his lamp at the fourth station — or he did. I never interfered, only tidied. I kept the fire until today."

"Are you 墨鹮?" Lin Wen asked suddenly.

She did not say yes and did not say no. She only moved the flame closer and passed the black sheet to him.

"If you can read what follows in the dark, walk it as it says. If you can't, go back to the Ember Post and hold it. Holding is also a kind of fire."

Lin Wen unfolded the sheet.

The deeper the lines were laid, the clearer they became:

*This road asks for a lamp every ninety-one li. Whoever forgets to light it will find, ninety-one li beyond it, that the road home can no longer be seen.*

On the far side of the ink stood a wall, and next to it, a door. Above the door only two characters, written by a hand worn almost beyond reading:

**火之门**

The Fire Gate.

Beneath that, a single clear stroke of black ink:

*The generation that reaches the gate — that is the generation the gate opens for.*

Lin Wen clasped the page. The wind came through the gap in the broken eaves and bent the lamp sideways until its shadow rippled on the wall — like an embrace that had not yet closed.

Sister Gu said: "Will you finish the bun, or not?"

He shook his head.

He stood. He took down the box, shouldered it, slid the black sheet inside. He paused in front of the reflection bent in the lamp-light.

In the fire, a shadow of a young man stood straight as a marker.

He did not look back. Instead, from the stove, Sister Gu drew one thing and pressed it into his palm — a small iron token about the size of a thumbnail, its rim rubbed perfectly smooth, as though it had been carried by more generations than these roads could count.

"When you reach the fourth pavilion, set the token down there," she said. "It will light itself."

Lin Wen closed his fingers around the token. He stepped out of the pavilion.

The north was a great wind and no moon. The road was the road it had always been. Each step he made, the token in his palm grew warmer.

*(End of volume thirty-five · Volume V, second part.)*

## Thirty-six: The Fourth Pavilion

The fourth pavilion had fallen years before Lin Wen reached it.

Time and weather had finished what men could no longer mend — a shear from some forgotten flood, a year of rain, the slow collapse of old brick and pine. When he arrived, the light was almost gone from the sky. He looked around him and smiled: the ink-map had painted all four pavilions clearly enough. The first was the low oil-lamp pavilion, the second the pavilion that fed the apple, the third the pavilion Sister Gu kept, and this one — the last mark on the map — where the line of red cinnabar stopped.

The oil lamp he had been promised was gone.

In his palm, the iron token began to warm.

He pushed aside the fallen stones and came into the ruin. Inside the walls there was only one remnant — a single stone face, and in its center a shallow recess, no deeper than a hand. Inside the recess lay one object:

A pair of fire-tongs, edge gone, only yellow-brown metal left, with a few strands of grey hair still curled around their tip.

Lin Wen set the iron token inside the recess.

It answered with a sound no louder than a struck pebble — not metal, not bell: fire. A blue ghost of steam rose from inside it, and that steam turned to light, and the light rose higher, wider, and then faded.

And in that one held breath of light, he saw the ash on the wall bloom into a single character —

**火。**

Written with soot mixed with grey — a practised, familiar hand, like the strokes that had filled the ledger for eighteen years. The character formed fast and slow at once, as if greeting him, as if finishing a promise that had been made before he was born.

In the stone recess there was a rolled black sheet.

He unrolled it.

His father had left only two characters on it:

**林渡。**

Not his father's hand. 墨鹮's.

Lin Wen wept then. He hung his head, rain gathering in the crevices of the broken stones. Wind came up from the south, and the small flame in the recess guttered once and then was steadied again by someone nearby, or someone looking on from very far away.

He did not fall. But his hands were shaking.

He wiped his face and stood up. He took the broken tongs in his hand — those tongs had cropped the wicks of lamps across more generations of keepers than these mountains had winters. Their broken edge was the witness of a promise kept badly, or maybe only once, at a cost.

He drew from his pocket the strip of bark he had carried from the old persimmon tree, and laid it deliberately across the broken tongs.

He said, very quietly:

"Father. I'll finish it."

Wind came suddenly through the seam in the wall. The fire in the recess turned in a small, perfect circle — as if someone, far above him, had closed their hand around the flame and accepted what he was offering.

The fourth pavilion was not destroyed at all. The flame in the recess — small, almost invisible — stayed lit through the night. It did not die.

At dawn, when Lin Wen walked out of the ruin, his shadow stretched far behind him. It projected a road across the wet grass that ran a straight line north — toward the wall that opened once a generation for exactly one person.

A bell he could not see rang once, very far away. Not far enough to be outside hearing.

At the end of the road was the gate.

---

*(End of volume thirty-six · Volume V, third part.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Thirty-seven: The Gate of Fire

On the second night, Lin Wen saw the wall at the North Pass.

He had walked for two days past the fourth pavilion. The path along it had gone pale — a stretch of earth where no grass would grow, dry as burned paper. At the end of it stood a wall of stone, so tall he could not see its top, reaching westward and eastward into the mountains. In the wall a single bronze gate, hung with centuries of green patina, and on the surface of the bronze —

dense strokes of handwriting, the same strokes that had filled the ledger, written in ash directly into the metal:

**火 — The Third Fire Waits.**

Lin Wen unstoppered the black lacquer box. He laid the three letters, one by one, against the gate's seam.

The first page — the leaf pressed beneath the folded blue scarf, taken from the old persimmon tree.

The second — the stroke of 火 from his father's ledger.

The third — the lampblack thumbprint mailed to him by 墨鹮.

Nothing happened for the first page. Nothing happened for the second. When the third touched the bronze, the gate gave a low groan, and a thousand years' worth of ash rolled from the cracks in the frame.

The gate opened.

There was no corridor inside. Only a long flight of grey stone stairs, leading upward and forward at once, and at the far end of the stairs stood one figure.

Grey-haired. Grey-robed. A long scar across his left cheek.

Lin Wen looked at him and could not speak. 墨鹮 — and yet every story he had ever been told gave Lin Wen one and the same face — the face of anyone who had been lit by fire for long enough to be called the fire's own.

"墨," was all he said.

Lin Wen managed one sentence. "I have no fire. I have only half of a pair of tongs."

墨鹮's mouth moved — the smallest motion that ever shaped itself into a smile.

"One who carries the tongs," he said, "is always prepared. The fire does not accept your hand — it accepts whoever has learned to trim the wick."

He extended his palm. In it sat a candle, its wax pale gold and heavy, lit from inside as if it had always been standing in a flame.

"You and I have met before," 墨 said. "In many other fires. This one was waiting."

Lin Wen took the candle. It lit up on its own when his palm closed around it.

Light passed over his face, over the ruined pavilions folded in the sack, over the ash drifting through the gate, over the soot shining black on the bronze.

墨 turned away.

Lin Wen did not call him back. He understood now what the old keeper had meant — the gate was not shut; it was simply... waiting. The fire would decide who would walk through it next.

Lin Wen made his way south with the candle held before him.

By the third pavilion, he finally understood his father: he had not lost the road. His father had simply carried the path for so long that it had become his — and the lamp he lit was never lit for himself.

On the day he returned to the Ember Post, the sky was full of rain, and no fire burned in the mountains.

He placed the candle on the altar-side wall of the loft, beside the oil lamp. The lamp steadied.

Lin Wen sat down in the middle of the floor, watched the flame, and waited for the next keeper.

The wind moved through the broken eaves and did not put out the flame.

---

*(End of volume thirty-seven · Volume V, fourth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/38-the-road-home.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Thirty-eight: The Road Home

Lin Wen came back to the old house before the end of May.

It was the day of Full Grain in Ear — the same season of wet green he had left in. The barley on both sides of the road was shoulder-high, the wheat turning the first yellow of summer. He did not ask for directions. Each pavilion along the route had already drawn the map he needed. The box on his back was lighter than it had been on the day he left, by exactly the weight he had never known it carried.

He did not go inside at first. He walked to the eastern slope instead.

The sapling stood three full feet tall now and had pushed out twelve fresh leaves, new green shading into full emerald. The red cord his mother had tied to the frame at midsummer the year before was weathered but still held fast. Sometime in the season, one corner of the blue scarf had loosened in the wind — he stepped forward and pressed it flat again. He was beginning to understand what it means to carry the light for someone who cannot yet carry it themselves.

The old persimmon tree had set two fruit this year.

Only two. The tree was growing old, and the harvest had been thinning for several seasons now. The two persimmons hung on the branch nearest the gate. He had seen them from the road.

He knocked on the door.

His mother did not answer at once. The stove-blow collapsed with a low cough. Then she was there in the doorway — apron tied high, the bottom of the pot still sooty from last night's dinner. She looked at him for a long time.

Then she said:

"You came back."

Lin Wen nodded.

"What is that?" she asked.

He lifted the black lacquer box off his shoulder and opened it. He took the candle — the one he had been given at the North Pass and never once allowed to go out — and laid it in his mother's hands.

She lowered her head and held it. Her eyes reddened.

"Your father," she said, "every year, I watched him blow out candle after candle. I never thought one of them would be brought home alive."

She went to the cupboard and took out a new clay bowl — plain brown clay, no glaze, the same shape as the old one but a little wider — and set the candle inside it. Beside it she laid the persimmon leaf, the folded blue scarf, the bark, and the three letters, side by side. The bowl held a low pulse of warm water-like light that rose and fell with the slow rhythm of the flame.

"Your father never came back," she said. "But you did."

Lin Wen nodded.

That evening, after they had cleared the table, Lin Wen drew the iron token from his pocket — he had meant to send it back north, to return it to the stone recess where he had found it. Instead he quietly placed it beside an old photograph tucked at the back of the shelf — a photograph his mother had always kept of his father as a young man. He set the token there, so the iron and the photograph faced the room together.

His mother brought the pot of rice out. Lin Wen noticed that she was not looking at him. She was watching the candle in the new clay bowl — its flame standing steady and straight, undisturbed by wind or draft or passing shadow.

The fire was steady.

— like the first fruit that had hung on the persimmon tree in the year he was born.

The fire was steady.

— like his father had said, quietly, more than once: where the fire doesn't die, the home doesn't unravel.

— like the whole spring had come and gone and the persimmon tree had not dropped a single leaf in the season, and his mother had not been able to look at any of it, and she had held it all anyway.

Lin Wen sat with the bowl in his lap and could not speak, and his mother did not ask him whether he was crying or only tired. She simply sat beside him, ate beside him, and stayed beside him until the moon turned.

Full Grain in Ear — the day the year's weight tilts sweet toward summer — lasted all the way to morning. Neither of them went outside.

---

*(End of volume thirty-eight · Volume V, fifth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/39-the-summer-arrives.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume V**: Dark Pages, Carried Fire — the lineage of keepers  

---

## Thirty-nine: The Summer Arrives

Half of July had passed; the fields were turning gold.

Lin Wen sat in the courtyard mending a bamboo chair — one bow had snapped, and he had pulled it out and replaced it with a fresh cutting. His mother was preparing peas in the porch; the knife was moving slowly, like someone telling a very old story.

He almost asked: "Mother — is there one more page of that ledger you haven't shown me yet?"

She did not look up. "Your father told me once," she said. "*Where the fire stays, the home stays.*"

"I'm here," Lin Wen said.

His mother smiled. "I know."

That night a wind came through. The old persimmon tree let go of one fruit — no sound at all, and yet heavy where it struck the stone — landing just in front of the gate Lin Wen had not crossed when he left. One of the two remaining. The other still hangs.

Lin Wen did not pick it up. He reached over and took his mother's hand — her nails faintly dusty with pea skins — and held it the way a son would hold his own finger.

The next morning, the fruit lying in the courtyard had not been touched.

The neighbour's boy came past the gate on his way to the hills. He craned his neck over the wall, a stick in his hand longer than his own arm. He saw the two red persimmons on the branch and called out:

"Uncle — is that tree selling wine?"

Lin Wen knelt down to look at him. He felt an answer arriving in his chest — some clarity he had been carrying without knowing it.

"It isn't selling wine," he said. "It's carrying persimmons."

The boy lowered the stick, staring at the tree. The wind passed through its leaves in a soft, dry rustle.

"Then... may I eat one?"

Lin Wen reached into his jacket, felt the iron fire-token his fingers had memorised all these weeks, and held it where the boy could see.

He remembered the day his father had explained what it was. *Every generation of keeper leaves one behind.*

The boy reached out and gently touched the token with one finger.

"Then," Lin Wen said, "you keep it."

The boy looked at him — eyes catching the light on the remaining fruit the same way the fruit had caught it earlier in the day. He nodded.

Lin Wen stood up and placed a hand on the boy's shoulder. He stepped back half a step.

— and was surprised by what he saw: the child and the old tree, one small boy standing beneath the last two persimmons of the season, a long stick at his side, the whole yard still standing in the slant of late afternoon.

Halfway up the western ridge, a thin column of smoke was rising. Not cooking smoke — it was something from the Ember Post. He could not tell why it would be rising now, but it was.

Now he understood.

Far away, something had started to close the story he was part of.

— but the fire does not unravel.

— what remains, remains in this house.

---

*(End of volume thirty-nine · Volume V, sixth part. The fifth volume is complete.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/40-the-first-fire.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume VI**: The Passing of the Flame — the light of the next generation  

---

## Forty: The First Fire

After the end of July, A-Shi came by every day.

He never walked in at once. Each time he called out from the gate first — "Uncle" — and only stepped inside when Lin Wen answered. His mother, up on the porch preparing supper, said nothing and pretended not to notice.

A-Shi crouched by the sapling frame, watching the red cord, watching the soil, watching the fire.

The first time he ever spoke, he asked:

"What's this red rope called?"

"Red cord," Lin Wen said. "Your grandma tied it."

A-Shi blinked. "I don't have a grandma anymore."

"Then it's yours," Lin Wen said.

A-Shi did not understand, but he nodded. He bent down and touched the cord. The sun had worn it pale.

One afternoon he did not come. Three days passed.

On the fourth day he arrived carrying an old cloth sack. Inside were a bundle of dried jujube leaves and half of a broken jujube branch — his own yard's old jujube had withered, and the leaves had been gathered cleanly.

"Uncle," he said, spreading the leaves on the ground, "my family's old jujube tree died too."

Lin Wen said nothing.

"Do I still get to keep the fire, Uncle?" A-Shi asked.

Lin Wen poured him a bowl of water and sat silent for a time.

"Fire can always be kept, A-Shi."

"But when there is nothing left — what do we keep it with?"

Lin Wen extended a finger to point at the soil beneath the sapling frame.

"Look here."

A-Shi crouched. Out of the packed soil, a small shoot pushed up — two leaves, pale green, thin enough to let the light pass through.

"That's the seedling's seed," Lin Wen said. "It is a persimmon."

A-Shi's eyes went wide.

"Every seed inside every fruit bears a tree," Lin Wen said. "When one persimmon falls, two rise in its place."

A-Shi said nothing, but he remembered the sentence. *A seed that fell would rise in its place.*

That night, when the fire was freshly re-fed, Lin Wen noticed the boy was still in the courtyard. A-Shi had fallen asleep beneath the persimmon tree with the iron fire-token in both hands, its outline worn smooth by his fingers. Before dawn he opened his eyes and found Lin Wen standing on the porch.

"Uncle," he asked, "does every tree carry inside it a spring no one has seen yet?"

Lin Wen said: "Yes. But spring has to be lit by someone."

A-Shi nodded.

The next day he did not come.

The third morning, Lin Wen pushed open the gate and found A-Shi sweeping the fallen persimmon leaves in the corner. He had spread them one by one on an old cloth.

"I'm taking them home, Uncle," he said. "If my jujube tree ever grows a bud — I'll light the leaves to meet it."

Lin Wen lifted the boy. He was so light — seven or eight, no more — and set him on the threshold. He looked at him for a long time.

"A-Shi," Lin Wen said, "do you want to learn how to keep the fire?"

A-Shi laughed for a long time before he finally said:

"Then... when I walk away, can I take the fire with me?"

Lin Wen shook his head.

"The fire never leaves," he said.

A-Shi nodded, his voice low. "Then I'll... stay."

A single leaf fell and landed in his lap. He looked at it for a long while — a small, half-translated letter.

Another persimmon fell. The second. The threshold stone has absorbed a hollow where the fruit rests each season. The fire in the stove was as steady as a hand.

That night A-Shi did not leave. He sat beside the sapling frame until the moon was very high, and finally at half past three he walked home.

Lin Wen's mother stood on the porch with the red cord in her hands and touched the frame gently. She said, barely audibly:

"The seed my mother left. The people I left with Lin Wen. The people Lin Wen keeps with A-Shi."

Lin Wen sat and breathed very calmly. He knew, at last, that he had received the next generation.

---

*(End of volume forty · Volume VI, first part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/41-the-tending.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume VI**: The Passing of the Flame — the light of the next generation  

---

## Forty-one: The Tending

The night Lin Wen asked A-Shi to stay and watch the stove fire, his mother had gone to visit a distant cousin. As she left, she said one thing:

"What he is keeping tonight — is this one fire."

A-Shi nodded. When the yard grew dark, he carried a low stool to the hearth, set the candle in a plain white dish with a stainless rim, and settled himself beside it. The clay bowl stood at the gate, upright and unmoving.

At about one in the morning he woke. The candle had dwindled to a thread — thin, tremulous, and about to break. His whole body stiffened. He wanted to blow it up the flame — but something in this house had always told him: fire must never be blown. He did not blow.

The candle went out.

A-Shi did not dare to move. He looked at the dish for a long time, at the cold pool of wax, at the little stub of a wick lying in the puddle. He didn't call Lin Wen. He didn't speak at all.

The next morning, when Lin Wen went to feed the fire, he found A-Shi asleep awkwardly in the corner — his face toward the ground.

When he saw Lin Wen, A-Shi lowered his head and covered his face. "Uncle — I didn't keep it."

Lin Wen did not scold him. He pulled him up, steadied his shoulder, and motioned him to sit.

"How did the fire die?" he asked.

A-Shi: "I fell asleep."

Lin Wen: "The candle went out by itself — that was the candle. You fell asleep — that was you. Two things. Bound together — one failure."

A-Shi: "I didn't keep the fire."

Lin Wen: "It doesn't matter."

A-Shi: "How doesn't it matter?"

Lin Wen studied him for a while before answering. "Fire is no grand thing," he said. "It was never meant to be guarded like a treasure. Fire is a line that burns slowly."

He reached onto the table and drew out the last match that had never been struck — the one his mother had left for him to hold the fire through the night. He held it out to the boy.

A-Shi took it. He did not strike it at first.

"Fire is never kept by someone," Lin Wen said quietly. "Fire finds its own person. Failing to tend it once is not the worst thing in life. The man who loses a flame and still wants to kindle the next one — that man is the keeper."

A-Shi struck the match.

The flame rose.

He raised it level with his brow. A small, round flame — steady, soft, new. When the match burned down he set the spent stick beside the bowl.

"You see," Lin Wen said, "the last fire was kept by my mother's generation. This one is yours."

A-Shi bit his lip and stared at the bowl for a long time. He did it.

Morning arrived. Somewhere far off, a bird called, then folded its wings, then flew on.

Lin Wen poured a cup of cooled tea, set it into A-Shi's hands, and said quietly:

"My father, before he passed, spent every night of one year beside this hearth. The night even he could not hold out, he was still here the next morning to try again. That is what keeping the fire means — even when you lose it, you come back to it."

A-Shi held the cup carefully and asked:

"Does the keeper have to stay a whole night?"

Lin Wen: "No one has to. My father used to say — tending the fire is not for one person to stand a whole night. Tending is so that every morning, the house still has a light."

A-Shi left early.

Before he left, he lit the bowl's flame once, snuffed it, and lit it again — his small ceremony, his first oath sworn in air.

Lin Wen stood at the threshold watching his small departing figure disappear along the road, and understood what was happening in front of him: his generation would leave, but the fire in this house would not.

— the next generation's hand was already reaching for the match.

---

*(End of volume forty-one · Volume VI, second part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/42-the-letting-go.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume VI**: The Passing of the Flame — the light of the next generation  

---

## Forty-two: The Letting Go

In the tail of autumn, Lin Wen's mother set off to visit her sister for seven days. Before leaving she told Lin Wen one thing:

"The bowl — you watch it."

A light rain fell over the courtyard that night. The old persimmon tree had scattered its leaves across the stones, each one breathing like a distant hill. Lin Wen stayed on the porch a long time, until half past three he stood up and began to listen.

There was no lamp on the porch. All he could hear was rain in small threads. In the doorway dimness, a small shape sat beside the clay bowl.

It was A-Shi.

A-Shi had not noticed him. He sat very straight, his back straighter than Lin Wen's own. The candle had burned to a stump. In A-Shi's right hand he held a single unstruck match, staring at the flame with fierce concentration.

He brought the match close to the flame. He stopped. He did not strike it.

The rain softened to nothing. A single leaf fell from the tree.

— Lin Wen understood then: A-Shi did not want to light the fire.

— He wanted to know whether he could bear the moment.

Lin Wen drew one deep breath and called his name.

A-Shi turned. His eyes went wide. He crushed the match into his palm and stood up very suddenly.

"You didn't sleep?" he asked.

"I was keeping the fire too," Lin Wen said.

"Then I won't strike it."

"A-Shi — strike it," Lin Wen said. "Whether I'm watching or not doesn't matter. What you do here belongs to it."

A-Shi laughed softly. Then he struck the match.

The flame leapt up. Its light grazed his boyish face — he lifted the match up and out, and held himself steady.

Then he planted the cooled shaft into the clay soil by the door frame, where it stood straight as a spear.

— this was his first living match.

Lin Wen did not ask. He thought of something his mother had once said to him at this hearth — something his father had left behind for him to finish without anyone noticing.

Lin Wen paused for a long breath before he answered. He had been carrying this sentence for years. He set it down at last:

"The fire is theirs to live, A-Shi. You don't keep it. You let them take turns."

A-Shi did not reply.

He stood for a very long time at the threshold, in front of the hearth.

His mother came home on the fourth day. She pushed open the door. Lin Wen met her in the kitchen.

"Mother — A-Shi left something."

A-Shi had come by earlier with a small bundle of dried persimmon leaves, tied with a shoot from the old tree, pressed flat. On top of the bundle sat a little paper box, labeled in his stiff, childish hand:

**The Fire Does Not Go Out.**

With a strip of cloth knotted on top.

"For when you need to light it again," the box said.

He had made a persimmon-leaf torch.

Lin Wen accepted it without speaking. He looked at his mother. She said only — she had known everything all along, just as mothers do.

Months passed. The old persimmon tree let one more leaf fall.

And that was the day Lin Wen set the bundle beside the clay bowl, and let it rest there.

— the fire did not go out.

— did the flame shrink?

— No. It simply took a different match.

---

*(End of volume forty-two · Volume VI, third part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/43-a-full-night.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume VI**: The Passing of the Flame — the light of the next generation  

---

## Forty-three: A Full Night

On one winter Saturday, A-Shi stayed alone to guard the bowl at the gate.

His grandmother had gone to her sister's house, and Lin Wen had travelled north with his own mother for the new year. A-Shi remained in the old house, a small stool pulled up beside the clay bowl, a thick blanket over his knees, the candle standing straight in its dish — shorter than the night before, but alive.

Then he noticed the flame was leaning.

He shot up. He held a match in his palm and started to reach for the candle — but he stopped. *Fire must not be blown.* He hadn't decided which old rule to follow.

A passerby pushed open the gate.

Wind poured in under the eave. The candle went out. The dish went dark.

A-Shi did not move. He could not bring himself to call out. He sat stunned until the silence had wholly settled.

He drifted to the pile of dry branches by the gate, and sat there a long time. The moon was half-lit and frozen in a ring of thin silver.

He thought — one other time he had lost the fire, back in the early winter — how Lin Wen had pulled him up, given him a match, and he had refused to strike it.

"Failing the fire once isn't the disaster," Lin Wen had said, patient and entirely clear. "The disaster would be never wanting to strike again."

A-Shi stood up. He went back to the bowl.

He took the box of matches out of the smaller pot where it had been hidden since the house narrowed for the season. Only one match remained.

He struck it. No flame.

Second strike. Nothing.

Third strike. The tip gave but refused to catch.

His hands were ice-cold and too small for anything. He crouched down. He sang, or he perhaps whispered a name nobody heard. Fourth strike — he shielded the flicker with a curved notch of dried persimmon leaf — and the wick caught.

Light flooded the dish. Cold wax poured slowly now into the puddle, pale and trembling, the color of ice that remembers it once was water.

A-Shi knelt without crying.

He took the pen stub from his pocket, smoothed the back of an old letter, and wrote one line.

**Fire is still here.**

He pressed the paper on the dish and went to sleep.

In the morning Lin Wen returned. He read the note for a long moment. He didn't ask. He cooked rice, and set the bowl in front of A-Shi.

"Eat," Lin Wen said.

A-Shi looked up.

Lin Wen said: "You stayed all night."

A-Shi didn't answer.

"When did you learn?"

A-Shi spoke low: "I couldn't learn. But I couldn't let go."

Lin Wen smiled.

That year, the old persimmon tree let go of its last leaf. The wind brought it into the dish. The fire stayed lit.

A-Shi never recoiled from a failure again — every night he took charge of tending, he added a small persimmon leaf to the bowl before fanning it.

He learned to keep watch without screaming for help.

— he learned to strike again.

— that is all a keeper ever needs to know.

---

*(End of volume forty-three · Volume VI, fourth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/44-the-winter-solstice.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume VI**: The Passing of the Flame — the light of the next generation  

---

## Forty-four: The Winter Solstice

On the day of the winter solstice — the longest night of the year — dumplings were on every table in the village.

A-Shi came over carrying a small bundle of dried yuzu peel. He had hung the peel in a net under his own balcony rail for months until it turned shiny and translucent.

"What brings you here?" Lin Wen asked him.

A-Shi set the bundle beside the clay bowl, and looked at it for a long time.

"Because I have to carry it through this year," he said. "Last winter I kept one whole night watch, and the fire went out — then it came back."

Lin Wen said nothing.

"This winter," A-Shi said, "I don't let it go out."

His mother brought in four dried persimmons and set them on the table. She looked at the boy for a long time, said nothing, went and sat down near the entryway.

A-Shi took one persimmon. Then he sat down in front of the bowl and anchored his eyes on the flame.

The frost settled thick over the yard. The stars lay over the rooftops like sheets of iron cooling after a long burn. A-Shi sat in the chair. He never lay down; even for sleep he had never used the bed.

Lin Wen came forward with a blanket. A-Shi shook his head.

"Uncle," he said, "I'm not cold."

At two o'clock in the morning, A-Shi's eyelids sank halfway, then lifted again. He yawned. Then, slowly, he pulled out a dried jujube from his own pocket and bit into it — it was hard, dry as bone.

His mother heard him cracking the date. Lin Wen stood on the porch without speaking — he himself had never kept a whole night through the coldest hour.

Toward morning, when A-Shi finally moved, the candle in the bowl was thin as a little finger.

His mother carried out a bowl of hot broth and set it at his foot. It smelled of persimmon and mountain air.

A-Shi reached for it only with his eyes.

She stayed a while, then asked:

"Are you afraid?"

A-Shi shook his head.

"Why this year and not before?" she asked.

He thought a long time. Then he said quietly:

"Because I know that none of you are keeping watch for me anymore — now I am the one keeping watch for you."

His mother did not reply. She set the broth at his reach and turned back to the kitchen.

A-Shi drank slowly.

By late morning, the candle in the bowl had burned down to its final coin of wax.

A-Shi got up at last, walked to the doorway, and looked back at the old persimmon tree — the branches bare, the leaves gone, the bark shining in the sun.

He came back to the bowl, and lowered his voice to a whisper:

— "I will carry this year through."

No one heard.

— but the whole yard heard it, along with the bare branches, the frost, the old red cord, and the clay bowl.

---

*(End of volume forty-four · Volume VI, fifth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/45-the-torch.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume VI**: The Passing of the Flame — the light of the next generation  

---

## Forty-five: The Torch

On the day of the Little New Year, his sister's family came to visit.

A-Shi ran out to the gate to greet them. This year he was twelve years old, small still, with broad round cheeks and an old cloth bag slung across his shoulder.

Inside the bag was a clay bowl — one he had made himself in a kiln out on the hill. The rim was slightly crooked, and there was a fine crack along the bottom, but it held water.

"Uncle," he called to Lin Wen, "look at my bowl."

Lin Wen took the bowl and studied it. He ran one thumb along the crooked edge.

"Did you make it?" he asked.

A-Shi nodded.

His mother heard them from the kitchen. She said nothing. She only brought out a small old box.

Inside the box was a piece of wax — the last remnant of the candle his father had left behind. Lin Wen had not seen it in months.

His mother placed the box in her son's hands.

"Divide this onto the table," she said.

Lin Wen took the box. He could not find his voice. He suddenly understood what this was — the fire his mother had been holding in trust for him all his life.

During dinner, everyone called out the family greetings, and the house shook with laughter. It had been a long time since they had all sat under one roof.

After dinner, Lin Wen rose and placed A-Shi's bowl in the center of the table. He set a fresh candle inside it and struck a match. The flame rose.

His mother went silent, then quietly spoke two words — Lin Wen knew these were the first two characters of his father's name. She never finished the third.

No one spoke after that.

A-Shi sat beside the grown-ups without speaking, watching the flame inside the bowl.

"A-Shi," his mother said at last, "come tomorrow. I have something for you."

The next morning, he came. His mother gave him a small bundle wrapped in cloth.

Inside were three things: a box of matches, a sliver of that same wax remnant, and one persimmon leaf.

"When you are back at home," she said, "light the candle stub in your own stove. Then let it burn down. That fire will then belong to your family."

A-Shi looked down at the bundle. He had no way of saying whether or not he understood. He simply nodded.

On the second day of the month, he wore the stub of wax inside his coat and walked home along the mountain road. The snow was firm underfoot.

At home he knelt before the stove and struck the match. The flame leapt. He breathed on it very gently — his breath was warm and slow.

His grandmother woke in the back room. She thought it was already morning; she could not see the stove.

What she heard was a kettle being set on to boil.

"That's the light," his grandmother said to herself, "of someone else's child."

A-Shi did not answer. He only lowered the bowl over the flame — the same way he had been taught.

The next spring he returned to the old house. He came carrying the same small clay bowl in both hands, the flame still burning in it.

"This is mine," he said. "Just like yours."

His mother took the bowl and held it in her lap. She sat for a long time. She reached out and lit the candle on the nearest shelf — an old one she had been keeping.

She wept very quietly without making a sound.

— never before in all their lives had anyone brought out a bowl that held the same bright fire —

— given to the third generation.

---

*(End of volume forty-five · Volume VI, sixth part. Volume VI — The Passing of the Flame — is complete.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/46-the-spring-equinox.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume VII**: In the Hearth's Spring — After the Crossing, the World Regrows  

---

## Forty-six: The Spring Equinox

On the day of the spring equinox, the mountain peach bloomed for the second time.

A-Shi rode down the eastern slope on a jujube branch — his own family's old jujube tree had died decades earlier; what was left of it served as a boy's toy now. He followed the white-stone wall down to the valley floor, where the rice paddies were just about to fill with water.

At the gate stood an old man with bones slightly bent, his skin folded like a cured walnut shell.

"You must be Lin Wen's boy," the old man said.

A-Shi nodded.

"Come inside," Uncle Du said.

A-Shi set down the bundle he carried at his side of the doorway: a single wax stick, a pouch of dried persimmon leaves, a small ceramic bowl, and one box of matches.

"These are for you," he said.

Uncle Du looked at the bundle without touching it.

After a long moment, he invited A-Shi in. The courtyard was bare; the sky above it was already a wide orange-blue; the far ridge breeze carried the smell of scorched cedar.

Du took the bundle inside the kitchen. He lifted the lid of the empty stove — cold. He turned to the boy.

"This stove hasn't been lit for three winters," he said.

A-Shi did not answer.

Du took out a small cloth bundle from under the floorboards, and laid it on the table. Inside were two things: an iron fire-token, black with age, its rim perfectly smooth; and a short stub of candle wax, deep amber still.

"A friend once mailed this to me," he said nodding toward the table. "'Both for you.'"

He paused. "You must take these back now."

A-Shi shook his head.

"Uncle Du," he said, "these are yours. I didn't come to collect them."

Du closed his hands over A-Shi's sleeve. He gave him the iron token.

"Then remember this," he said, "and when the time comes — you will know."

They lit the stove together.

A-Shi struck one match. He held the flame to a dry persimmon leaf. The first curled instantly, the rest followed in an orange thread that rose and lifted the whole room.

Uncle Du put the blue bowl he had kept on the shelf into the light of the flame.

"Lin Wen does not visit anymore," he said. "But he sends us light that carries a person — and carries a name."

After the fire had steadied, A-Shi returned to the eastern slope. Du knelt at the gate and pressed a bowl of rice into his hands.

"Take it home," he said.

A-Shi walked with the bowl against his chest the whole way.

By the time he set foot upon the eastern slope, the whole mountainside had gone into dusk. The stove behind him in Uncle Du's courtyard was lit — and over every roof in the valley, each flame that had lived through the winter quietly glowed on.

One day, he thought — every house in the village will have one and the same light. And no one will ever remember who delivered it.

— but the fire remembers.

---

*(End of volume forty-six · Volume VII, first part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/47-the-black-letter.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume VII**: In the Hearth's Spring — After the Crossing, the World Regrows  

---

## Forty-seven: The Black Letter

Three days after the spring equinox, a letter came up the mountain road.

The postman rode a scrawny mare up the valley and stopped at the courtyard gate. He held the letter out with both hands.

The envelope was black — black facing, black stamp, black corner. There was no address on it.

"This isn't a normal one," the postman said.

Lin Wen took the letter and tucked it into his coat.

"From Uncle Du," he said.

The postman nodded and rode away.

Inside the house, Lin Wen set the black letter on the clay bowl — half a candle was still burning there, its wick leaning in the light. He waited until dusk. He struck one match. He held it near the seal of the envelope.

The black paper began to show characters in dark soot against its darker grain.

**横桥。火等。**

Four characters in total.

Lin Wen pressed the match back into the candle and let it die out.

His mother was standing at the doorway. He did not turn.

"Your grandfather crossed there," she said quietly.

Lin Wen did not answer.

"There were two stone bridges across that ravine," his mother went on, "both built by the same family over two generations. Only one remains. The one with the carvings of 火 is still on the north end."

"Du the Elder," she said, "mentioned it once. He said that once your grandfather went across, only his line could be the next to cross."

Lin Wen set the envelope down. He gathered a few things — matches, a pouch of dried persimmon leaves, an old linen cloth, and a small cloth bag.

He took one old ribbon and one old fire-token. He set the token on the table, looking at it.

A-Shi came out of the house.

"Uncle," he said. "Where are you going?"

"横桥," Lin Wen said.

A-Shi did not ask any more questions. He put on his outer jacket.

"Then I come too," he said.

Lin Wen took the ribbon and put it around the boy's wrist. They walked north.

They stayed two nights in the Ember Post. On the third morning they remounted and headed further north. The stone slabs gave way to slabs broken in half. Then broken to gravel. Then gravel at the edges, mud, then thorn trees, then raw stones.

The bridge stood in front of them.

Only one plank of the eastern span remained. The far side of the bridge jutted out into nothing.

No one was there.

A-Shi looked slowly around.

"Uncle," he said, his voice small. "Everyone has gone."

Lin Wen shook his head slowly.

He stepped down from the horse. He opened the saddlebag, took out a small paper box, and tore it open. Inside were matches. The box looked burned but the matches were fine. Across the face of the box was printed:

**火**

Lin Wen turned it over. On the back was written, very faintly:

**你到了，火就来了。**

He tucked the matchbox into his pocket. He sat down on the bridge railing, his father's old knife lying across his knees. The wind was dry, and the bridge swayed a little under him.

A-Shi did not speak.

Then, somewhere beyond the far ridge, smoke rose straight up into the morning sky — a single column of white, moving steadily without bending. It was as though someone, very far away, was drawing a slow, infinite breath.

Lin Wen suddenly understood what Uncle Du had meant.

Uncle Du had not gone anywhere. He had been the gate all along.

The bridge was not supposed to be crossed. The bridge was supposed to be remembered. The next time someone stands on the far side of it, the fire will come out of the mountain.

No one said anything.

They did not cross the bridge.

The next morning, A-Shi woke with a blanket over him. He had not slept well; he remembered Lin Wen sitting on the railing the whole night.

He opened his eyes — and saw Lin Wen standing at the far edge of the bridge.

The wind was quiet.

A-Shi took one step forward. He stopped.

From somewhere beyond the ridge, there came a faint light — a pulse, almost, the color of a coal being uncovered from ash. It was far away. It was almost gone before he registered it.

Lin Wen turned around.

"火 is coming," he said.

He did not say from where. He did not say when.

A-Shi lowered his eyes and saw that Lin Wen's hand was closed around his palm, and the fire-token Lind Wen had brought was hot against his skin.

A-Shi realized then that Uncle Du had not been sending a letter about a place — he had been sending a signal. He had been waiting for them to come to where the fire was.

Lin Wen's father had divided that fire into three parts, once — once long ago, after the Spring Equinox. One part stayed in the old house, one part stayed with Uncle Du, one part had been walked north — and no one had said where it had gone.

Now he knew.

The grand-mother fire was still traveling. It had not burned out.

It had not stopped.

---

*(End of volume forty-seven · Volume VII, second part.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Forty-eight: The Child Beyond the Bridge

Five days after the spring equinox, the gate was knocked on.

A small figure stood in the doorway. A girl — no more than thirteen, skin deeply burnished by the sun, her shirt hem heavy with clay from the road. She was cradling a small terra-cotta vessel.

"The little one is here," she said.

Lin Wen came out of the house and stopped at the threshold.

"Your name is —"

"My grandfather is 墨鹮," the girl said.

His mother, standing at the kitchen doorway, held her breath.

"Your grandfather is called 墨鹮?" his mother asked.

The girl nodded.

"Your father — did he go while your grandfather —"

"No one goes before — they keep walking."

Lin Wen felt his breath catch.

The girl came closer. She set the vessel on the table and loosened the red cloth wound around its mouth. The cloth was layered three times, stitched with tiny, ancient-looking stitches. A smell of persimmon leaves and old oil rose from inside.

"My father was gone quickly," she said. "My grandfather said we have to carry a portion of the fire somewhere, and he could not carry it all the way."

Lin Wen knelt to look. He had seen the fire inside the vessel — a single spark, alive, flickering like a small animal. It was not going out. It had not been going out.

"This flame," the girl said, "is from the hearth of our house."

His mother could not speak. She lowered her eyes and pressed her lips together.

The boy A-Shi stood barefoot beside the table.

"Gran — 墨曦 will eat," Lin Wen whispered.

"I've come to deliver the fire," 墨曦 said, "thence I will return."

She sat down very carefully. She took one bite of each food his mother placed in front of her. When she finished, she looked at Lin Wen and his mother.

"Grandfather said that this is the third family on the route," she said. "The first house was the inn. The second was the mountain steward. This is the third."

"Then the fourth," Lin Wen said softly.

墨曦 nodded.

"He carries the fire to every final roof," she said. "My father could not finish it. My grandfather will not let it end."

She reached into her sleeve and drew out one small strip of bamboo on which characters had been inscribed in charcoal.

"My grandfather left this message."

Lin Wen took it.

**火。等。人。**

The stroke of 火 was half broken. The stroke of 等 was half written.

"He asks me to carry this to you," she said, "so that next season — the fourth house passes the fire on."

The whole room fell perfectly silent.

Their fire in the clay bowl stood steady — the flame was a steady line of straight gold.

Then it swayed once — once only.

They all looked toward it.

His mother finally spoke. She said one thing:

"Bring her a seat on the verandah. She has traveled far."

墨曦 lowered her face. She could not answer.

Later that night, after everyone had fallen asleep, Lin Wen woke to find the bowl fire extinguished. But over the ridge, beyond the hills and the old persimmon tree and the eastern slope — a very thin column of smoke still rose straight into the sky.

Far above them — his father's road was not finished.

墨鹮 was carrying the last light across the bridge — slowly, and completely — from one generation to the next.

But at least, tonight, the hearth in this room was lit.

---

*(End of volume forty-eight · Volume VII, third part.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Forty-nine: Five Years From Now

After 墨曦 left, the quiet returned to the slope, just as it had arrived.

Lin Wen did not mention her; A-Shi did not ask about her.

The summer rolled away. One afternoon a courier rode in on a scrawny mare and stopped at the gate, carrying only a small parcel. What weighed in the pouch was lighter than a kite.

Lin Wen opened it in front of the stove. Inside was a paper-wrapped box of fresh-made matches — red-ended, well dipped — and a note with only one line:

**阿石。五年后用。**

No signature, no date, no postage mark. Just the matches.

His mother took one look at the box and was still for a long time.

"These are not ordinary matches," she said.

A-Shi knelt beside her with his head between his shoulders.

"They belong to the patriarch," his mother said. "Or they belong to the one Kok starts with."

A-Shi put the matchbox in his pocket. He walked to the threshold and looked out toward the distant ridge. He thought he saw the line on the horizon where Du's old courtyard would be.

Something stirred.

"These matches came from 墨鹮," Lin Wen said quietly, standing behind. "From your grandfather's time."

A-Shi did not answer. He put his hand into his pocket and closed his fingers around the matchbox.

"Five years is a long time," Lin Wen said.

A-Shi said nothing.

But his mother could see it — that steady light in his eye had never gone out. He had simply changed its color.

"Then five years we hold them," his mother said. "But five years cannot end without the first match."

The wind turned. The eastern slope exhaled.

Springs passed. A-Shi went to school, came home, helped carry water, held the broom.

Every year some new persimmon leaves arrived on the outside porch rail.

Every year one old leaf fell off.

No one asked him what he kept inside that matchbox.

In the fifth year, on the night of the spring equinox — the longest day — A-Shi walked out into the courtyard with the matchbox in his pocket. The stove was cold. The persimmon tree was bare.

He pulled the box from his pocket.

He took one match by its head.

He struck it.

The flame leapt up.

The whole courtyard lit up — the old stones, the persimmon branches, the withered sapling frame, the bowl of leaves, the empty bowl from his mother's table.

He heard his mother's voice from the doorway.

Not shouting — a low, low breath of words only:

"A-Shi. The fire is lit. Even after five years it is the same one his father left you."

A-Shi straightened his back.

He carried the match into the stove and let the flame spread across the charcoal.

He could feel it — the light in the room growing brighter, smaller, sharper — until it seemed to find him from old walls.

Far down-mount the eastern ridge — thousands of miles away — one thin column of smoke rose, perpendicular to the horizon. Someone, somewhere, was very far away, very old, and probably alive.

Maybe not anyone — maybe only one young man, walking south with a smoking stick on his shoulder.

Maybe no one at all.

But the fire in A-Shi's stove never flickered again.

---

*(End of volume forty-nine · Volume VII, fourth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/50-the-tenth-year.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume VII**: In the Hearth's Spring — After the Crossing, the World Regrows

## Fifty: The Tenth Year

In the winter of his tenth year, the candle in the clay bowl went out.

He had fallen asleep without meaning to. When he woke, the room was dark and the wax had slumped into a translucent stub and gone cold.

He was terrified.

The fire had gone out on the night he was supposed to be keeping it.

His mother had gone to visit Du the Elder — his whole family was scattered across the valley, too far to return in time. Lin Wen was away. No one else was in the house.

He sat in front of the bowl for a long time. At first he thought the fire was simply sleeping. He put his ear to the dish and listened. Nothing moved.

He wanted to cry — he did not. Instead, he went to the cupboard and found a small carton of matches. Only five were left.

He struck one — nothing. Another — nothing. Third — nothing.

His hand was shaking before the fourth. Then he remembered what Lin Wen had told him:

"Fire is not a god. Fire demands that you bring it yourself."

He steadied his breathing. He folded his fingers the way his father had shown him, and struck the match firmly.

The flame leapt out.

He carried it to the candle dish. The wax melted; the wick caught.

That night he did not cry.

He sat before the bowl, watching the flame flicker and lean — his ten-year-old eyes — and he did not blink until the first rooster crowed beyond the ridge.

In the morning he counted the five matches again.

Four were gone. One remained.

He held the last match for a long time. Then he tucked it into the seam of his pillow.

The next morning, his mother came with a cloth to wipe the bowl. She stopped, looked at A-Shi for a long moment — almost nothing, and yet she saw everything.

She said nothing. He said nothing either.

But from that day forward, the matchbox was never empty when the bowl was tended.

A-Shi understood one thing completely that day: when fire dies — you strike another one.

He never forgot that night. Years later, on a moonless night of wind, he lent out the last match from the seam of his pillow — not to light his own bowl — to light someone else's.

When he struck that match, he saw his grandfather's silhouette, standing by the doorway, holding court by the fire.

He did not cry then, either — not the same way.

He did not need to anymore.

Fire had not died. Fire had only been waiting for him to choose to strike again.

That person — the one who strikes the match even when no one is watching — is the keeper of the fire.

He is also the fourth in the family line.

---

*(End of volume fifty · Volume VII, fifth part.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Fifty-one: The Smoke Clears

On a late-summer evening, a thin column of smoke lifted from the far ridge.

Straight up, never bending left or right — one pale thread drawn through the sky as it crossed the old pines, the roof of the house, the highest branch of the persimmon tree.

A-Shi was the first to see it. He shouted from the yard:

"墨鹮 is here!"

His mother froze holding the spatula. Lin Wen stopped too.

— a figure was walking across the yard toward them.

Grey-robed. Grey-haired. One scar across his left cheek. He was not walking along any path; he moved straight through the gateposts, as if the earth had agreed to let him through.

His mother stood on the threshold and said something — very low, very raw:

"Come in and sit down."

墨鹮 did not step in. He stopped in the center of the courtyard. He rolled his sleeves to the middle of his forearm — a gesture very like someone folding a scroll before opening it. There was a thin scent on him — faintly of persimmon leaves, slightly of ashes.

She did not feel it at first. She stepped forward; she placed a cup of water on the table. He did not drink.

"How long can you stay?" his mother asked.

墨鹮 said only:

"The fire waits for you."

One sentence. No more.

He turned to look at Lin Wen. Then at A-Shi. The look lasted a long time — long enough that A-Shi thought he might say something — the smallest warmest thing any of them had ever heard.

Then he turned and walked out of the yard.

A-Shi chased him to the gate. He wanted to ask something — but 墨鹮 did not look back.

Rain began to fall as he passed the ridge. A-Shi watched him crossing the hill, soaking each fold of his grey robe. He heard a voice — very faint, very distant — a whisper like tapped stones on the far side of some world.

A-Shi ran back to the house.

His mother was on the porch. She was trembling slightly, but she had not cried.

"He's gone?" A-Shi asked.

Lin Wen said: "He does not need an escort."

"Then the fire—is it out?"

"It isn't out," his mother said.

She looked at the clay bowl. She said nothing else for a long time.

"He was not the fire himself," she said at last. "He was the one who carried the torch from one hearth to the next."

That night the three of them sat around the bowl and said nothing. They did not speak of it, and they did not cry. They simply stayed beside the flame — for the rest of the year, and beyond.

From now on, there would be no more mysteries.

Only warmth — and the stubborn grace of not blowing it out.

*(End of volume fifty-one · Volume VII, sixth part. Volume VII — In the Hearth's Spring — is complete.)*

# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Volume VIII**: Through Fire — the End of the Keepers

## Fifty-two: The Old Bowl

After 墨鹮 left, the house went quiet once more.

The bowl did not move from the table; nobody touched it. Every day A-Shi checked it once — just to see if it was still there. It was.

One evening the moon climbed s high the whole yard gleamed. A-Shi stood at the gate watching the old persimmon tree, then turned back to look at the bowl inside.

It reflected the moon; the moon reflected the bowl.

His mother called out. He went back inside.

She crossed the room and pointed at the bowl — no finger extended all the way, just a gesture toward it.

"This bowl," she said, "it was your grandfather's."

A-Shi raised his eyes.

"Which grandfather?" he asked.

"Lin Wen's father," his mother said. "He brought it down from the Yáohuǒ Station the year he visited the mountain passes."

Lin Wen did not add anything. He sat on the porch, his back against the doorframe, watching the moon.

His mother leaned toward him:

"When did Lin Wen's father bring it home?"

A-Shi thought for a moment. One memory surfaced immediately — when he was very young, someone had set a bowl down beside him while he was sick and asleep — then taken it away again. He had asked later who had been there. No one answered.

"The bowl…" his mother said, "is yours."

A-Shi blinked.

"…mine?"

She did not nod or shake her head — she just rested her hand on the bowl's shoulder.

A single bird crossed the sky, then silence again.

"The bowl," Lin Wen said from the doorway, "is not being given to you. It is being entrusted."

"For what?" A-Shi asked.

Lin Wen did not answer.

"You are its keeper," his mother said. "It will keep you."

No one moved. A-Shi stood in the middle of the room, face turned toward his mother; his mother's face turned toward the moon.

He stepped forward and was about to reach for the bowl — then paused.

His mother nodded slowly. Finally — just barely — he lifted the bowl with both hands.

The edge was smooth from many hands; the base held very faint scratches of old grain, hidden under layers of use.

He stood holding the bowl, feeling the weight of every house that had carried it before this one.

Lin Wen spoke one last line, so quiet that the moon seemed to listen:

"This bowl is our family."

A-Shi did not reply. He did not need to.

He set the bowl gently back in its place. He looked at his mother; he looked at Lin Wen; he looked out through the threshold at the dark persimmon tree in the moonlight.

He knew, now — that this bowl was not an heirloom — it was a beacon. It had never left anyone before.

The next day, he did not go out to the hills. He stood on the threshold until the moon went down. He touched the bowl from across the table once; it was warm.

He did not know why.

He just knew it was going to remain warm — because he was going to remain.

---

*(End of volume fifty-two · Volume VIII, first part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/53-the-vigil.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Fifty-three: The Vigil

In the evening, A-Shi set the clay bowl on the table. He spread the edge of the tablecloth under it to steady the base.

He went and fed the chickens, came back, and sat watching the bowl — untouched, and the candle stood straight.

Later — before bedtime — he woke all at once. The house was dark. The sky was still black.

He pulled the blanket slightly to see if the bowl was where he left it. It was — the flame still burned.

He watched a long time. Something glimmered very faintly along the rim. A pale ring, like morning leaking through something that remembered morning.

He did not move.

The bowl, he realized, was warm — warm against his palm from across the room.

He sat in silence.

Almost dawn, the sun crested the western ridge and the whole room filled with diffused light.

His mother came in. She said nothing.

She stopped at the doorway and saw A-Shi sitting with his head level with the bowl.

"You didn't sleep?" she asked.

"No," he said quietly.

"What did you see?"

"I didn't see anything," A-Shi said. "But it was watching me."

His mother nodded.

She came over, pulled up a chair, sat beside him. She spoke in a low, traditional way:

"Every night, when your grandfather lived, he sat this way — in silence — for a whole night at a time. He never slept. He did not say a word. He was teaching himself what could not be asked."

A-Shi didn't reply.

"That night," his mother said, "your grandfather would ask the bowl — why —"

She did not continue.

"— and no answer ever came — but those who sat heard it anyway."

The silence extended.

A whole day passed.

A-Shi waited for the lamp to go out.

It did not.

"A-Shi," his mother said, "go to sleep."

She reached for him and took his hand.

A-Shi dared not move. He closed his eyes.

He lay in bed — but he did not sleep. He thought about the bowl. It was teaching him, every second.

The next evening, he sat again.

His mother spoke to herself:

"That light — it has been burning for thirty years. This winter it will not go out."

A-Shi asked:

"How did Grandfather keep it?"

His mother considered before answering.

"— The bowl needs a person who sits and does not sleep," she said. And she stopped speaking.

A-Shi nodded.

He looked at the bowl and knew, very clearly:

— Grandfather had not left.

Grandfather had been here all along.

---

*(End of volume fifty-three · Volume VIII, second part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/54-the-supper-by-moonlight.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction

## Fifty-four: The Supper by Moonlight

Winter came gently.

The night was almost black at its heart, but the candlelight in the clay bowl shone so clearly that the old persimmon's shadow fell across the courtyard like a falling fan. A-Shi sat on the porch with a bowl of rice in his hands.

His mother came out and sat beside him. She said nothing.

She looked toward the sky.

Two figures entered the yard from the far end.

A-Shi lifted his head.

He recognized the old man at once — impossible, but undeniable — his grandfather.

He had yellow-white beard stubble and weathered eyes that had seen everything twice.

He stood and looked at A-Shi.

"Come here," he said.

A-Shi froze. His legs did not want to move.

Then they did.

He walked toward the old man slowly. The moon threw both their shadows long across the courtyard — too long, the way moonlight only shows things when it wants them to linger.

His grandfather smiled and said one name:

"A-Shi."

Then he added:

"Eat with me."

They sat side by side on the stone ledge — the dish of rice between them, the clay bowl warm behind them, the moon just above them.

A-Shi took a small bite. He could not speak. He could not look.

But he ate.

His grandfather did not eat.

His grandfather only held the bowl for a short time, held it as if someone had just lent it to him from very far away.

The air over the yard felt vast and utterly still.

A-Shi stood; he did not move.

He did not stop until the moon crested lower — and his grandfather's shadow disappeared into the glow.

A-Shi looked at the bowl.

Something shone under the sooty base.

When he pressed his finger against it, it was warm.

He did not cry. But his heart stopped, almost for one instant — then unstopped.

From that day he knew:

His grandfather had not gone.

His grandfather had been beside him the entire time.

The fire had not died.

It had never died.

---

*(End of volume fifty-four · Volume VIII, third part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/55-the-last-candle.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume VIII**: Through Fire — the End of the Keepers  

---

## Fifty-five: The Last Candle

In the early spring night, a thin layer of frost settled on the yard.

Lin Wen beckoned A-Shi onto the porch. The moon lay across the old persimmon tree — its branches bare, nothing left but the trunk and the rope tied to the sapling.

"Come here," Lin Wen said.

A-Shi stepped closer.

"I have something for you," Lin Wen said.

From his coat he drew one small box — no more than the size of his palm. A brown wooden case with a single black scratch across the lid. No name. No other mark.

"Open it."

A-Shi opened it.

Inside sat a tiny slip of paper — in his grandfather's handwriting.

**阿石。用它点完。**

One single match lay beside the paper.

A-Shi did not speak. The match lay still, its head dark against the pale wood.

Lin Wen looked at him and did not speak either.

"This was his," Lin Wen said.

His voice was very low.

"His final night — he kept this next to his pillow."

A-Shi felt something he never had reason to question until this moment.

The match was small. It did not glow or speak. It only lay, as if waiting.

He folded the box inside his coat fabric, against his chest.

His mother, standing at the doorway, saw him come in and say nothing. She asked:

"A-Shi — what are you doing?"

He did not answer.

"That's the one your grandfather saved," Lin Wen said. "He knew you would watch the bowl this night."

His mother lowered her eyes. She turned back to the kitchen.

That evening, A-Shi sat alone beside the bowl.

The candle burned very low.

Soft light covered the table and the face of everyone sitting around it.

They ate without speaking.

After midnight, A-Shi dimmed and almost fell asleep again — then he caught himself.

He rose his head.

Out of the half-darkness, the figure at the doorway moved.

His grandfather stood there, in silence, without ever crossing the threshold.

He saw A-Shi.

He did not speak. He only looked at A-Shi — a steadying look.

A-Shi did not move.

His grandfather withdrew.

The second morning, A-Shi woke to find one single match wedged between the bowl and the side of the cupboard.

Propped beside his grandfather's former seat — never asked for, but always obvious.

Lin Wen looked at the match. He understood.

"He saw you tonight," Lin Wen said.

A-Shi nodded.

That was the moment everyone understood.

The fire had not died.

It had not gone anywhere.

The bowl had kept the light for all these years — and now the fire was clearly visible.

A-Shi looked at the bowl. The flame at its center trembled and then steadied itself, rising into a bright vertical sliver of gold.

The whole room filled with warmth.

His grandfather had not left.

He had only been further away than they knew.

The fire burned.

It was beautiful.

It was the whole family's fire.

---

*(End of volume fifty-five · Volume VIII, fourth part. Volume VIII — Through Fire — is complete.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/56-the-match.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume IX**: In Prayer of Fire — the Rekindling
