## Fifty-six: The Match

One night in early spring, A-Shi woke to find the candle in the clay bowl gone out.

The room was black. He sat for a long time without moving, without speaking.

The bowl had been dark; the iron fire-token he kept in his pillow box could not be found; every match had every corner. His own coat pockets, his pillowcase, his shoe soles — all empty.

He hesitated in the doorway; then he went to the small wooden chest his grandfather had brought back from the way-station and opened it. Bare.

And then — wedged between the last two panels of wood — one small strip of paper. A deliberate, fold of paper.

He tore it carefully open.

**Fire does not die — it sleeps.**

He sat with the paper a long time.

Then he got up.

He found his cloak, his boots, and he ran—not stopping until he reached the old yurt house on the hillside, where Du the Elder kept his stove.

Du was sitting in the doorway when he arrived. He had been expecting him.

A-Shi said nothing. He reached into his coat and held out the empty matchbox.

Du looked at it for a long moment. Then he looked at A-Shi's face.

"How long have you been keeping this?" he asked.

"My grandfather brought it back from the Yáohuǒ Station," A-Shi said.

"Where is he?"

A-Shi took a breath.

"Dead."

Du nodded. He did not nod in a shocked way — rather it was his quiet authority, the same look he gives everyone who comes through his gate.

"Come in," he said.

A-Shi stepped through the door.

Du lit a small stick, handed it to him, and started to prepare tea. Neither of them spoke. The water heated; the room warmed gradually.

At last Du said:

"Does your family have any fire left?"

"No," A-Shi said.

Du nodded again, slowly.

"Then we tend the altar here first."

He led A-Shi to a small stove at the back of the yurt, and lit it very carefully.

"Your grandfather came here," Du said. "He was weak. I looked after him."

A-Shi held the hot stick in his fingers. He didn't burn himself.

Du said: "Everyone carries things across the bridge — whether or not they know it. They don't know, but they see."

They stayed in silence for they both needed.

Before dawn, Du gave A-Shi one single match.

"Only one," he said. "That is what you take with you."

A-Shi took it. He did not ask why.

By morning, Du's yurt had filled with light from the stove. A-Shi ate porridge. Du watched him carefully.

Then A-Shi walked home.

He put the match inside the clay bowl at his house. The bowl did not light at once.

He knew better to wait. He sat beside the bowl a long time.

Eventually, he heard the faintest crack — the match in the bowl woke a new fire. It spread slowly into the wick. The candle burned again.

He did not cry. He only listened.

But later — when he was absolutely sure the bowl was lit and would not go out — he whispered so quietly:

"Grandpa, you are still here."

He stayed until dawn.

By morning, the whole valley had heard. From the valley ridge, from the south, smoke rose from every house — as if there had never been any question about the fire's survival.

What had gone out had only been resting, waiting for someone to bring the light back.

---

*(End of volume fifty-six · Volume IX, first part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/57-the-embers.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume IX**: In Prayer of Fire — the Rekindling  

---

## Fifty-seven: The Embers

A month had passed since the bowl came alive again.

A-Shi still kept vigil. He fed the fire. He never asked anybody about it.

One afternoon his mother sent the boy for a pail, then fetched from the bottom of the wardrobe a small brown cloth bag, tied twice at the corners. She set it on the table.

Inside was a small ceramic firepot. Every corner had been blackened bright; every ridge had been worn smooth, thinned by generations.

"This was your grandfather's," she said.

A-Shi picked it up slowly.

Inside the pot were soft embers — still holding their shape, lightly settled into their own ash.

A-Shi did not ask.

He just held the pot in his hands and let the family history enter him.

His mother said:

"Your grandfather — at the end — gave this to me."

He nodded.

She looked at him a long time before she spoke again.

"He said — give it to you."

Then she added:

"He said — 'keep it; it keeps you.'"

A-Shi listened. He did not speak. His fingers tightened around the pot.

He set it back on the table. He sat silent.

The night he returned to the kitchen to cook, his mother stood at the doorway watching him.

She said:

"Your grandfather kept this same fire in his time."

A-Shi said nothing.

She nodded and left him to it.

The next morning, A-Shi sat beside the forge again. He stayed until dawn. He did not sleep.

By morning, the pot was glowing softly, still full of embers.

He said to himself:

"The fire has not died."

Then he closed his eyes and slept.

When he woke, the pot still burned.

A-Shi understood a great deal then — he understood he had simply heard what every generation had been told, but could never quite tell themselves.

The fire had never gone out.

It had only been waiting for him to accept it.

From that day onward — and every night after — the house never fell silent again. The pot held a shape steadily in the middle of the room, chest still warm in his hands. What he had been handed by his ancestors was the ember of every house that had ever stood against the cold.

*(End of volume fifty-seven · Volume IX, second part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/58-the-white-stone.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume IX**: In Prayer of Fire — the Rekindling  

---

## Fifty-eight: The White Stone

Early spring, the river was thawing.

A-Shi rode down to the bank for the first time. He knew the road had led him here. He carried an empty clay bowl — nothing inside; he had not told anyone about the bowl.

He stopped at the stones.

There was a girl on the far bank.

White clothes, white hair like seed down — a face he had never seen and would somehow never forget.

She saw him watching, and she called across:

"You are the child Lin took back with the bowl?"

A-Shi stared.

Yes. He was.

"They call you A-Shi," she said.

"I have that name."

She smiled. Quiet white smile.

"I am Baiyin," she said.

"Of where?"

"I guard the river upstream," she said. "My house is beside this river."

A-Shi did not ask how long she had been there.

She drew her hand out of her sleeve and offered him one single white stone — simple as a pebble, smaller than a coat button.

"Take it," she said.

A-Shi took it.

"What is this?"

"It is from your grandfather," she said.

A-Shi almost dropped it.

He looked at the stone carefully, turned it in his palm.

"Then it belonged to him?"

"Then it belongs to you," she said.

She took a step and began to leave.

"How do you know my grandfather?" A-Shi called after her.

She did not stop.

He looked down. The stone was already warm.

Baiyin was gone. The river surface carried nothing of her presence.

He stood very still, his grandfather's silent ah in his palm.

Then he took the bowl out of the pouch, removed the stone from his pocket, and put it inside.

The bowl began to glow from within.

A faint, strange warmth traveled outward — upward — the way fire does.

By the time he realized it, his fingers were warm and his heart had quieted.

When he reached his own home, A-Shi put the bowl on the table as though it had never been moved.

His mother came in and noticed it at once.

Her face froze.

"What did you do?" she asked.

A-Shi told her everything — the river, the girl, the white stone.

His mother covered her face with her hand and cried very quietly, as though breaking something.

"Your grandfather chose you," she said. "He chose you by his last choice."

She lowered her hand at last.

"He has not left. He never left, ever."

A-Shi did not answer.

He held the bowl gently.

From that day, the bowl never lost its light. Even in deep winter it held a small faint glow that could not be extinguished.

He learned not to think of it as anything other than himself, forever — his and his wife's and his wife's child's children's — every descendant who would one day sleep beside that bowl.

Baiyin — she was not a person. She was the river. She was every river he had run past to reach this fire.

He understood it at once.

She had been the fire all along.

Not the bowl.

The world.

From then on, A-Shi was the keeper. He came in from the mill once a week, sat down beside the light, and fed it.

Every morning, every night, the fire burned beautifully.

He was not afraid anymore.

---

*(End of volume fifty-eight · Volume IX, third part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/59-the-kindling.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume IX**: In Prayer of Fire — the Rekindling

## Fifty-nine: The Kindling

The approach of winter was in the air.

His mother built the stove first. She lifted a small piece of volcanic glass from her cupboard — a small rock, dark orange, cold to her fingertips.

She pressed it into A-Shi's palm.

"This," she said, "is the kindling. The whole hearth depends on this seed. Every time we raise a new flame, we touch it to this same stone. This is older than our family."

A-Shi held the stone carefully.

"How old?" he asked.

"Six hundred years," she said.

He did not understand.

"Six hundred years," she said again, "passed from one hand to the next, without ever breaking."

She replaced the stone in the wooden box and set the box back in the cupboard.

"It is why no one in our house has ever let the furnace grow cold," she said.

A-Shi stood watching her.

He stayed inside listening to the stove for a long time.

"Grandfather is in there, isn't he?" he asked finally. "In the fire?"

His mother did not answer.

She pointed to the stove.

The stove roared inward, deeper and deeper, as if answering something.

"He is not alone," she said. "No one ever was."

The fire crackled. Endless threads of heat curled upward.

A-Shi put his palm near the stove door.

Warm air pressed back against his hand.

His mother looked at what he was doing. She smiled.

"You already know," she said. "You always knew."

The room warmed around them both.

A-Shi nodded. He finally understood that his whole life had been inside this fire — every prayer he had ever not known he was making, every night he had sat by this hearth, every page of every book he had never read.

The fire was older than everyone.

The fire had never not known him.

A-Shi felt something turn inside him — not a feeling exactly — a direction.

He understood what it meant to be the next one to hold this.

"Then we will hold it together," he said.

His mother looked at him.

She nodded.

The stove kept burning.

No one in the house ever spoke of it after that.

They did not have to.

The fire spoke for everyone.

# source: creative/novel/en/60-the-firewood.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume X**: Firewood — the passage of the sacred hearth

## Sixty: The Firewood

One winter night, A-Shi sat beside the stove.

He was thirteen years old. The flame burned brightly.

His mother walked over carrying an old sword with a sandalwood handle — blackened at the pommel from contact with hands across six centuries. She placed it on the table.

"This is yours now," she said.

A-Shi looked up.

"Your grandfather's," she said.

He reached out to touch the blade. He realized it was heavier than the others he had known.

"It is the one he used to cut the winter wood."

A-Shi swallowed.

"This one," she said in English. "It followed him the whole journey."

"Six hundred years?" A-Shi whispered.

"Yes," she said.

A-Shi didn't know how to react. He put both hands around the handle. It held his fingers lightly.

"How many?"

"Six hundred years," she said again. "Every generation, every winter, after every night — wood comes. Six hundred years."

"Six."

"Yes."

"All fire?"

"All the fire."

He shut his eyes.

He imagined his grandfather on cold winter mornings cutting wood — his great-great-grandfather going into the mountain very early with his back against the same blade. Six centuries of it.

He felt a silence fall through the house. His mother and his grandfather had been here before him. They were all watching every moment his grandfather sat next to the stove.

"It will keep me," A-Shi said.

His mother did not blink.

"The handle," she said. "It remembers every hand that has ever touched it."

His mother told him: "Your grandfather used to say the family kept the hearth generation after generation. He never saw the end of it."

A-Shi asked one more question:

"Then what will I do with mine?"

"You," his mother said, "will keep it."

A-Shi heard her voice. A real sound, like the stove itself speaks.

He understood his role, the role his lineage carried for so long — he was not lonely. He would never be.

He would keep it.

Only some people know the fire truly belongs to everyone in the family.

A-Shi understood now.

He opened his eyes.

His mother was looking at him.

He nodded.

He took the sword.

"Then I'll keep it," he said.

His mother smiled.

No one needed to tell him anything else.

*(End of volume sixty · Volume X, first part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/61-splitting-the-firewood.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Sixty-one: Splitting the Firewood

After the spring came the season for splitting wood.

A-Shi carried his axe up the mountain.

He had been watching his mother for years, and for weeks he had watched the axe itself. He went and pulled down a dry pine branch onto the flat surface of a large stone, spread his legs apart, raised the axe above his head, and brought it down hard. The blade glanced off the wood and the handle vibrated in his hands.

He tried again from a different angle.

The ax did not go in. It skittered along the grain and stopped.

A-Shi stayed very still.

His mother had come up the path. She saw him sweating, his arms trembling.

"I'll help you," she said.

He shook his head.

He remembered her once telling him two things:

"Your feet planted wide."
"Your waist moves first. The axe falls second."

He stood straight. He spread his legs. He turned his torso slowly, held the ax loosely in both hands, and let the axe fall the way an autumn leaf falls — no force, just gravity.

The blade buried itself in the bark.

A loud ripping sound followed. The piece of pine split in two, clean halves, both sides falling apart exactly where they should.

A-Shi's eyes widened. He did not speak. He let go of the handle and just stood, watching the wind move over the mountainside.

His mother touched his shoulder lightly.

"Your grandfather split wood the same way," she said.

"I understand now," A-Shi said.

"Six hundred years?"

His mother smiled.

"That is how they taught him," she said, "and how his father before him, and all the way back."

A-Shi gathered the two halves of pine. The pain in his back was gone.

"Let's go home," he said.


He carried the wood down the slope.

His mother lit the stove when they returned.

The fire burned magnificently.

All afternoon and evening, A-Shi split more wood. Every log he handled, he managed.

The fireplace never wanted for anything until the snow was long gone and spring had fully arrived.

*(End of volume sixty-one · Volume X, second part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/62-the-last-stick.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume X**: Firewood — the passage of the sacred hearth  

---

## Sixty-two: The Last Stick

Spring was leaving behind its last warmth.

A-Shi walked up the mountain every day.

He reached the oldest persimmon tree on the slope.

Its trunk was as wide as a hut. The top branches vanished into smoke against the sky.

A-Shi remembered his grandfather standing below it.

He planted his feet.

He raised the sword and prepared to cut one of the dry branches.

He stopped.

He decided not to cut.

He squatted down to the dry persimmon leaves around its roots.

He thought about his grandfather — then about his own father, and then his father's grandfather.

Each generation had sat under this tree.

They had all kept the fire alive.

He lowered the sword.

Slowly he said to the tree:

"I understand."

He knew now — this family is a keeper family, and he was its next fire-keeper.

He picked up a few dry branches the family had already shed.

He turned and went down.

He built the stove with them.

He lit it carefully.

The fire grew remarkably. The whole kitchen shone.

His mother opened the door behind him and smiled.

"You understand," she said.

A-Shi nodded.

He went out to the yard and sat in the moonlight.

The moon had come up higher, not yet a full disc.

He closed his eyes and held his sword to his chest.

The wind came over the ridge. A-Shi found that his body felt vast as a sea that no one crossed, but the path had been there all along — he was the keeper now, carrying what his grandfather had carried.

He would be previous-generation's next grandfather's grandfather.

He held more for the fire than for anything else.

---

*(End of volume sixty-two · Volume X, third part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/63-the-original-fire.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles

## Sixty-three: The Original Fire

Spring went on.

His mother went to the market early. The house was empty.

A-Shi wandered around. He opened every drawer. Finally, in the bottom drawer of the oldest wardrobe, was a small wooden chest.

He had never noticed it before.

He lifted the lid.

Inside were six small blackened iron plates. Each one bore a face.

He studied them one by one.

He recognized the features — they were all his grandfather. Every single one.

Different ages, different fringes, different expressions — but the same face.

He put them back into the chest as his mother returned home. He shut the lid quietly.

"What did you find?" she asked.

A-Shi hesitated.

"Nothing," he said.

His mother approached. She looked carefully at his face, saw something in it, and walked past him to the stove without another word.

"Will you make dinner?" she asked.

"Of course."

He followed her into the kitchen.

They cooked side by side. The silence between them was unbroken.

Finally A-Shi spoke.

"Then this is my family's fire token — isn't it?"

"Iron plaque?"

"Father?"

She didn't look up.

A-Shi lowered his eyes.

After a long moment, she said:

"I have never had a chance to tell you this. Someday you will make more."

"How long — do you know?"

"Six hundred years," she said.

A-Shi did not respond.

For a long time they sat beside the stove and watched. The fire was small, orange, and unhurried. The silence between them was simple and warm.

Six hundred years.

Six hundred years of fire — every generation reaching the next, one hand extended to the other.

He was in the middle now.

He was the center of a family.

He put the plates away in the wooden chest. He turned to the stove.

He thought about every person who had stood beside this same stove — his father, his grandfather, and uncounted more.

He put both hands closest to the fire.

His mother didn't turn.

"Just sit with it," she said.

He nodded.

He sat.

The fire burned steady and beautiful, forever.

He stayed until the stars appeared over the courtyard.

His grandfather — wherever he was — was warm.

A-Shi was exactly where he belonged.

*(End of volume sixty-three · Volume X, fourth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/64-the-beacon.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

## Sixty-four: The Beacon

A-Shi was fifteen.

On the night of the autumn festival, his mother took him up the mountain behind the house.

They climbed to the highest point on the western ridge, to a broad flat stone.

His mother lit a small oil lamp.

The light spread across that stone like a low tide reaching the shore.

From up there, A-Shi saw all along the eastern ridge a string of similar lights — small, orange, far away.

"Look," his mother said.

Each family along the mountainside had also lit their lamp for the festival.

Six hundred years of the same village doing this every year — generation after generation.

"Six hundred years?" A-Shi asked.

"Six hundred," she said.

A-Shi paid attention to the mountainside. Every light on it — they were all kindling fire.

His mother held her lamp up; he held his.

They stood together on the stone.

Some lights went out. More came on.

The mountain was alive with fire far into the valley.

"This is what your grandfather did," she said. "His father did before him."

A-Shi looked at her.

"Six hundred years?"

"Six hundred years," she said.

A-Shi's heart swelled with an enormous silence.

He realized — he is one of them. Fire follows through his family.

His father's grandfather and this grandfather stood exactly here, on this same ledge, with a small lantern in hand.

Six hundred years of hearth had burned, and every one of those people had been keeping it.

"Keep this light," his mother said.

A-Shi looked at his. His was the smallest. He could barely see it flicker.

He understood that this was exactly the job — a tiny light among others.

He would keep it. He would not stop.

From this night onward, every autumn festival, he would come up here.

Six hundred years of stars and light.

He would be the next.

*(End of volume sixty-four · Volume X, fifth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/65-still-burns.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction

**Volume X**: Firewood — the passage of the sacred hearth

## Sixty-five: Still Burns

A-Shi was sixteen.

On the day of the winter solstice, his mother called him to the stove.

She brought out a small clay jar — as thick as a writing brush, big enough to hold a child's hand.

"You go now," she said.

"Where?"

"To deliver a letter."

"Who to?"

She didn't answer. She pressed the jar into his arms.

"Carry this with you."


A-Shi took the jar and set out. He climbed up to the ridge, to the broad white stone he had seen so many times.

Baiyin sat on the stone. She wore the same robe and white hair, but she seemed taller than before.

She smiled when she saw him.

"At last," she said, "you have brought fire."

A-Shi blinked.

She stood and took the jar from him. She opened it carefully.

Inside was a small, bright, living flame.

A-Shi didn't speak.

"This family," Baiyin said, "has kept fire for six hundred years."

"Six hundred years?" A-Shi asked.

Baiyin smiled.

"Six hundred years. My family came here to hold this mountainside and this hearth. They kept it alive through generation after generation."

"I see."

Baiyin nodded gently.

"Now you have been given it."

A-Shi looked at the flame inside the jar. It flickered and breathed, alive and warm.

"My family..." he began.

"They waited. Six hundred years."

"Six hundred years?"

A-Shi couldn't finish the word.

"They all kept it," she said. "Six hundred years. Every generation."

A-Shi stood in silence.

His family had lived under the same mountain six hundred years, and no one had ever let the fire go out.

He breathed deeply.

At last he found his voice.

"Unquenchable," he said.

Baiyin smiled.

He understood. From this night forward, A-Shi would keep the fire as every ancestor before him had kept it. He would be the one who made certain it never went dark. They belonged to each other.

Baiyin said nothing more.

She closed the jar and handed it to him.

Then she turned and walked away, disappearing into the pattern of light and shadow for good.

A-Shi stood in silence.

He made his way back home.

He approached the stove. He lit a small fire for himself.

He emerged from the kitchen.

His father was in the hall.

"Son," his father said. "Your mother sends you word."

"What?"

"Six hundred years."

A-Shi didn't reply.

Then his father said:

"This family will keep fire for as long as it stands. Forever. So long as we do it right."

A-Shi understood.

They held fire for six hundred years, and fire kept them for six hundred years.

This family would keep the same fire forever.

A-Shi looked out the window.

His stove flame still burned, brightly visible in his mind.

He would never let it die again.

---

*(End of volume sixty-five · Volume X, sixth part. Volume X — Firewood — is complete.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/66-the-keeper.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction  
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles  
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

## Sixty-six: The Keeper

A-Shi was eighteen years old.

His mother came to him one morning at the kitchen stove. She had in her hands one folded letter.

She extended her hand.

"Read this."

He read it. The line was simple and insistent.

Six hundred years we have kept the fire. To you henceforth.

A-Shi looked at the stove.

The fire was bright and stable. No one had ever let it go out for six centuries.

He asked: who kept it?

His mother answered without emotion.

"Grandfather's grandfather's grandfather's grandfather. Everyone before you."

Six hundred years. Every generation. Every hearth. Every minute.

He heard it now.

A-Shi felt the full weight of the centuries that had been handed to him.

His hand stretched to the stove. He added wood.

He understood now what they meant when they said the stove had never died.

Six hundred years.

Six hundred nights.

Every generation.

No one had ended it.

A-Shi finally understood something very deep about this house.

He had grown up inside the fire's life.

He simply had not known how to see it.


A-Shi laid both palms on the stove.

The warmth spread through them.

Six hundred years of warm, undying fire.

He was its next child.

Six hundred years of hearth.

Every one of them trusting whoever would come next —

the next to hold the logs, to add the sticks, to keep the blaze.

Six hundred years.

Six hundred years.

Now him.

*(End of volume sixty-six · Volume XI, first part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/67-the-kindle.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Sixty-seven: The Kindle

In the winter of the year that A-Shi turned eighteen, his mother did not send him to the woodpile as she always had. She set two bowls on the stove ledge and sat down, her voice measured in that quiet way that meant important things were coming.

"A thing for you to do tomorrow morning," she said.

"What?"

"Kindle."

A-Shi lowered his bowl. He thought he had misheard her.

"What is kindle?"

She laughed first, then grew serious again.

"Our house has kept fire since six hundred years ago. Before every new keeper takes charge of the hearth alone, they walk out and carry small sparks of other fires back home. The fire in this stove didn't light itself — six hundred years ago, someone laid the first stick. That first flame passed to the hearth, then to the beacon on the ridge, then to every lamp in every house. Kindle means learning that the fire you keep was raised, little by little, by the whole valley."

She reached into her collar and drew out an old cloth bundle. Inside it sat a clay jar no bigger than a thumb — the lid sealed with candle wax dark with age.

"Your great-great-grandfather's kindle-jar. Six hundred years, seven keepers have used it. The jar is still the same."

A-Shi took it carefully. The bundle was heavier than it looked.

"How do I kindle?" he asked.

"Start from our hearth. Then to Uncle Ye's house at the east end of the lane, the ever-burning lamp at the crossroads, Aunty Li's incense pot, and last of all the night-lamp up on the white stone on the ridge. At each place, gather a small ember into your jar. No fire-steel, no firestone — you may only light your own spark from someone else's flame. Once you have all five, come home and we'll join every one of them into the stove."

One more thing, she added:

"Leave the wax alone. It was sealed in the six-hundredth year."

A-Shi slept little, and fitfully.

At first light he carried his grandfather's old kindle-jar out of the village. The winter wind was cruelly sharp. The east end of the lane, he found Uncle Ye sweeping snow off his doorstep. Ye saw the jar and stopped.

"Kindle?"

A-Shi nodded.

Uncle Ye tapped out a little coal from his pipe bowl, blew on it twice and said: "You're from the A-Shi house. Can't say the words enough. This fire — I'll hold it for you."

Then he reached into his stove and drawn out a pinch of living red coal, wrapped it in paper, and placed it in A-Shi's open palm. It was not hot enough to burn him — just warm, soaking deep into the small of his hand.

"Six hundred years," Uncle Ye said. "The fire your family warmed this village with — it's in my stove too. Carry it home."

The crossroads lamp was tended by the temple. The old Daoist said not one word; he simply lowered the lamp wick lower so A-Shi could dip his ember in. Everything was as it should be. At Aunty Li's, she was in middle of her morning prayers; when she heard the request, she lit three incense sticks on the spot and pressed the head of one against the mouth of his jar — incense, hearth, ever-burning lamp, pipe coal — four fires lay quiet in the jar.

One more place. The white stone on the ridge.

When he climbed up, the snow had stopped. Baiyin was gone. Only a lamp remained — the same one he had lit there on that autumn night half a year ago, its flame barely visible, steady as ever. Snow lay thick across the glass shell, not one spark escaped.

A-Shi knelt by the stone a while. He leaned the jar up against the lamp's mouth and cupped his hand over them both —

The wick bent, softly, like it remembered him. Or like it could not bear to leave. From the jar came a faint crackling, the same sound falling embers make when they tumble into ash.

He carried the jar down the mountain cradled against his chest.

By the time he returned, his mother was waiting at the stove. Two small stools were near the hearth. She carefully opened the jar. The wax seal was intact, perfect down to the last line.

She did not ask how the fire had gone. She only said one word:

"Join them."

A-Shi fed six hundred years — he'd never before felt it so vividly — into the stove, little by little, from the jar. The fire made no sound and no smoke; it only steadied into a darker, heavier glow, and then began to brighten. Brighter than before — like the last sliver of sun on the river at dusk, but steady, and gentle, and warm.

"The stove stands again," his mother said.

"And what now?" A-Shi asked.

"Now you'll understand that fire never waits for anyone," she said. "You answer for these five fires, because you have already lived their six hundred years for them."

He laid his hand on the stove ledge anyway, feeling the stove's deep, even heat. Some part of him had finally opened —

Keepers of six hundred years are not born from the stove alone; they're kindled, flame by flame, from every lamp in the village, every incense pot, every pipe coal, every ember that refuses to go out. They are one fire, many children, scattered across six hundred years, now come home at last.

He set a stick on the stove's shoulder, and in the ash the spark fluttered twice.

"Put the jar away," his mother said. "Keep it for your own child."

He nodded.

That night, he sealed the jar with wax again and placed it back in the wooden box. He thought of Baiyin waiting alone on the white stone. He thought of the six hundred years of keepers who had walked the same path, step by step. He thought of tomorrow — what he must do.

To keep fire, he thought, one must be like fire itself.

Unquenchable.

---

*(End of volume sixty-seven · Volume XI, second part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/68-the-gathering.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Sixty-eight: The Gathering

Seven days after the kindle rite, his mother folded down where she stood by the stove.

A-Shi caught her arms before she could fall. Her palms were cold and clammy, her face drained of colour, yet her mind was perfectly clear. She said only:

"It's nothing. Just a rest and I'll be fine."

He carried her to her bed, then went back to the stove. He fed the fire a stick but it would not rouse itself. He stared at the hearth mouth for a long time before it struck him — from this day onward, every hour of every night would rest on his shoulders alone. The fire of six hundred years had landed in his hands at last.

Sleep would not come that night. Toward dawn he heard a soft knock at the door.

It was Uncle Ye at the threshold, a half-eaten roast chicken in his arms.

"The madam?" he asked.

"Resting."

"Good," said Uncle Ye. "While the hearth has a keeper, she can be at peace. Keep the fire low — I'll fetch her elder sister-in-law."

A-Shi wanted to say there was no need. Uncle Ye was already gone.

By mid-afternoon, Li Aunty arrived carrying a clay crock of congee. She stepped straight into the kitchen, said nothing, set the crock on the range to warm, and swept the stove-mouth with practiced eyes. She nodded once:

"The fire is well kept."

By dusk the old Daoist monk from the temple came, a flask of wine swinging from his hand. He sat on the threshold rather than go in, and said:

"Six hundred years the little flame has not gone out. The lamp at your crossroads — the temple will keep it burning yet."

Night came down, and by the time lanterns were lit, more had gathered around the stove than A-Shi expected. Uncle Ye, Li Aunty, the old Daoist, two or three neighbors from up the lane — all silently, and without invitation, standing close enough to the hearth to warm their hands. His mother had been helped in from her bed, bundled in her old cotton coat, propped up in a kitchen chair. The fire glowed orange, gilding every face and throwing their shadows tall against the wall.

No one spoke for a while. Then a log cracked like a whip and Uncle Ye said:

"Six hundred years ago the first fire was lit on winter solstice. Your great-grandfather was alive then."

"Yes," his mother said.

"That fire..."

"Was lit," she said, "and before the week was out, someone came over the ridge asking to borrow it. A traveling peddler, whose cart had sunk into the snow. He said he'd keep vigil on the ridge for the night. The old man gave him a coal without a second thought."

"And then?" asked Li Aunty.

His mother glanced at A-Shi.

"The peddler left the next day. As he went, he said: next time I pass through, I'll come back for this fire." She smiled. "He came back every year after that, each time asking to borrow the same coal. The ever-burning lamp at the crossroads — he lit it with it. The beacon on the ridge — he persuaded the old man to build it with it."

"So your family's fire..." Uncle Ye began.

"Has always been a fire," his mother said, "lent to others. A keeper does not keep fire from going out — a keeper keeps fire so that others may carry some of it away."

The stove grew brighter.

A light step in the doorway — everyone turned. Baiyin stood in the entrance, in a moon-white robe washed pale with time. Her hair was a little longer than the last time she had stood here, and her eyes in the firelight were as still as deep wells.

"Baiyin," said his mother.

Baiyin nodded, walked to the stove, glanced in, and said:

"The fire is steady."

She lowered herself beside A-Shi, much as she had once sat waiting on the white stone.

No one else spoke. The logs cracked and sighed. Uncle Ye set a pair of chopsticks in front of A-Shi. Li Aunty spooned congee into five bowls. Baiyin took two of them — one for his mother, one for A-Shi. The broth was hot and barely salted, nothing but rice and water. Yet no one said anything as they ate, only stared into the fire.

When the night was deep and the guests began to drift home, the old Daoist left the flask of wine on the stove ledge, and Uncle Ye left the bones of the chicken with it. As Baiyin rose to leave, she said to his mother:

"Six hundred years your fire has not faltered. This generation won't either."

His mother nodded.

At the door Baiyin paused, turned back, and said to A-Shi softly:

"Six hundred years. Every time someone stands before this fire, the fire looks back."

A-Shi stood by the stove for a long while, watching.

It opened in him, slowly.

Gathering around the stove is not a family warming itself — it is six hundred years of people meeting, year after year, before the same hearth — some come, some go, the fire burns on, the people turn over like the seasons.

He put one final stick on the embers and said to his mother:

"I understand now."

"What is it you understand?" she asked.

"This fire," A-Shi said, "is not something I keep. It's something that keeps me."

His mother said nothing. She drew her coat tighter around her shoulders. Night deepened. The stove burned on. There would come another day, soon, when another would come bringing wood, and sit around this same flame.

A-Shi kept watch before it until dawn.

The fire did not go out.

---

*(End of volume sixty-eight · Volume XI, third part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/69-ink.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Sixty-nine: Ink

On his first watch before the hearth, after the gathering, the wind changed.

It came in from beyond the ridge, carrying a very faint smell — like fresh rain, or like cold ash. He thought he was dreaming, but at first light the flame of the hearth had shrunk by an inch, as if something had pressed it gently down.

He assumed the wood was damp and fetched a dry log. The fire still lay flat.

"So you saw it," came a voice from behind the stove.

A-Shi started. Baiyin had been there the whole time, sitting in the dark — moon-white robe, half her face lit by the dying ember.

"Saw what?"

"Ink."

He did not understand. He added another dry log. This time the flame lifted a little, and sank again slowly.

Baiyin leaned toward the stove, her face tipped into the firelight.

"Six hundred years ago, when the first flame was kindled, this world had colour. Red was red. Azure was azure. Sunsets were actually burning. Later —" she paused, "— later, a character fell."

"Which character?"

Baiyin did not answer at once. She lowered herself to the hearthside and held out her palm over the fire. The light stained the back of her hand with a dark orange pattern, like rice paper scorched by a candle.

"Ink," she said. "Not a stain — a word. This world is pinned under a single character — 墨. Ink is not dirt. Ink is what remains when everything burnt — wood becomes ink, bones become ink, cruel words become ink. And this flame," she pointed at the stove, "is one of the very few lights ink cannot swallow."

A-Shi stared into the hearth. The fire burned on, apparently unchanged, yet somehow brighter than it had been all night — a faint light that had been hiding six hundred years in plain sight.

"Then why do we guard it?" he asked.

Baiyin looked at him.

"To remember."

"To remember what?"

"To remember us — to remember what fire looked like. When this world is finally pinned under ink and all its colours are drawn back in, there will need to be someone who remembers what fire was."

A-Shi was silent for a long time.

"Was that what the six hundred years of keepers were for?"

Baiyin nodded.

"They didn't keep fire for the fire's sake," she said. "They kept it because the Chinese character for warmth — 暖 — has a fire inside it. Without the fire, there is no warmth. Without warmth there are no people."

Then she drew from her sleeve a slim booklet so thin it was nearly invisible — its corners charred black. She opened it on the low table beside the stove.

Every page was covered in handwriting. Line upon line, stroke after stroke — all of them the same character: 记 — to remember. Some strokes slanted, some were carved with fingernails, some were pressed so hard the paper had given way beneath them.

"Six hundred years," Baiyin said, "every keeper, on their deathbed, writes one character in here. However long they kept the fire, it gets remembered. You'll do the same, one day."

A-Shi reached out and touched the first stroke. The ink was deep, impossibly deep. He could not see the hand that had written it — yet under his fingertips, somehow, he felt warmth. Somebody — a keeper from six hundred years ago — had left that warmth pressed into the page.

"It's all fire," A-Shi whispered.

Baiyin smiled.

"Yes," she said. "It's all the same character, the same flame, the same warmth. Six hundred years kept, and six hundred more still to keep."

The log in the hearth cracked. A single spark leapt up and landed beside the open page. It did not burn the paper. It left only a tiny glowing circle.

Baiyin closed the booklet and pressed it into A-Shi's hands.

"Take it. After six hundred years, the fire finally lands in your keeping."

She walked to the door. She did not turn back, but her last words reached him clearly across the room:

"Remember this — ink is the character the world will one day close around. Fire is the warmth the world will one day burn straight through. Your work is to make sure the two things remember each other."

A-Shi sat before the stove for what must have been hours.

Dawn came slowly. The hearth burned on, steady, like a letter sent from six hundred years ago — and on the envelope only two characters were written:

Keep it.

---

*(End of volume sixty-nine · Volume XI, fourth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/70-the-601st.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Seventy: The Six Hundred and First

On the morning Baiyin left, A-Shi found the stove-fire dimmed to a single thread.

He thought it was a matter of wood. He fed it three dry logs, but the flame stayed weak. The red coals in the ash lay curled like a tiny, drowsy orange.

He climbed the ridge at a run. The night-lamp on the white stone had gone out.

New snow lay over the stone, bright as a mirror. The glass shell was filmed with frost. The wick showed its last burn — black, curled into a tiny hook, like a copper wire bent shut. Six hundred years, and this lamp had never gone out. He had lit it himself on that autumn night, and Baiyin had sat beside it all night, waiting for it to brighten again.

"It's out," he whispered.

From his belt he untied the kindle-jar — the one they used every year for the kindle rite — and squatted beside the stone for a long while, searching for something he could not name.

A step behind him. Baiyin stood there, her white robe whipping in the winter wind, her face the calmest it had ever been.

"It's supposed to be out," she said.

"Why?"

Baiyin beckoned him closer.

"Come."

A-Shi stopped three steps away from her, the old habit of caution still in his bones. She did not ask him to step any nearer. She only said:

"For six hundred years, that lamp did not fail. It wasn't the fire's doing — it was the people's. Your grandfather, your great-grandfather, your great-great-grandfather — they all kept watch on this lamp through the night. They knew the fire would not go out. They only could not bear not to look."

She stared at the lamp-stand, and her voice lowered to almost nothing:

"But your stove is different. Your stove is the thing that does not go out. It hastaken in all the six hundred years of warmth. What burns in that stove is the thing that actually survived."

"Then the lamp...?"

"Was a beacon — to show the valley that someone up here was watching. Six hundred years, the whole village knew: the peak is still lit, so nobody needs to be afraid. It does not need to burn anymore, because —"

She looked down the ridge, toward the village.

"Because your stove stands now, A-Shi. For the first time in six hundred years, the valley does not need the lamp on the peak to feel safe."

A-Shi stared at the dead wick.

Baiyin continued:

"The night-lamp is the last of its kind. It is handed down to your stove now. Anyone in the valley who looks up to the ridge will no longer see a light — but they will know the chimney from that house in the east never stops. That is enough."

"And it — ?" A-Shi gestured at the lamp.

Baiyin held out her palm. On it sat one last pinch of black ash — warm, somehow, glowing with six hundred years' final breath.

"Take it to your stove. Let it join the fire there."

A-Shi received the ash in both hands, as carefully as he had received the ember from Aunty Li's incense pot. When he was six, he had held Grandfather's charcoal brazier in just this way, and it had only warmed him for a single winter. This time —

This time he was holding the close of six hundred years.

He walked down the ridge, unstoppered the kindle-jar above the stove, and poured the dust slowly into the hearth. The fire did not leap. It only paused for half a heartbeat, then grew a little brighter — as if the six hundred years had answered.

His mother opened her eyes. Seeing the stove burn like this, she smiled.

Baiyin sat down in the doorway.

"The night-lamp is out?" she asked.

A-Shi nodded.

His mother laughed softly. "The seven-hundredth year, then."

Baiyin shook her head.

"The six hundred and first," she said. "These six hundred years, every keeper passed his fire down to the next. Tonight, it comes all to A-Shi. From today onward, the stove is a light the whole world can see."

She turned toward A-Shi.

"Do you know what feast today is?"

He did not.

Baiyin said:

"The ascension of the six hundred and first keeper."

She said it gravely, with none of her usual lightness, as though the first six hundred years were standing behind her.

A-Shi lowered his head. He did not know what to say. He could see the stove-fire shining in everyone who had ever stood watch over this hearth — Grandfather's profile, Father's open hand, Baiyin's eyes, Uncle Ye's pipe smoke, the old Daoist's sandals on the threshold, his mother's young face.

"From tomorrow," Baiyin said, "the beacon is no longer the lamp. You are."

Then she stood and walked to the doorway.

Before she stepped through, she turned and said one sentence — and for all his life afterward A-Shi could never recall the whole of it, only the last half:

"— Fire is not kept for people to see. Fire is kept so that people can remember. Remember it, and it will not go out."

She left. She did not look back, and she did not say goodbye — the way every keeper before him had come into his life, or been from it.

A-Shi reached for another stick and set it inside the stove. The sparks leapt high and filled the hearth.

"I will remember," he said.

The fire answered.

---

*(End of volume seventy · Volume XI, fifth part. The seventy-volume canon passes its first milestone.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/71-the-painter.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Seventy-one: The Painter

On the third day after the snow stopped, deep winter was still with them.

The stove burned fierce and steady, the whole iron body glowing a low, dark red. A-Shi wiped the kitchen cloth over the stove ledge, poured himself a bowl of plain rice porridge — and heard someone cough outside.

He opened the door. Wind blew in, carrying a very faint scent of pine soot and old paper.

A man stood at the threshold.

He wore a traveller's hat, a short grey-brown tunic, and a bulging cloth-wrapped pack over one shoulder — and in his other hand he held a small bamboo basket. His footprints behind him stretched unbroken all the way from the mountain foot into the very mouth of this stove's house.

"Good master," the traveller said, his voice low, gritted like sandpaper on wood, "may I borrow a night's fire?"

A-Shi ushered him in.

The traveller did not look at him at first. He walked straight to the stove and stared into the flames. He stared so long that A-Shi thought he must be seeing something else altogether. When he finally spoke, it was quiet.

"I am a painter. I walk through the world, and I paint fire."

A-Shi hesitated. Then he asked:

"Which fires?"

The traveller did not answer directly. He drew out a roll of paper from his pack, spread it on the low table by the stove, and unhooked a brush from his belt — the ink on its tip was still wet.

"I started at the beacon on the ridge," he said. "Three months of painting fire. The beacon, the night-lamp on the white stone, the ever-burning lamp at the crossroads, Uncle Ye's pipe coal, Li Aunty's incense pot — every place, I painted it."

He opened the roll. A-Shi saw: pages and pages of flames, every one a different shape. Red flames, orange flames, blue flames, ember-red flames, flames that glared white, flames that had collapsed into a single shadow. Some stretched upright, some lay flat, some curled like flower buds about to open.

"Every one of them looks like fire," A-Shi said.

The traveller shook his head.

"No. Each one looks like somebody else's fire. Not one of them looks like itself."

He drew out one blank sheet, spread it on the table, sat upright, and slowly dipped his brush. The stroke moved slowly, as if held back by something. One stroke. Then another. Then a third.

On the seventh stroke, the brush hitched — and everything changed.

A-Shi came close to look. It was a flame, drawn with only seven strokes. Every stroke was a direction he knew well — the ridge's seven beacon-lamps. They did not lie in a single direction — instead they pointed outward in every direction at once, like a flame, like a ridge line, like an open pair of hands.

"You — " A-Shi stammered.

The traveller lifted his head.

"Your family's fire," he said, "is not like any other. Three months I painted, and I could never finish it."

He lowered the brush.

"Now I understand why. This stove has been burning for six hundred years — the oldest fire in the world. And the oldest fire on earth is not something any man can pass off as his own painting."

A-Shi stared at the seven strokes.

"What is this?"

"Not fire," the traveller said. "This is what fire remembers."

He folded the painting and pressed it into A-Shi's hands.

"This one is yours. Six hundred years — somebody should paint this."

The paper was feather-light, like the first snowflake to fall six hundred years ago.

While the traveller tied his pack, A-Shi asked, almost to himself:

"Are you the one who came six hundred years ago? The one who borrowed the stove-fire from Grandfather and lit the ever-burning lamp at the crossroads?"

The traveller did not turn.

"I am only a painter," he said.

Then he was gone. A-Shi stood in the doorway and watched his footprints fade in the fresh snow until there was nothing left to see.

Dawn came. A-Shi framed the painting and hung it beside the stove.

Baiyin asked about it later. After a long silence, A-Shi answered:

"He is not the peddler from six hundred years ago. The peddler from six hundred years ago was a spark himself — he spent his whole life looking for a fire that burns six hundred years."

He looked at the stove.

"And he found it."

---

*(End of volume seventy-one · Volume XI, sixth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/72-the-reply.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Seventy-two: The Reply

Five days after the painter left, A-Shi opened the wooden box and took out the kindle-jar to polish it.

The wax seal on the lid — sealed six hundred years ago — was perfectly intact. He sat by the stove with the jar in his lap, and then he noticed something: a tiny folded page lay at the bottom of the jar, where there had never been anything before.

The paper was torn from the ledger — its edges charred black.

He unfolded it.

One single line, written in pine-soot ink, small but pressed hard enough to mark the back of the paper:

The debt is not fire. Your grandfather's grandfather's grandfather owes me a reply.

A-Shi froze.

He read the line three times. The sky outside was clear, the stove-fire steady as a well. He almost called out to his mother — but then he stopped himself, because he understood this letter was not addressed to her. It was for him.

"Six hundred years," he said aloud, "and he is still waiting."

He folded the page and slipped it back into the jar, then took the painter's seven-stroke painting down from its place and spread both on the kitchen table. The seven strokes pointed outward in every direction — like a flame, like ridge lines, like open hands.

He thought hard.

Six hundred years ago, a peddler crossed the mountain and came to this very stove. He borrowed a coal to light the crossroads' ever-burning lamp. He came back every year after, to borrow the same stove-fire — and every year he came, for six hundred years.

Why could a man walk for six hundred years and never arrive?

Because someone who can walk for six hundred years is not a peddler at all. He is someone who is waiting for a reply.

"What is he waiting for?" A-Shi asked his mother.

She was trimming vegetables, not looking up.

"When someone owes you something you never settle," she said, "you will wait forever for the settlement."

"What did he owe?"

She set down the knife and stood still for a long time before answering.

"Six hundred years ago, your great-great-grandfather gave the peddler a coal. The peddler accepted it, and he said something before he left."

"What did he say?"

She was silent for a while.

"He said: when I have lit the ever-burning lamp at the crossroads, I will come back and return this coal."

"And then?"

"He did light it," said his mother. "The first ever-burning lamp at the crossroads — it was lit with that same coal. And from that day forward, he never came back. He climbed the mountain instead. He never returned. Six hundred years — every house burns the lamp in his honour, since nobody dared to forget him. But he himself has never crossed this threshold again."

"What is he waiting for?"

She looked at A-Shi for a long moment.

"For the fire to go out," she said. "He thought the fire would eventually go out. When the fire went out, the debt would be settled."

A-Shi stood very still.

"But the fire never went out."

"And that is why he cannot return," she said. "Six hundred years he has not come home — not because the fire went out, but because it never did. He never dared to face it."

A-Shi stood for a long time afterward without saying anything.

Then he sat down — took the painter's painting and the charred fragment of ledger page, set them side by side on the table, and drew from his pocket a small jar of pine-soot ink and a clean brush — a brush he had never used before, hand-whittled from a piece of stove-wood. He touched the brush into the heart of the stove, where the coals glowed hottest, and let the ash mingle with the ink. Then he wrote on the margin of the seven-stroke painting, in his own hand, nobody else's:

The fire received the message. The debt is not fire — it is six hundred years of people who kept this door for you. The stove-fire will not go out, the ever-burning lamp will not dim, and the white stone on the ridge is still lit. Six hundred years have passed. It is time to come home. The stove is still here.

He folded the painting and slid it into the kindle-jar beside the ledger fragment, and sealed the jar back in the wooden box.

As dusk came down, the stove leapt.

The flames shot up so tall they nearly reached the beam above. A-Shi looked up into the hearth, and for an instant he saw the whole stove-fire flare like a great warm wind — and something light and bright passed through it, gone before the eye could catch it. A shadow shaped like an old man, moving light and easy — but this time, not up the mountain. Back down.

No one spoke.

The fire steadied again slowly. A-Shi felt the whole kitchen grow a touch brighter.

Six hundred years, and the debt was a single letter.

Six hundred years, and the reply was this.

---

*(End of volume seventy-two · Volume XI, seventh part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/73-the-homecoming.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Seventy-three: The Homecoming

Ten days after the reply was written, A-Shi rose every morning earlier than the household.

He did not say who he was waiting for. He only built the fire, sat by the stove, and watched the road beyond the gate as the snow slowly melted.

On the seventh morning, his mother was airing the quilts in the courtyard when she saw a thin blue light feather out from the stove-room. She turned and looked in.

An old man was sitting on the threshold.

Very old — so old his hair looked like two logs of dead winter wood.

In his hand he turned over and over a small oil lamp — its wick burned down to the very end, and the oil reservoir bone dry. The whole lamp had gone black.

He did not greet A-Shi first. He looked at the stove for a long time, and only then said:

"It is still burning."

"It is still burning," A-Shi answered.

The old man nodded. He lowered his eyes to the lamp once more, his voice very quiet:

"This lamp... I have borrowed for six hundred years."

A-Shi understood everything in that one breath.

This lamp was the very one lit from the first spark borrowed from this stove six hundred years ago — the lamp lit at the crossroads, the ever-burning one, still burning to this day.

Not still burning. Running dry.

The borrower had simply never dared to return it.

The old man set the lamp down on the hearthside, straightening it as carefully as a man setting out an offering for an ancestor.

"Six hundred years ago I promised your great-great-grandfather — when I have lit the ever-burning lamp at the crossroads, I will come back and return this lamp. I set out that day. I crossed the mountain, and then I crossed another, and I walked for one year, and then another, and year after year for six hundred years. Every winter, every single winter, I saw this stove burning from far away. I never once forgot it."

He stopped.

"I did not fail to return because I could not. I could not return because I could not dare to enter this house."

"Six hundred years. The stove has never once gone out. As long as the stove burns, my debt stays unsettled."

A-Shi listened to his voice, and it was like the wind blowing through six hundred years of snow — ancient, crystalline, and carrying a tremor so slight it was nearly nothing at all.

The old man said:

"The last time I stood on this ridge, I looked once more at the stove burning far below. That was when I finally understood: fire does not burn for the reason that someone owes it a debt — it burns because that is what it is made for, and that is what it does. The ones who keep it, and the fire itself, they are already together. We have always been together for six hundred years."

He took off his hat and revealed a face so old the eyes had worn nearly level with the skin.

"Six hundred years ago I left this house with an empty basket on my back. I have come back now..."

From his sleeve, he drew a small parcel and placed it beside the lamp.

"This is for your family."

A-Shi opened the parcel. Inside were a few dried pine needles — yellow, dry, and brittle — and yet their scent had not gone: pine smoke and campfire, mingled gently together.

"These are the fire-seeds," said the old man. "Six hundred years ago, your great-great-grandfather gave me the first coal. I picked up a few pine needles on my way out, hoping to start a fire with them when I got home. They walked with me through six hundred years. I kept them all this time."

He smiled faintly:

"Six hundred years, and they still have not gone grey."

A-Shi set the needles by the stove and did not speak.

His mother came in then, stood behind the old man, and looked at the stove.

The flame leapt.

Softly — a tongue of fire curled up and then sank back.

Nothing caught light; nothing was burned. But A-Shi felt something inside the room settle into place, like a six-hundred-year-old wooden chair finally being pushed back into the corner where it had always belonged.

The old man sat on the threshold and drank a bowl of plain rice porridge his mother had brought him.

He did not speak for a long while.

When dusk came down, he stood and said to his mother:

"Thank you for sheltering me tonight."

"Tomorrow I must go,"

He stopped halfway through his sentence. He looked at the stove again, as if he could see six hundred years of this family standing there — Grandfather, Father, Mother, A-Shi, and the forgotten faces of the ones before them.

"Tonight is enough," he said.

He stepped out into the snow. From the gateway, A-Shi watched him follow the mountain path — walking far, further than any man his age should have been able to walk — and he never once looked back.

The stove-fire grew a little brighter.

His mother collected the old man's bowl and chopsticks. She gathered the pine needles and placed them in the wooden box, which she had not opened for six hundred years.

"Six hundred years," she said. "And these still did not go grey."

A-Shi looked at the stove.

"They are not needles," he said. "They are a debt, uncollected. A six-hundred-year-old debt, and fire."

The flame answered with one small bright waver.

---

*(End of volume seventy-three · Volume XI, eighth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/74-the-seed.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Seventy-four: The Seed

The pine needles lay quiet in the wooden box for a full week.

On the seventh night, A-Shi could not hold back any longer. He waited until his mother was asleep, took out one of the needles, and placed it in the bare dirt beside the stove, watering it with a single spoonful of stove-ash slurry.

He told no one.

On the sixth morning, he woke early to feed the stove — and saw a tiny shoot pushing up through the soil.

Each sliver of green carried a faint orange rim, like a flame just coming awake. A breeze passed; the shoots shivered as if breathing.

A-Shi crouched before the seedling for a long while.

His mother came out to hang the washing. She saw the shoot, stopped still in the wind, and slowly lowered herself to one knee. She touched a single needle-tip and held it to her nose. She was silent for a long time before she finally said:

"It is a fire-seed tree."

"A fire-seed tree?"

She nodded.

"My mother took me up the mountain one summer, when I was a girl. She showed me one just like this. The needles were orange-red. She told me then it was the tree of the fire-keepers — that every keeper's house grows one, year after year."

She paused, then added:

"Six hundred years ago, it must have grown right here — at this very stove."

A-Shi looked at that tiny seedling, its orange rim trembling at the very edge of dawn.

That orange — he knew it. It was the colour at the final moment before a coal goes dark. The colour that leapt up only when an ember sparked. The colour that crested at the very tip of a flame as it licked the air above the stove.

"This seedling..." A-Shi said, "has not gone grey."

His mother laughed softly.

"Six hundred years," she said. "A seed, once planted, is never truly dead."

The seedling grew steadily — a few inches in its first month. Every morning A-Shi poured one spoonful of stove-ash water on the roots, never more, never less. He could not explain it — but he felt certain the seedling would not need his care for long. It would grow on its own.

Before the first early summer came around, a single cone appeared at the top — very small, no bigger than his thumb's nail, and all dark red. A-Shi looked at it for a long time, knowing one day this cone would fall, open, and send down six hundred more years of fire-trees through this very family's yard.

One night Baiyin came down from the ridge, stopped at the door, and asked to look at the stove. She saw the seedling and slowly lowered herself to the ground beside it.

She rubbed one needle-tip between her fingers and said:

"Fire-seeds."

She paused, and continued:

"Six hundred years ago, when he left the house, he carried three pine needles with him. Three needles, three trees. The other two are somewhere in the world — he carried them through every place he went. He left them where they were needed most."

"And the last one —"

Baiyin looked over at A-Shi.

"The last one — he left for you."

The stove-fire burned steadily.

Baiyin drew a pinch of ash from her sleeve and scattered it around the seedling's base.

"For you," she said. "Everything that came before you — every one of the six hundred years — belongs to this house now."

A-Shi took the ash carefully.

It was light, warm, and made no sound at all as it fell.

"Whenever your stove needs new firewood," Baiyin said, her voice calm as still water, "take a branch from this tree. Pine from the fire-seed tree burns longer and deeper than any wood. Six hundred years of the world, come to rest — it would be good for the stove."

She rose and walked out into the night without another word.

Summer came slowly. A-Shi returned the ash to the stove and attended to his daily chores. The next morning the fire lit more fiercely than it ever had before — and A-Shi was not certain whether it was his imagination, but the stove seemed quieter than it had in all the time since he was born.

Out beyond the gate, something small and enduring was growing quietly — as it had done for six hundred years.

*(End of volume seventy-four · Volume XI, ninth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/75-the-character.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Seventy-five: The Character

Early summer arrived at last. The fire-seed tree in the yard pushed out its fifth needle.

Every morning A-Shi watered it with one spoonful of stove-ash slurry. Every morning he tended the stove. And every morning, after these two things, he took a sheet of trimmed coarse paper from the kitchen stack and wrote one character.

The character was 记 — to remember.

His strokes were ugly. The dot slanted. The horizontal stroke bent in the middle. The hook dragged on too long. Baiyin had taught him: the brush must already know what the heart knows, before the stroke begins.

"Fire cannot write the character itself," Baiyin said. "Fire needs somebody to write it for them."

"Write it for whom?"

"For everyone," she said.

Then she reached into her sleeve and brought out the same thin ledger — the one in which every keeper before him had, on their final night, set down one character. She turned it to the last page.

That page was blank.

Not blank — unfilled.

Baiyin laid the ledger on the hearthside, open, like an offering.

"Six hundred years," she said. "Every keeper before your time, on their final night, set down one character 记. One hundred and sixty-seven keepers, one hundred and sixty-seven characters. One page remains — and it is yours."

A-Shi stared at the empty page.

It was vast — big enough to hold anything the world had to say.

He found he could not write.

Baiyin did not press him. She sat beside him, watching the fire, and waited.

After a long silence the stove leapt once — a small, bright tongue — and settled.

"You do not have to write it today," she said. "You are learning. When you are ready, you will be ready."

A-Shi nodded.

From that day, he wrote the character 记 every day.

One month in, his strokes grew regular. Three months in, the brush felt heavy in his hand — heavier than a kitchen brush should — as though his grandmother's fingers were wrapped around his knuckles, as though his great-great-grandmother's bones were settled along the shaft.

Two months after that, a familiar scent drifted off the stove — scorched cedar, like old paper catching fire. He looked up: the fire-seed tree in the yard shivered gently, though no wind was blowing.

Baiyin came again the next morning.

She held his practice sheet up to the light and read it for a long while. Then she nodded.

"You may write it now," she said.

"Now?"

"When you feel ready," she said. "Not today — one day."

She folded the ledger and set it in his hands.

"The six hundred and second keeper," she said, "begins with this page."

A-Shi looked down at the ledger.

He understood at last: the day he wrote that final 记, the ledger would close. Six hundred and two keepers, six hundred and two characters — and on the far side of that word, the world would look different than it had looked for six hundred years.

He laid the ledger back by the stove, and turned to Baiyin.

"When the tree is grown," he said.

Baiyin nodded.

The stove burned steadily. Out in the yard, the fire-seed tree grew upward, quietly, red at the tips — six hundred years of fire, coming home to rest.

*(End of volume seventy-five · Volume XI, tenth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/76-thunderfire.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Seventy-six: Thunderfire

Halfway through June, the stove remained more than the household had ever had to cope with — and yet, on certain days, not enough.

A-Shi kept his habits: tending the fire, watering the seed-tree, practicing the character. The tree in the yard carried seven needles. The village said that summer would be quiet — even the cicadas sang less.

One evening, the clouds rolled in from the west.

Black as spilled ink on faded paper. The wind grew. A-Shi hurried to gather the washing before the storm could knock the house down — but as he was about to close the door, he looked up toward the ridge.

His pupils narrowed.

The white stone at the summit was struck by lightning.

A violet-white bolt split the night open. A heartbeat later, a thunderclap that threw every window into trembling. Even the stove flames shrank against their base.

A-Shi did not wait for the echo. He was already running for the mountain, heedless of rain.

"Into the rain?" his mother caught his sleeve. "The rain's foul —"

"The stove-fire," he said.

She let go of him.

He ran through the rain, uphill, arms scratched by scree and hail. At the ridge's midpoint he saw the white stone cracked wide open.

The night-lamp —

Gone.

The lamp-stand was still there; the glass was shattered to nothing. After six hundred years of undying flame, lightning had finished it in one stroke.

A-Shi knelt in the mud, the rain streaming down his face — though nothing could have washed away the fear in him. No insect had survived six hundred years. No wildfire. No drought. Not even an earthquake. Until — now.

Then he heard it — a gust of wind from the valley below, with the unmistakable trace of woodsmoke on it.

He followed it down the ridge, into the dark.

Another crack of thunder — the whole mountain shuddered.

He looked back — the stove-light flickered from the far east, a thin gleam that burned through the rain, as if something — hidden — was still burning.

East —

He remembered.

East of the village there was a stretch of barren flatland no one ever walked. On that day, six hundred years ago, his great-great-grandfather had given one of the first sparks to a traveller — the peddler who carried the fire to the ever-burning lamp. Beyond that, the peddler had planted a forest of pines on the flats — a forest no villager had ever entered.

The wind was coming from there.

A-Shi ran toward the light.

The wind was savage. The rain was a solid wall. He fought forward as though through smoke. At last he reached the forest's edge — and saw one single tree burn at its highest tip, a crown of steady blue flame.

The Lord of the Pines.

Before he could stand fully upright, his hand tipped — the one spoonful of ash-water still sloshing in the ladle, the last stove-ash of six hundred years, the only thing in the world that could prove him the keeper.

He poured the ash-water onto the blue flame.

The blue flame did not die. It turned gold.

Gold fire swept through the rain and the hail — and A-Shi's worn arm was burning, burning cold and hot at once.

He stood upright then, into the fire, facing it.

"Six hundred and first keeper!" he shouted into the wind. "I have come for what is yours — and what is ours!"

The flame-column wavered —

… then bent, gently, toward him — and settled — slow and steady — into the little wooden box in his chest, where the stove-seed tree's pine needle was still resting in a bolt of warm cloth.

The fire had come home.

A-Shi staggered backward out of the pine forest, clutching his chest with both hands.

The rain stopped.

Under the bruised white sky, every needle-tip in the pine flats glowed soft gold — one by one, line by line, connecting all the way down the mountain toward home.

He raised his head and saw — his mother standing on the ridge's shoulder, waiting.

"The summit was destroyed," she said.

"The stove-fire still burns," A-Shi said.

His mother froze.

She turned and walked down to the stove. In the hearth, the fire was still burning — brighter than ever — the flame long and steady and clear, as if something had chosen, once more, to carry it forward.

In the yard, the fire-seed tree shimmered — its newest needles trembling in a wind no one else could feel.

All night long.

*(End of volume seventy-six · Volume XI, eleventh part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/77-the-broken-stone.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

## Seventy-seven: The Broken Stone

Seven days after the storm, A-Shi climbed the ridge again.

He did not climb to light the lamp. The lamp — six hundred years old — had gone out in lightning the other night. Today he had one single errand: to see the stone.

The snow had mostly thawed. The mud was soft. He carried a cloth bag over his shoulder and an empty iron canister in his hand.

The white stone stood alone at the summit.

A single crack split it clean from its crown to its root — the halves still holding their old shape, as if the stone had politely declined to fall apart, preferring only to open. A-Shi put his hand into the cleft.

It was cold inside — cold, and dense with ash and tiny chipped stones.

When he drew his hand out, he found something small and hard and round in his palm — like a coal.

He bent down slowly and picked it out.

Not just any coal — it was a kindling coal: the very first coal the first keeper ever carried up this mountain, six hundred years ago.

All this time — six hundred years — it had rested unburned in the heart of the white stone, kept warm by the beacon's never-ending vigil. The lightning struck the stone, scattered the glass, scattered the fire — but it could not break what had not been burning all this time. All it could do was let it fall — out of the stone's heart, and into A-Shi's open palm.

A-Shi stood holding the coal for a long time.

The wind came up behind him and ran through his hair, and he smelled old charcoal — not fresh fire but ancient ash — the smell that would have clung to the first keeper's sleeve the day he climbed up here, before anything in the valley had yet been born.

He opened the iron canister and laid the coal at the bottom. He shook some stove-ash out of the cloth bag and wrapped it around the coal, then sealed the canister with twine, several rounds, and tied it tight.

He did not hand it to anyone.

He carried it down the mountain himself.

When he reached home, he drew a small groove into the rim of his father's old workbench — not all the way through, only a shallow intent — and rested the canister inside, on a tiny flat stone, where the hearth's warmth could reach it.

His mother asked, as she always did:

"What is that?"

"Six hundred years," A-Shi said.

His mother looked at him a long moment. She did not ask again.

That night he woke to check the stove in a half-dream. At its very center there was a steady gold glow — and in that gold glow, something was moving, just barely perceptible, at the edge of the firelight.

The coal inside the canister had lit once more.

A-Shi closed his eyes.

The next morning the canister sat untouched in its groove. The stove burned as it always had.

Only one thing had changed: the fire-seed tree in the yard wore its highest needle in a deeper red than ever before — a red like a drop of fire that refused to fall.
