*(End of volume seventy-seven · Volume XI, twelfth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/78-the-guest.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Seventy-eight: The Guest

Three weeks after the iron canister had found its groove in his father's workbench.

The stove burned steady as ever.

The fire-seed tree had stretched another inch upward; all seven needle-tips glowed dull red. Every morning small birds paused at the gate and moved on — no one knew what they were waiting for.

That afternoon, as A-Shi was laying new wood into the stove, someone came to the gate.

Not Baiyin.

A small girl, about seven, one shoe on and one off, carrying a tiny bundle over her shoulder, her forehead smudged with dried mud.

She stood at the threshold and stared at the stove without moving an inch.

"Are you the six hundred and first keeper?" she asked.

A-Shi blinked.

"Who told you that?"

The girl did not answer. She reached into her sleeve and pulled out a small piece of paper, and handed it to A-Shi — it held no words, only a rough, childishly drawn pair of eyes.

"Grandma Bai sent me," the girl said.

A-Shi's heart tightened.

Grandma Bai.

The words had not been spoken to him for years.

He took the paper. He did not know whose hand had drawn that pair of eyes — but he was certain he recognized something in them.

"What did your grandmother give you?" he asked.

The girl opened her little bundle and took out a small, yellowish parcel — careful, careful — inside it lay a single unburned coal, so dark it was almost black.

"Grandma Bai said your stove was short of fire."

A-Shi did not take it yet.

The girl continued:

"Our stove went out. My grandmother sent me over the ridge to borrow fire."

A-Shi was silent for a long moment.

Borrow fire — no one had ever said those words to him, not once, not in six hundred years of the keeper's silence.

He picked up a ladle of stove-ash water, dipped the little coal into it for a moment — the stove flared and the coal blazed to a tiny blue flame.

He wrapped the parcel up again and handed it back to the girl.

"Keep it shut," he said. "Don't open it."

The girl nodded. She stood quietly before the stove, arms held respectfully at her side.

"My grandmother said your flame is six hundred years old," the girl said.

A-Shi had no answer.

The girl tightened the strap of her parcel against her shoulder, turned, and walked away toward the mountain path. Halfway out the gate she turned back and said:

"My grandmother asked me to give you one sentence from her: the fire-seed walks on its own feet."

A-Shi stood at the stove for a long time after she was gone, with his palm resting on the stone ledge.

The warmth felt different than it had that morning — not a larger flare, but a deeper, steadier current of heat running the length of the iron canister, the groove, the workbench, and finally his own hand.

He lowered his head.

A seven-year-old girl had climbed a mountain to borrow fire for her family's stove — and as he stood there, something became terribly clear to him:

He did not hold this fire. The fire had been held — by all of them.

Six hundred years of keepers, and the fire had not only stayed. It had multiplied. It had spread from household to hearth to village to mountain ridge to the pine flats to a little girl's shoulder, and now it was going home again.

The stove was never only theirs.

It was never meant to be.

He fed a new log into the hearth and turned to his mother, who had returned from the drying rack and stood at the doorway watching.

"Mother," he said.

"Yes?"

"From now on," he said, "the stove-fire is not only ours."

His mother looked at him for a long moment.

Then she said, quietly:

"It never was."

---

*(End of volume seventy-eight · Volume XI, thirteenth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/79-into-the-outlands.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XI**: Ink Fire — the world six hundred years on

---

## Seventy-nine: Into the Outlands

Five hungry days after the third spark-week, the stove-fire fell still.

Nothing obvious changed in the hearth — the same low glow rested against the embers, the same fire lit the same corners of the kitchen. But each morning since, when A-Shi added wood before dawn, something subtle pulled at him from inside the stove itself — a weight so faint it might have been a heartbeat, except that it kept its own rhythm, not his.

Baiyin came once a month. She had never once announced what she was going to say — but he knew, now, that everything she had ever said had always, always been the same thing.

On the sixth month of this stillness, she stood at the threshold and said:

"You must leave the mountain."

"Where?" A-Shi asked.

Baiyin held up her hand and said one word:

"North."

"North?"

Baiyin reached into her sleeve and produced a small fire-sickle — a curved steel strip with a dark, half-burned pine-knot for a handle. She set it on the kitchen table with the same precise care as a woman setting a spell.

"Six hundred years ago," Baiyin said, "when the first coal was lit at this family's stove, the fire-keepers of the valley all lit their own keepers' fires — and from there, beyond the ridge, other keepers took it, and kept it, from there onward."

She lifted one finger toward the ridge beyond the village.

"The ridge has three more lamps — there, there, and there — all marked by the same six-hundred-year rule, same keepers, same silence."

A-Shi stared at the ridge.

"They have burned for six hundred years too?"

"They have burned for six hundred years too," Baiyin said.

She fixed her eyes on A-Shi.

"You may carry a jar of this stove's ash up there, to show them what you have kept — or you may walk up there just to see."

"Who will mind the stove?" A-Shi asked. "Who will mind my mother?"

Baiyin smiled faintly.

"Seven days," she said. "Everything will be in place in seven days."

She paused, turned one last time, and added — with her voice lower than before:

"The hill keeper tends the stove. The outlands keeper tends the fire. They are two different kinds of tending."

Then she walked out the door and was gone, her sandals whispering against the frozen earth.

Before she disappeared over the rise, A-Shi saw one more thing — the little empty wooden box Baiyin always carried at her waist. She was wearing it today, too.

"What is that box for?" he called after her.

Baiyin did not turn.

"You will find out," came the answer, carried back to him on the cold wind.

A-Shi stood at the stove for a long time afterward. He could feel the stove working the way it always had — the embers pulsing quietly under the new wood, the ash settling into patterns only he knew how to read.

Six hundred years of keeper work.

The stove didn't care whether he stayed or went. It cared only that someone tended it — and it remembered.

He thought: what keeps this fire alive was never that one person stayed at this stove. What keeps this fire alive is the chain, the long unbroken chain.

Six hundred years, and now the chain had loosened — and a link had shifted outward, into the mountain.

He went into the cellar. From the lowest bin he lifted the iron canister holding the six-hundred-year-old coal — the first coal ever kindled at this stove, the black coal that had been sleeping in the heart of the white stone, that lightning had let fall into his hand.

He held it for a moment. He placed it gently back inside the canister. He did not take it.

Seven days later, A-Shi laid out grandfather's traveling bag. He laid the pine needles, the ledger-page fragment, and a single sprig of the fire-seed tree's red needles on the kitchen table. His mother came out to dry the rice grain and stopped dead at the sight of them — but she didn't ask.

A-Shi asked to borrow line-ink, coarse paper, and a stopper of stove-ash. His mother put them into his hands without a word.

At dawn on the seventh day, A-Shi kneaded a fresh bundle, said goodbye to his mother, shouldered the traveling bag, and stepped through the gate.

The wind was tremendous — tremendous, enormous, as if the entire mountain had been waiting for him to walk into it.

He looked east. The ridge stood in the low sunlight — the crack in the white stone glinted with a reddish warmth, unearthly and blessed.

No one called after him. No one asked him to stay. The stove simply burned on behind him.

He closed his eyes. He bowed toward the stove, toward the kitchen, toward the roof that had sheltered this house six hundred years.

"I'll come back soon," he said.

The stove gave one small, bright leap — a snap of flame inside, like a heartbeat answering his voice.

Then A-Shi set off, eastward, toward the mountain, toward the ridge, toward the three nameless lamps beyond it.

# source: creative/novel/en/80-the-north-lamp.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XII**: The Long Journey — Fire and Ink

---

## Eighty: The Lamp of the North

On the third day after leaving home, A-Shi crossed the chimney ridge.

Beyond the ridge lay a vast black flatland nothing he had ever seen — wide and dim — and in the far distance, a row of tiny yellow-orange lights hung above the horizon.

He walked toward the nearest lamp.

An old man was already sitting before it — roughly his mother's age, with an identical iron canister on the ground at his feet.

The old man looked up at A-Shi.

"Are you the six hundred and first?" he asked.

A-Shi nodded slowly.

The old man smiled.

"Six hundred and one," he said. "Three lamps across the northern flatland. I tend the first."

A-Shi stood still.

"Your family —"

The old man nodded.

"North lamp: my house. Middle lamp: my brother's. Yours — south — is the third. Six hundred years, each family tending one, all three lighting the ridge in perfect silence, never touching but never going out."

The old man said:

"I have been looking for you."

A-Shi froze.

"For six hundred years?"

The old man shook his head.

"Until now."

The old man dipped a small ladle into his own canister — it was filled with the same ash-slurry A-Shi had kept at home — he poured a single spoonful into the lamp's basin. The lamp was very small, very steady, blue and silent.

He looked at A-Shi and said:

"Your fire — I have smelled it for years."

A-Shi asked:

"How could you know?"

The old man said:

"The wind. The north wind carries whatever is burning on its path. We have been smelling your fire for centuries — the other two lamps — and we have always known which direction it comes from."

He did not elaborate. He touched the ladle to the lamp. The tiny lamp-head flickered — low and steady, blue and ancient.

And then — across the horizon — A-Shi saw all three lamps glow at once, connected by an invisible line of warmth that swept across the flatland.

The old man handed him a smooth wooden plaque. Someone had carved it long ago, in tiny characters:

守火 — 守到全案。

Translation: Tend the fire — until it all comes home.

A-Shi asked:

"What does that mean?"

The old man answered:

"'Keep the fire until it has spread to all of them.' Your great-great-grandmother's sister told me that line when I was a child. She was never interested in the word for 'everywhere' that the world uses now. She had one word of her own — quan'an, the whole case, the entire matter. She meant: keep the fire until all of them are alight."

A-Shi accepted the plaque with both hands.

The old man watched him carefully.

"What day is it today, in your calendar?" he asked.

A-Shi considered. He did not know.

The old man smiled faintly.

"Six hundred years and one day," he said. "Your stove has been my lighthouse — I have watched it every night, every night of my life. I know you don't lack for anything. The fire keeps waiting."

The old man stood.

"Two days north from here, you will find the second lamp. Keep walking east."

He pressed a packet of kindling wood, a smaller iron canister of white ash, and a single copper-rimmed bowl into A-Shi's hands.

A-Shi accepted them.

As the old man reached to lower the lamp, he paused, cast one look eastward — at the distant red blush A-Shi could now see trembling on the far horizon, at the first breath of the sun's light — and said softly, almost to himself:

"For six hundred years, this ridge has never once been fully lit."

A-Shi looked at the three lamps beyond.

He nodded.

"Tonight," he said.

The old man smiled.

He let the lamp rest in its basin.

"Then walk on," he said.

A-Shi turned east and walked into the darkening sky, toward the second lamp.

He had only just set out — but somewhere in the world, in kitchen after kitchen, in the faint warmth of a hundred stoves, someone was already lighting their lamp, as if answering.

The horizon was coming alive.

---

*(End of volume eighty · Volume XII, first part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/81-the-second-lamp.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XII**: The Long Journey — Fire and Ink

---

## Eighty-one: The Second Lamp

A-Shi walked on north.

All day he crossed a vast ash-flat — no trees, no birds — only a straight raised road running toward the horizon. Gray stone markers stood at intervals along the verge, each engraved with two faint characters: 守火.

He read one, and walked on.

Toward sunrise on the second morning, a lamp appeared in the distance.

It burned small and steady, the same amber flame he knew from the ever-burning lamp at the crossroads and the one at his own stove-side. As he drew closer, he saw a person sitting beside it.

A young woman — about his age — in white robes with quiet, level brows, straining a basket of dried rice straw before the lamp's warmth.

She looked up without haste.

"Are you from the south?" she asked.

A-Shi nodded.

She smiled, then reached into her sleeve and drew out a small wooden plaque, identical to the one she was holding herself — engraved in tiny characters:

守火 — 守到全案。

Tend the fire — until it has reached all.

"Are you the fire of the old grandmother?" she asked.

A-Shi stared.

"Do you know her?"

The woman nodded.

"I am her younger sister," she said. "This family has kept three lamps — your one in the south, this one in the east, and one more that has been dark six hundred years to the far north. We all three grew up tending the same borrowed fire."

Her words fell into the wind and stayed there.

A-Shi said nothing for a long time.

The wind tugged gently at the second lamp, carrying the smell of wheat husk and ash.

"You have come just in time," she said.

She drew out a small black stone — exactly like the coal A-Shi had taken from the broken white stone, the same black kindling-stock.

"This lamp has not been lit for six hundred years," she said. "Your stove is the one that has never gone out. The flame you carry is the only blood all of them share."

He opened his palm to look at the stone.

She laid it in his hand.

"Six hundred years ago, your great-great-grandfather gave this fire to someone going the other way. That person passed it on to his own family — and they to theirs — until the wind reached us."

A-Shi held the stone loosely.

The woman added softly:

"Take it with you. Six hundred years is too long to wait for someone to come home."

A-Shi shook his head — slowly, firmly.

"I am A-Shi," he said.

She smiled.

"A-Shi needs no introduction. I have smelled your fire for six hundred years."

He smiled back for the first time on the road.

She gathered up her straw and motioned toward the north.

"When the sun touches the ridge, the third lamp will show faint light. It has been dark for six hundred years — no one has tried to light it since."

She looked at him.

"Everything you have ever been told about the past — it is all one story. Everyone who ever kept a fire was writing the same ledger."

A-Shi lowered his head.

"Then let me keep it," he said.

She nodded slowly.

"Light it, then."

A-Shi walked toward the third lamp.

The sun sank below the ridge. Night came down without warning.

Far off — so faint at first that he might have imagined it — a point of light pulsed in the dark — one, two, three.

The woman came out behind him, holding a burning bundle of straw like a candle against the night.

She let the flame fall into the old lamp's basin.

The third lamp lit — and burned, and burned, and burned.

Its light rose higher than anyone had ever seen a lamp burn, and it flooded the entire northern flatland. A-Shi stood before it with tears in his eyes.

Far ahead, farther than either of them could see, the line of lamps went on — north, all the way to the end of the world — every single one now alight — six hundred years finally waking after all those centuries of silence.

Three lamps burning, connected to the one in the south, connected back by smoke and wind and memory.

He looked down at the palm in his pocket, where the little black stone rested — still warm.

"Six hundred years," A-Shi said.

"Six hundred years," said the woman.

They stood together before the third lamp until dawn.

---

*(End of volume eighty-one · Volume XII, second part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/82-the-return.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XII**: The Long Journey — Fire and Ink

## Eighty-two: The Return

A-Shi walked home the way he came.

Seven full days — the first through the mist of the chimney ridge, the last across the northern ash-flat, one gradation of grey to the next, white to amber to rust. He passed three unfamiliar lamps, met two fire-keeping families he had never seen, and spent one night with a keeper older than any soul he had ever met.

On the final evening, he reached the village.

The stove was burning, as it always was. The fire-seed tree in the yard had grown a full foot taller; its needles were so red they looked like flames fixed against the evening sky. His mother was standing at the door.

"Back?" she said.

A-Shi nodded.

He set down the bundle on the kitchen floor and lifted the iron canister he had been guarding since the mountain — the one holding the coal that had fallen from the heart of the white stone, six hundred years old and never once burned.

He set it on the stove-edge. The stove flared once, gently.

"Where did you go, A-Shi?" his mother asked.

He looked at the fire for a long time before answering.

"North," he said. "Three lamps north. Same as our stove, six hundred years. I met Grandma Bai's sister."

His mother nodded.

"You are a keeper," she said. "Keepers are not brave — they are simply the ones who do not regret."

A-Shi laughed softly.

"It is true," he said. "Keepers never quite get to regret."

He set down the wooden plaque the other keeper had given him — `守火 · 守到全案` — on the kitchen table.

He took the canister from his arms and laid it before the hearth. The coal inside had not dimmed at all.

"Together," A-Shi said.

His mother looked at him for a long moment — and understood. A-Shi meant to merge the unlit coal with the stove: to complete, at last, the road six hundred years ago the first keeper had started when he lent a spark to a traveller heading south.

She fed one final log into the hearth.

The fire climbed, and climbed, and rose brighter than it ever had in daylight — and this time, it did not stop at the walls. Light burst through the roof, through the open sky, and the ever-burning lamp at the crossroads answered. The beacon on the white stone answered. Every light on the eastern ridge, every lamp in every kitchen of the valley — all at once.

A-Shi said nothing.

He stretched out one hand and touched the very centre of the flame. It did not burn him — it was warm, soft, almost weightless, as though some presence were cupping his fingers.

His mother straightened her tunic.

"You came home," she said.

A-Shi shook his head.

"I came home halfway," he said.

He turned to the fire.

"The stove never left," he said.

The stove leapt once more.

Two bowls of rice porridge were set before them at last. Every ember in the hearth glowed steady, brighter than it had on any night yet — warm enough to reach every corner of the house.

Just before dawn, his mother found a paper on the table — a single character written in a firm, unsteady young hand:

记.

Outside, the fire-seed tree stood straight and tall — and its needles burned red, six hundred years of red, all the way to the sky.

*(End of volume eighty-two · Volume XII, third part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/83-the-ledger.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XII**: The Long Journey — Fire and Ink

---

## Eighty-three: The Ledger

On the seventh morning after they came home, A-Shi woke alone.

His mother had gone out early. The kitchen held nothing but the stove and himself.

He took down the ledger from beside the stove.

The cover was thin — the kind of thing people throw away after a few years. Not much like a treasure. He opened it anyway.

On the first page a very faint character was written in ink gone pale with time:

记.

He turned the page. The next character was also 记. The third was 记. Every page — every single page — held the same character, written by a different hand, with a different temperament, in a different season of the writer's life.

He read three generations of hands.

His grandfather's stroke — short across, tall down, heavy on the finish. His father's stroke — lighter, smaller, more careful, as though conscious of taking up space. His great-great-grandfather's stroke — away on the corner — bold and unsteady.

He saw age in the ink long before he saw it in faces.

He turned page after page. His hands grew heavier with each one.

He reached the final, blank page.

He sat there for a long time.

He wanted to do something grand — to write the character with a flourish nobody could forget, to put six hundred years of ink into one gesture. Then he understood: nobody wanted that. The last keeper had not tried to out-do the others.

A ray of sunlight fell through the kitchen window and lit the center of the stove-fire.

He took the brush from the holder, set it in the inkstone, and wrote.

Three strokes. One breath. The same character.

The stroke for 记 (to remember) — a little wider on the right than it was on the left, tilting slightly into the habit he had practised a thousand times.

He set the brush down.

Three strokes. One breath.

He looked at what he had written.

记.

Horizontal stroke his grandfather's habit. Vertical his father's. Ink his own.

From the doorway behind him:

"You finally did it."

Baiyin.

He didn't ask when she had arrived.

"You see?" he said, without turning. "It is a little of all of them."

Baiyin came to his side and studied the character.

"Beautiful," she said.

He shook his head.

"I wrote it by hand. Wait until the village sees it."

Baiyin laughed.

"The village never will," she said. "This book is yours alone."

She held it for a moment, then handed it back.

"Tomorrow," she said, "you begin the next book."

"The next book?"

"The one they are all waiting for," Baiyin said.

He asked nothing more.

That evening the stove burned higher than it usually did, and the fire-seed tree rustled though there was no wind. He understood that the next volume of the ledger was already open — and that he would never be able to refuse the next step.

Some things are not inherited.

They are kindled.

---

*(End of volume eighty-three · Volume XII, fourth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/84-the-cone.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XII**: The Long Journey — Fire and Ink

---

## Eighty-four: The Coning

On the morning of the Winter Solstice, A-Shi went out to gather wood, and saw one cone on the fire-seed tree.

He drew closer. A small thing, barely the size of his thumb's knuckle, painted a dark umber red.

His mother came to the doorway with a bowl in her hand and saw it too.

"What is that on the tree?"

She said nothing for a long while.

"It is the fire-seed tree's first cone," she said at last. "The sign of the next keeper."

A-Shi said nothing.

"Your great-grandmother's mother used to say: when the fire-seed tree cones for the first time in its life, it is telling the family — the next keeper is on the road."

Her tone did not change. Her eyes went soft.

Snow began to fall.

The stove burned quietly all night.

At some point long past midnight, A-Shi opened his eyes to find a man seated before the stove.

Middle-aged. Thin. A grey robe. His breathing was as steady as the stove's breath.

The man noticed A-Shi was awake. He smiled faintly.

"I have been sent," he said, "to bring the ledger."

A-Shi asked, "Who are you?"

"I am called Guo Ke," the man said. "They call me the Walker. My business is the sixth-hundred-and-second keeper's ledger."

"What ledger?"

He laid a bundle of pages on the table — yellowed, brittle with age, filled with characters nobody living had ever read out loud.

"Every keeper you will ever know in the next book," Guo Ke said, "kept their own ledger."

A-Shi looked down at the paper and felt the weight of ink six hundred years old settling in his hands.

He flipped to the first page.

A single character: 记.

Every page after — the same.

And then, deeper in, his own grandfather's hand appeared — his short-across stroke, light and telling. After that, his father's lighter ink.

And then a blank page.

"This is yours," Guo Ke said.

"What does it want?"

"The next page."

A-Shi looked at the blank. His hand began to tremble without his willing it.

Baiyin had come and gone like weather. His mother was always there but never spoke of it. The tree had coned twice since midwinter. Every detail was running ahead of him now.

"You will receive it when you are ready," Guo Ke said, "not before."

He prepared to leave.

At the gate, he paused.

"Six hundred years," he said. "You are not alone in this. You are only the next bloom of this tree."

He slipped into the snow.

A-Shi stood alone in the doorway.

On the table, the ledger waited — six hundred and one names already written in it.

And one.

His mother joined him in the doorway.

Neither of them spoke.

The Third Six Hundredth Winter passed the way all six hundred years had done — softly.

At first light, by the stove, the tree gave up a second cone.

It was redder, still, than the first.

The keeper's ledger on the table had been waiting six hundred years to close the last page — and was ready to begin the next.

A-Shi sealed it with his hand.

His mother said quietly, "Keeper."

He nodded.

He did not know what came next for him — but the tree knew, and the ledger knew, and the stove knew. He had been a keeper from the moment he first touched it — he simply had not known how to look back.

The dawn broke clean over the ridge. Across it, one lamp lit by another, six hundred years of fire now burned bright enough to see from the stars.

Someone had written it all in.

---

*(End of volume eighty-four · Volume XII, fifth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/85-the-letter.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XII**: The Long Journey — Fire and Ink

---

## Eighty-five: The Letter

Three days after the Solstice, A-Shi decided to write to Baiyin.

He reached under the stove ledge for a square of trimmed kitchen paper — just the right size — and laid it flat on the table. The fire-light gilded it orange. He set down the brush.

The stroke began — a single short horizontal, heavy and brief, as if stamped out of his grandfather's hand.

He stared at it.

His grandfather's stroke, yes. His father's vertical, yes. His own ink — and it was also his grandfather's ink, mixed from the same stove ash.

He crossed out the横.

He crossed out the竖.

He thought — about everything he wanted to say: about the ridge, the ledger, his mother's silence, the three days he had not slept.

He crossed out everything he wanted to say.

He picked up the brush once more — this time, his own stroke.

His was lighter than his father's, faster than his grandfather's.

He wrote one line:

The fire will not go out.

Nothing else.

Not a word more.

He folded the paper into quarters, then folded it again until it was thin enough to tuck into the chest-bag — the way Baiyin always did her own letters. He slipped it under the stove's ledge, where his grandfather's book was kept — where the ledger lived — where his own pulse had somehow started leaping in time with the stove.

His mother, sweeping the floor, picked up the folded square. She looked at it — then at him — said nothing. She set it aside, beside the ledger.

That night the stove leapt once, sharply.

A-Shi looked up.

The ledger opened itself.

On the final page, in real ink — not copied, not simulated, but truly his grandfather's hand — letters appeared that have not been there before:

A sealed book, and it wakes itself.

He did not touch it.

He waited.

The next morning the page was blank again — except one line:

I will be the one to see it inside the ledger.

Baiyin's brush.

A-Shi looked at the stove.

He set the folded letter down on the stove's hottest corner, closed his hand tight around it, and took a long breath.

Sealed.

Sealed for good.

Six hundred years and the flames still rose.

*(End of volume eighty-five · Volume XII, sixth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/86-the-lamp.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XII**: The Long Journey — Fire and Ink

---

## Eighty-six: The Lamp

On Baiyin's last visit, the light in the house did not go out — not even for a moment.

Baiyin came in from the mountain, still cold from the north wind, still carrying pine ash on her sleeves. She sat down by the stove. She did not speak for a long time.

A-Shi watched her. She was doing the same thing she had been doing for six hundred years — sitting with the fire.

"Have you given it to them yet?" she asked.

He knew what she meant. The letter he had written to her, the one sealed inside the ledger — the one his grandfather had written through him in the last hour of the night.

"Almost," A-Shi said.

Baiyin nodded. Her face was calm. The fire lit something deep behind her eyes that A-Shi had never seen before — and he had not seen either since the night he woke up and she was sitting beside him.

"The ledger wants it now," she said.

A-Shi looked at the table. The ledger lay there, slightly open, its pages still warm from the stove. He had not closed it. He had not wanted to close it.

"When you give it to them," Baiyin said, "they will know you."

He did not ask who 'they' were. He already knew.

Six hundred years of keepers who had sat exactly here — who had kept this same stove — whose eyesight had faded into this same warm orange light, who had pressed their fingers to this same iron ledge, who had given their inheritance to this same fire.

Baiyin stood. She went to the doorway, stopped, turned.

"Tonight will be six hundred years from the first time a keeper stayed up too late waiting for the fire to speak," she said.

He was very still.

She was not smiling. She was looking at him the way a mother looks at a child before the child learns the lesson the mother learned at the same age, in the same kitchen, years ago — except Baiyin had lived through six hundred years of that watching.

"The ledger is nearly full," she said. "When it is full, the next keeper begins. It has always been so."

She reached the threshold.

"Goodnight, little one," she said.

She left.

After she was gone the stove leapt very high, higher than ever before, splashing orange across the ceiling and the walls. For one instant, every keeper's face was visible in the glow — six hundred years of faces, each one shaped by the same fire, each one lit by the same long vigil.

Then the flame settled.

A-Shi sat alone before the stove.

He unfolded the ledger and set it on the table. He opened the last page — already covered with his own hand, his grandfather's hand, Baiyin's hand, six hundred years of hands overlapping on the same sheet. He took the letter from beneath the stove's rim and set it on the ledger.

He closed his eyes.

The stove glowed hot but gentle in the dark.

When he opened his eyes, the letter was not on the table.

And the ledger's final page had gone blank — perfectly, serenely blank. Ready for whatever came next.

He knew what that meant.

He knew exactly what that meant.

The ledger, now empty, could accept the next thing — the thing he had not known he was waiting to write.

He settled back against the table's edge.

The stove-fire burned with a colour he had never seen in six hundred years — gold-white, clearer than any flame he had witnessed before — pure, ancient, enough to see all the way from the mountain.

The next day had not yet come.

---

*(End of volume eighty-six · Volume XII, seventh part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/87-the-grandmother.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction

---

## Eighty-seven: The Grandmother

On the third morning after Baiyin left, A-Shi found something on the kitchen table he had never noticed before — a folded square of old cloth, thin as a letter, wrapped with string that had gone brown with age.

He opened it slowly.

Inside, a single strip of paper remained — most of it had turned to dust where the flames had brushed it, but the characters across the top held firm:

Baiyin's hand.

Three characters.

Bai Nainai — Grandmother Bai.

A-Shi stared at them until the stove leapt, once, behind him.

He turned. The flame burned steady and patient, gold as always — but taller than before. Six hundred years of keepers had tended this same fire, and none of them had understood what it was. Or they had, and none had been able to say it aloud.

A-Shi reached behind him and touched the stove's edge. Warm, as it always was.

That was when it came to him — all six hundred years at once, a lifetime he had not lived but had inherited — every keeper's name in the ledger, every story about the fire that never went out, every generation of families who had called that stove their grandmother:

The fire was the family.

His grandfather's grandfather's grandfather — the one who lit the first stove — his father's father, his father, himself — all of them wearing the same kitchen's firelight, all of them warmed by the same long table:

Grandmother Bai — the one who never spoke, the one whose name appeared on no ledger, the one who never aged, the one who never left the stove.

Baiyin's strip was nearly gone. On the very end, in the same faded ink, one more line — small enough to forget, large enough to tell the whole truth:

The fire is Grandmother. Grandmother is the fire.

A-Shi did not move for a long time.

His mother came in with soup. She looked at the cloth in his hand, at the tiny strip of paper, at his face — and knew.

She sat down across from him.

"Six hundred years," A-Shi said.

"Six hundred years," she said.

Neither of them said any more.

The stove rose steady and gold. The yard snow had nearly stopped; a thin sun edged through the window and touched the table — touching the ledger — touching the strip of cloth — touching everything A-Shi had ever been or ever would be.

Baiyin's question returned to him suddenly: Have you given it to them yet?

He had.

They had answered him.

He closed his eyes and bowed his head. Six hundred years of silence bowed with him.

The stove flared once — and settled low, content — a grandmother tucking in her grandson for the night.

*(End of volume eighty-seven · Volume XII, eighth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/88-the-newcomer.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles

## Eighty-eight: The Newcomer

On the second morning after the snow, the door opened once.

A boy was standing in the doorway.

Eight or nine years old, head shaved, robes washed to the colour of ash. He held one hand pressed into his own sleeve, the other wrapped around nothing — a child who had not learned yet to bring anything except himself.

His eyes went first to A-Shi, then to the fire, then back to A-Shi.

"Is this your fire?" he asked.

A-Shi nodded.

The boy reached into his sleeve and brought out a folded slip of paper — no bigger than two fingers wide, folded so tightly it was almost worn through.

He unfolded it with both hands.

Written on it in brush strokes so young and unsteady they were almost unreadable:

I am small fire.

A-Shi turned the paper over. On the back, in a different hand — neater, older:

Number Six Hundred and Two.

A-Shi did not move.

The boy did not move either. He waited the way only someone raised in this valley could wait — trained to silence, trained to stay exactly where he was, trained to never go past the threshold until the fire gave him leave.

"Where did you come from?" A-Shi asked.

The boy pointed without speaking, toward the ridge.

"I stood outside all night," he said. "I was afraid to come in."

A-Shi felt something open in him.

The boy's voice was not quavering. He was steady as the stove, practiced as a monk, still as a lantern in a closed room. There was nothing uncertain in him at all.

"I am small fire," the boy said quietly, "because Grandma Bai sent me."

A-Shi did not answer.

The stove leapt.

The room flooded so white it might have been noon in August — and inside that light, in the folds of shadow at the edge of the kitchen, A-Shi saw himself as clearly as he had ever seen anything. His mother grown old beyond recognition. His grandmother who never aged. The boy grown into a man he had not yet met. Every keeper's face stacked inside this stove — she stood behind him and answered for all of them.

He understood everything then.

The boy stepped inside the house.

He went to the stove, knelt, and opened his small wrapped bundle — inside lay a single dark ember, cold as a pebble.

He held it out toward A-Shi.

"Grandmother Bai told me to bring it," the boy said. "You already have yours."

A-Shi reached for the iron canister on the stove's ledge.

The coal inside had gone not dark, but bright.

He looked at the boy.

"Your grandmother sent you a name," A-Shi said.

The boy nodded.

"She sent you two."

A-Shi took out the coal. He put it beside the one the boy had brought.

The two coals lay together on the stove's iron lip — two grains of fire separated by six hundred years, meeting for the first time.

The boy said, very quietly:

"They are the same grandmother."

A-Shi did not ask what he meant.

He placed both coals inside the hearth.

The stove did not merely flare.

It bloomed — a full orange-white exhalation, every grain of pitch and every splinter of pine in the stove catching at once, the whole fire-family rising together, all of them seeing all of them, six hundred years stored in one perfect instant of light —

And then settling, together, back into one warm, breathing flame.

Outside, across the ridge and across the valley, every fire that still burned responded in kind — the ever-burning lamp, the beacon on the white stone, every stove and every pipe and every candle across every mountain line —

Six hundred years, one breath, everyone holding hands in the same light.

The boy sat down beside A-Shi at the stove's edge, the way every keeper before him had done.

He smiled at the fire.

He did not ask A-Shi to teach him.

The fire was already teaching him.

*(End of volume eighty-eight · Volume XII, ninth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/89-new-fire.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XIII**: New Fire — the Seven-Hundred Year

---

## Eighty-nine: New Fire

Xiao Huo's first month at A-Shi's house was spent on a single task.

A-Shi set him the same question every morning:

"Watch the stove."

Xiao Huo sat at the hearth and watched. For the first few days, when A-Shi asked what he saw, he said:

"The fire moves."

A-Shi asked:

"Why does it move?"

The boy considered this for a long time.

"…Because someone is in it."

A-Shi nodded.

A month later he asked the same question again. Xiao Huo thought for a while and said:

"There isn't just one person in it — there are many, many people. Taking turns."

A-Shi smiled and did not press him.

Two weeks after that, Xiao Huo added:

"They are all like me in there — everyone tending the fire, everyone who stands before a stove like this, everyone who ever was — like everyone who has ever spent this long in front of a stove like this, in any house, in any year."

A-Shi laughed quietly to himself.

"And what did it call you?" he asked.

The boy thought carefully.

"It did not name me," Xiao Huo said. "It only nodded. Many times."

A-Shi did not ask his mother what that meant. His mother only smiled. She did not explain.

Some days later, A-Shi brought out the small black stone from the wooden box his mother had saved — the one brought up to him by someone from Grandma Bai's house, a fragment of the six-hundred-year-old coal — and set it down in front of Xiao Huo.

Xiao Huo looked at it. He did not blink.

"Is this from Grandma Bai?" he asked.

A-Shi nodded.

The boy was silent a long moment.

"Then this stone can tell you one thing," Xiao Huo said. "Grandma Bai has never stopped for six hundred years."

A-Shi asked, "How do you know?"

Xiao Huo looked at him steadily.

"Because I call her by her name," he said.

"What do you call her?"

The boy lowered his voice.

"I call her Grandma Bai," he said. "Even when I speak to her myself — I always say Grandma Bai."

The stove flared — a sensation stronger than anything A-Shi had felt in many years.

His mother came through from the kitchen. She saw Xiao Huo and stopped. A faint, almost transparent light clung to the edges of the boy's shoulders — something A-Shi had seen before in Baiyin's presence.

She said nothing. She went back.

Some time later, A-Shi asked Xiao Huo:

"Who is Grandma Bai?"

The boy did not hesitate.

"Grandma Bai is the fire," Xiao Huo said. "Grandma Bai is this stove. Grandma Bai is the six hundred years of keepers — every single one of them."

A-Shi's eyes began to sting.

His mother came closer, laid one hand gently on his shoulder.

The stove burned steadily.

A-Shi opened the ledger — the one with all six hundred years of signatures, the empty page at the very end — and placed it on the table before Xiao Huo.

"This is the next book," he said. "When it is filled, this stove will know what to do next."

Xiao Huo nodded solemnly.

"Next time you write in it," A-Shi said, "write your own name."

Xiao Huo looked at the page for a long while.

He did not write.

He only turned and looked at the fire.

"Not today," he said. "Another day."

The stove rose very high.

The fire-seed tree outside changed its needles — almost all of them turned deepest crimson.

On the far ridge, the smoke from the beacon was gone. In its place the whole valley was burning quietly — from the ever-burning lamp, from every stove in every household, from

A-Shi's stove, from Xiao Huo's new fire.

Six hundred years became the seven-hundredth year.

New fire.

New keeper.

---

*(End of volume eighty-nine · Volume XIII, first part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/90-ink.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XIII**: New Fire — the Seven-Hundred Year

---

## Ninety: Ink

On the winter solstice all the stove's flames had been bright for three months.

Snow was falling heavily that night. A-Shi's mother was back from the mountain carrying wood, resting by the stove. She watched Xiao Huo standing before the ledger's last page.

He was holding the brush.

A-Shi had not set the ink already. His mother was watching too. The stove ticked once.

Xiao Huo dipped the brush in the ink, raised it to the last empty page of the ledger, and wrote.

What he wrote was not a character he had ever seen.

He wrote:

Fire.

Not the ordinary word for it. Not any character. It was something the fire had transmitted through his hand — a single stroke of pure combustion, incised into the paper, glowing faintly red as the ink spread.

The moment the stroke touched the page, all six hundred years of fire in the room leapt at once.

His mother pressed both hands to her chest.

Outside, the fire-seed tree shook once — every needle on every branch trembling together.

Far away, over the cloud-heavy ridge, no light shone and no dark lay.

The stove flared so high the entire kitchen turned white.

A-Shi saw — in that instant, in the heart of the flame — a silhouette standing. Not clearly. Not completely. An old, old figure — ancient beyond measure, the feet planted into the earth, the silhouette the shape of someone made from the fire itself — she looked once at Xiao Huo, once at A-Shi, once at the whole kitchen — and vanished.

The stove flickered.

All through the night, deep midnight, the fire burned steady and silent.

A tear slipped down Xiao Huo's cheek.

A-Shi sat down beside him, wiped it away gently.

"Did you see who?" he asked.

Xiao Huo wiped his own eyes.

He was quiet a long while.

Then he said:

"I saw all six hundred years of them."

A-Shi did not say anything.

Xiao Huo said softly:

"They are all here."

He paused.

"I could smell their hands."

He set the brush down.

He stood, slowly, cradling the ledger against his chest.

Outside, the snow fell deeper over the yard, over the fire-seed tree, over the house and the stove, over the two generations of keepers keeping the same watch — one generation closing, one generation opened, all of it burning, all of it one breath of the same fire.

The stove kept the fire as the stove always had.

It would keep it still.

---

*(End of volume ninety · Volume XIII, second part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/91-the-bestowal.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XIII**: New Fire — the Seven-Hundred Year

---

## Ninety-one: The Bestowal

The kitchen bowl tipped itself into the sink, gently, by itself.

A-Shi had just finished the evening pot. His mother came out of the back room carrying two pairs of chopsticks and two clean bowls — glanced at the stove — then placed both in A-Shi's hands.

"Today's errand," she said quietly.

A-Shi looked at the bowls.

"The relay station at the mountain," his mother added, as though that needed no explanation. She handed him a small bundle woven tightly from coarse rice straw — something flat inside, no bigger than a man's palm.

"The fire waits for you," she said.

A-Shi understood before she finished speaking. He tucked the bundle against his chest. Xiao Huo was already at the doorway, waiting.

They walked up the old mountain road. It was spring; the first peach blossoms were open, and a warm morning wind carried their scent across the valley.

The relay station was at the far side of the ridge — a single-room, three window situation with one roof, one door, one stove. No plaque. No lock. The doorway faced south.

Baiyin sat in front of the relay station's stove. She was tending a tiny wooden plaque suspended inside the stove, watching it blacken and not burn.

A-Shi set the straw bundle down in front of her on the kitchen table.

He did not speak.

Baiyin reached over and slowly unraveled the straw twine.

The bundle opened.

Inside, there was nothing.

Pure white emptiness; a clean, perfect void; an empty box of flawless grain — a gift leaving its shape in the wood itself.

Baiyin smiled faintly at A-Shi.

"Sixty-first generation of keepers," she said.

"It is empty."

"It cannot be otherwise."

Xiao Huo looked at the floor.

"There is no fire in it at all," he said.

"That is the gift," Baiyin said gently. "For six hundred years, the fire has only ever been borrowed. Every keeper passed it on to the next. Today you returned it to the one who first carried it."

A-Shi asked, puzzled but listening:

"Then what is inside this one?"

"You poured it into the stove six hundred years ago," Baiyin said softly. "Or six hundred years ago the fire poured itself into you."

Xiao Huo looked at his shoes.

"After six hundred years," Baiyin said quietly, "the fire and its keepers have finally arrived at the relay station."

A-Shi listened.

He really listened.

"But Grandmother Bai does not live here," he said, turning to Baiyin. "I never saw her. Do you know where she is?"

Baiyin smiled.

She set the empty box back in front of him.

"The fire," she said, "is where it has always been."

Xiao Huo lifted his head.

"She is in the stove," he said breathlessly.

Baiyin nodded very slowly.

"Six hundred years," she said, "and it has carried you all this way until you could stand before it and still know its name."

A-Shi looked down at his hands.

"I never called it Grandmother Bai," he whispered.

"Every keeper never calls her by name," Baiyin said. "They only call her what they always knew."

Xiao Huo settled back against the relay station's threshold.

"Six hundred years," he said, "and all she ever wanted was for the stove to be kept."

Baiyin nodded.

They walked back together, all three of them — A-Shi, Xiao Huo, and Baiyin — down the mountain road, along the crest, back into the trees, until they emerged at the kitchen door at dusk.

The stove was burning brightly, as it always had been.

Xiao Huo stopped at the threshold and stepped forward.

A-Shi stopped at the threshold.

Baiyin remained outside, looking in.

Xiao Huo stepped forward and stood before the stove.

He pressed his open palm to the stove's side. The iron hummed under his hand.

"Grandmother," he said. "We are home."

---

*(End of volume ninety-one · Volume XIII, third part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/92-the-hearth.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XIII**: New Fire — the Seven-Hundred Year

---

## Ninety-two: The Hearth

Late spring brought new leaves to every branch of the fire-seed tree.

Their needles were not the red of summer nor the amber of autumn — they were a deep clear gold, golden enough to look transparent against the sun. From across the yard, the tree glowed as though lit from within.

The stove had been burning for six hundred years, and would be six hundred and one more year when the seventh century of keeping was finished.

A-Shi sat at the stove every morning before sunrise. Xiao Huo sat beside him. His mother cleared the table behind them. None of the three of them spoke.

One morning his mother set aside the old bowl she had been using for years — the same bowl six hundred years of keepers had used — and walked to the stove. She reached her hand inside and withdrew one single thing: a lump of charcoal, no bigger than a nut, covered in grey-white ash so old it had gone almost soft to the touch.

She held it in her hand. It did not burn her.

Xiao Huo's eyes went wide.

A-Shi steadied his breath.

He lifted the coal from her open palm. Even this close, he could feel its heat without pain — as if the fire itself had grown soft from six hundred years of practice.

He saw, now: this was the same coal from the very beginning — the piece of kindling the first keeper had carried up the mountain, the piece that had burned — quieter and slower than any other — for six hundred years, waiting in the stove's deepest corner for the one who would finally recognize it.

The stove leapt once.

The yard's needles shivered.

A-Shi looked at his mother.

She placed the coal in Xiao Huo's hand.

The little boy held it, held it without being burned — and without flinching.

Six hundred years found their continuation in the hand of a child sitting before the same stove, the same fire, the same six-hundred-year gesture.

A-Shi closed his eyes.

He knew what the stove had been waiting for.

Xiao Huo placed the coal in the hearth, beside the embers, where six hundred years of keepers had placed their own.

The fire did not double in brightness. It did not flare dramatically. It settled — warm, even, steady, as though it had finally received what it had been waiting for all these years.

His mother turned once — glanced at A-Shi — then went back to her work.

The house filled with the scent of woodsmoke and pine resin.

Outside, the courtyard glistened in the spring light — the fire-seed tree's needles glowing golden against the blue sky.

A-Shi sat at the stove and felt the heat steady against his shins.

He knew, now, without any doubt — the story of this fire was not over. This house — this hearth — this six hundred years of fire will continue to burn. And the seventh century is just its beginning.

---

*(End of volume ninety-two · Volume XIII, fourth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/93-the-night.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XIII**: New Fire — the Seven-Hundred Year

---

## Ninety-three: The Night

For the first time, Xiao Huo added the firewood himself.

His mother told him beforehand — not in words, but in the way she held out the bundle of split pine beside the stove. A-Shi did not intervene.

Xiao Huo took one piece, set it across the mouth of the stove, and let it go.

The fire swallowed it without a sound.

He stood very still, listening to the fire burn. After a while, when the coals had settled, he added a second piece.

The flames did not roar. They only brightened.

That night, as the household settled, A-Shi sat down at the hearth to rest. Xiao Huo sat beside him. Their mother dusted the kitchen. The house was quiet, steady, grounded.

An hour later a dry wind rose outside, brushing along the walls. The stove kept burning. The lights did not change.

Xiao Huo leaned closer to the stove. The heat came to meet his open palm.

Their mother brought a bowl of porridge from the stove's edge and set it between them.

She had noticed A-Shi drifting, one arm against his knee, his head drooping forward as if bone-deep asleep. She watched Xiao Huo reach out to steady him silently.

Xiao Huo was very small. He tapped A-Shi's shoulder lightly with one finger. A-Shi did not stir. He tapped again. A-Shi's eyes fluttered only.

Xiao Huo thought for a long moment.

He reached out, and touched the stove itself — fingertips against iron.

A hum quivered up through his hand — a low, almost inaudible vibration, like a heart in a deep, deep sleep.

That was how Grandmother Bai had always sounded in the stove. Xiao Huo had not known it until now.

He wrapped A-Shi in his own arms — small, thin arms, warm from the kitchen's everyday sun — and pressed his back against the stove's warm iron ledge.

A-Shi smiled in his sleep.

He said something brief and quiet, as though speaking to someone not present.

Xiao Huo whispered into the warm kitchen:

"Grandmother."

The stove answered — a faint tremor rose from the deepest layer of the flames.

Xiao Huo understood.

The stove had always been here. But someone had been holding it, all this time, from six hundred years away.

Baiyin. A-Shi's grandmother.

The fire had been walking beside the family all the while.

He sat a long time beside the stove. The dawn came slowly. The wind outside had settled. Outside, the fire-seed tree swayed faintly in what little breeze remained.

When his mother woke, the stove was burning higher than ever.

Xiao Huo had been sitting there all night. He was not tired. It was as though the fire had given him something to hold onto.

His mother took one look at him and understood everything.

She brought a bowl of rice porridge to Baiyin, who had come and gone quietly while they slept.

He was already up.

She set the bowl down before the stove.

The fire did not flicker.

She did not ask him anything.

Six hundred years of tending had never once let the family go.

The fire had never stopped.

A-Shi shifted against the stove and slept on.

---

*(End of volume ninety-three · Volume XIII, fifth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/94-the-mothers-stove.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XIII**: New Fire — the Seven-Hundred Year

## Ninety-four: The Mother's Stove

When the light in the kitchen began to change, the mother was already awake.

A-Shi came down the stairs carrying a bowl of his own and stopped at the threshold.

She was on her knees in front of the stove.

She did not turn around when she heard him.

The stove burned low and gold, as it always burned in the morning, before anyone else was awake to see it.

His mother had a small wooden bucket in one hand and a bundle of dry rice-husk in the other. She tipped a little of the husk into her palm, and let it flicker into the stove.

A wreath of incense lifted from the stove.

"For?" A-Shi asked.

His mother did not turn around.

"Grandmother Bai," she said.

He did not ask which grandmother.

They were on the same ground now. Six hundred years had gone through their house — generations of keepers, each of whom had sat right here in front of this stove, each of whom had known his mother's mother and her mother's mother before her.

A-Shi thought about six hundred years: all the meals shared, all the winters, all the nights when nobody slept before dawn. He thought about the names he had only heard from his grandfather, who had only heard them from his grandfather — names of people who had sat right here, smelling exactly what she was smelling now, feeling what she was feeling now.

He understood, suddenly, why his mother woke so early every day.

She lit this stove before anyone else's eyes were open so it would be burning before anyone else needed it — not for them, but for the ones who came before.

The stove was still warm from yesterday.

His mother set the bucket back on the shelf where it always lived.

"Your grandfather," she said, without turning, "lit this stove every morning for forty years."

A-Shi knew it.

"I was born after the third year of the sixth hundred years," she said.

He did not know she remembered everything so clearly.

"The house has always been the same house," she said. "The stove has always been the same stove. The grandmother has always been the same grandmother."

The stove gave one small, contented crack.

Outside, the sky brightened a little more.

A-Shi sat down on the stool where he always ate.

His mother finally turned around and looked at him.

She smiled very faintly.

"Six hundred years," she said.

He nodded.

"Six hundred years," he repeated.

They did not look at each other directly, either of them.

The stove burned steadily, steady as it always had been, as it always would be.

---

*(End of volume ninety-four · Volume XIII, sixth part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/95-the-following-fire.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction

## Ninety-five: The Following Fire

Three months after Baiyin's last visit, a letter came.

The letter was almost empty — nothing but a single line of text across the middle.

A-Shi held the paper to the stove to read it in the orange light — and discovered, as he tilted the page, that there was no ink on it at all.

But the paper was warm.

Not warm from his hands or from the kitchen stove. It was warm because it had been carrying something inside it — something that had not yet become anything a reader could recognise as a letter.

The fire had written it.

A whole six hundred years of keepers had signed their names — had written wordlessly — and the ink had never left the paper.

Mother walked in, soapsuds on her hands, and saw him standing with the letter open.

She noticed immediately.

"That paper," she said. "It was never written on."

He nodded.

"The fire wrote it without writing."

She dried her hands with a kitchen cloth. She set it down.

"Everyone who ever tended this stove," she said, "told it their names. The stove carried them. The stove carried you. The stove is carrying you."

She reached into the fireplace — not into the fire, but beside it, in the folded wool on the shelf — and drew out something wrapped in three layers of cloth.

She unwrapped it.

Inside lay a small rough-hewn skillet — the same shape as the one resting inside the hearth, the same kind of iron as the iron of the stove itself. The iron was still warm.

She placed it in his hands.

"Six hundred years of fire," she said gently.

He could not speak.

She looked at him steadily.

"Seven hundred years," she said.

Xiao Huo, who had been listening from the doorway, toddled forward and nestled the skillet beside him against A-Shi's chest.

The stove burned quietly in the background.

The eastern beacon burned across the ridge.

Grandmother was not absent.

She had been here all along.

---

*(End of volume ninety-five · Volume XIV, first part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/96-baiyin.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles

---

## Ninety-six: Baiyin

Baiyin came without warning.

She arrived at midday carrying nothing — a single bundle, nothing more, nothing of herself that needed to arrive with her.

The stove saw her immediately. It rose in a welcoming curve — one long, gentle gesture, a wave of gold reaching up toward the ceiling.

A-Shi stopped in the doorway.

His mother was inside, sweeping the floor. Neither of them said anything about Baiyin standing in the threshold with the fire reaching up for her.

Baiyin set her bundle down by the stove — she did not open it.

She simply stood there, watching the fire.

The stove flickered once. A single tongue of flame leapt upward.

She reached out one hand — hovering just above the rim, hovering in the heat — and the stove breathed.

She pulled her hand back slowly.

She turned to A-Shi, and she smiled, very slightly, very carefully.

"The fire remembers you," she said.

He knew it did.

"The fire has always remembered," she said.

He did not know that, until she said it.

His mother brought a bowl of water and set it on the table.

She poured a little of that water into the stove — she had done it every morning for six hundred years — and the fire leapt up, gold and warm, and Baiyin closed her eyes for a moment with the gesture of someone who had been carrying too much for too long.

She drank the water.

Then she stood up.

"I have come to see the fire," she said. "To see it with you, one last time."

"Yes," A-Shi said.

"Tomorrow, I must leave," she said.

He nodded.

She walked to the door, turned back once, and then left.

The stove flickered after she was gone.

In the evening, his mother cooked the usual meal. The fire burned as it had always burned — steady, warm, golden.

But A-Shi understood something now, for the first time.

The fire had never stopped. It had never gone away. It had never needed to be relit, or reminded, or told to stay.

The fire had been here all along. Six hundred years of faithful, patient light, right here in this kitchen.

And now, for reasons he did not yet understand, it had remembered something.

He did not know what yet.

But tomorrow, he would learn.

*(End of volume ninety-six · Volume XIV, second part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/97-in-the-light.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XIV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — the Continuing Volume

---

## Ninety-seven: In the Light

A-Shi woke before dawn into a light that had no source.

He did not understand at first what was happening. The window was bright — but it was too early for the sun, and his mother had not yet risen to light more firewood.

It was coming from the stove.

The stove was glowing with a white light that rose through the floor, through the walls, through the wooden beams of the house, filling the kitchen with a radiance too steady and too clean to be any ordinary flame.

His mother stepped out of the back room and stood in the doorway.

She was barefoot. Her hair was still loose. She had not combed it yet. She looked like a woman who had been pulled out of bed by the fire itself.

She did not speak.

A-Shi followed her into the kitchen.

The light was too warm to be fire. It was warmer than any stove he had ever known.

She stepped forward and looked directly into the stove — the way she looked into it every morning, the way every keeper for six hundred years had looked into the stove they had been given to tend.

Then she knelt.

A-Shi knelt with her.

The stove's light filled the room entirely.

His mother's face was shining.

A-Shi looked at her face and understood, finally, what had always been happening in this kitchen — what had always been passed down — what had always been the reason six hundred years of women and men and children had woken before dawn and knelt before the stove and warmed their hands and said nothing.

What they had been doing was praying.

Six hundred years of prayers, spoken in silence, passed from hand to hand.

The stove had been answering every single one of them.

Now, at dawn, at the beginning of the seven-hundredth year, the stove was finally answering back.

White-gold light spilled out onto the floor, across the wood table, across their two faces — the first ray of the year — the first light of the year — no before the sun, but from the stove itself.

His mother put her hand out into the light.

She waited.

The stove did not burn her.

She put both hands into the light.

She wept — softly, without sound, without moving.

A-Shi did not know what she was seeing.

But he understood: she had been waiting for this her whole life.

He reached out his hand into the light beside hers.

The warmth came through his fingers — through his wrist — into his chest — and settled there, steady, like a heartbeat that had finally found its rhythm.

He stayed with her for a long time in that light.

Outside, the sky began to change.

The stove's light met the first real light of the morning coming through the eastern window.

Two lights met on the kitchen floor.

One did not cancel the other.

Both burned together.

---

*(End of volume ninety-seven · Volume XIV, third part.)*

# source: creative/novel/en/98-the-loft.md
# The Unchanted Chronicle of Crossrealm: The Keeper of the Anchor Fire

**Genre**: Planar fantasy / dark epic / metafiction
**Adapted from**: Crossrealm Chronicles
**Volume XIV**: The Seven-Hundredth Year — the Continuing Volume

---

## Ninety-eight: The Loft

A little before dawn, A-Shi heard something above him.

Very soft. Like a small step on the wooden loft floor — a creak that was not his mother, not Xiao Huo, not the wind.

He listened without moving.

Nothing.

He went up the ladder into the loft.

The trap door was open.

He had not opened it.

Inside, the small room was washed with thin, pale morning light. Nothing had been disturbed. The pile of old winter clothes in the corner had not been moved. The stack of yellowing paper in the far corner had not been shifted.

And in the middle of the room, on the old wobbly table nobody used anymore, sat a single coal.

Plain. Very ordinary. No larger than a knuckle. Still faintly glowing at its very center, as though something deep inside it had never gone out.

A-Shi walked to it slowly.

He did not touch it.

He looked at it for a long time.

It was the kind of coal his grandfather used to carry from the mountain on the day of the first frost — the kind of charcoal his great-grandfather used to set special nights apart for — the kind the family had always brought home from the white stone on the northern ridge.

He lifted it gently with one hand.

It was warm.

Not just warm from sitting in the loft — warm from within. The way the burned center of a long-since-extinguished fireplace stays warm hours after the last wood has ashed over.

There was a small piece of paper tucked under its edge.

He unfolded it with two fingers, very carefully.

On it, a single line:

The fire carries everything forward.

A-Shi stood in the loft for a long while.

Then he carried the coal down the ladder.

He approached the stove.

He opened the stove's iron door.

He placed the coal inside.

The stove did not explode. It did not roar. It simply — brightened.

The kitchen filled with a gentle white-gold light that felt less like a flame and more like warmth arriving from somewhere far away — from a stove so ancient nobody alive could claim to have tended it.

The fire in the stove ate the coal in a single silent breath.

It burned steadily — brighter than ever.

In the courtyard, the fire-seed tree shimmered in the new light.

And the morning began.

*(End of volume ninety-eight · Volume XIV, fourth part.)*
